Electronic Stamp 
Established/proper name 
Fitusiran  
(Proposed) proprietary name 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
Pharmacologic class 
Antithrombin-directed small interfering ribonucleic acid 
(siRNA) 
Other product name(s) 
SAR439774 or ALN-AT3SC 
Applicant 
GENZYME CORP 
Dosage form(s)/formulation(s) 
INJECTION, SOLUTION 
Dosing regimen 
The starting dose of fitusiran is 50 mg once every 2 months. 
The dose should be adjusted if needed, to maintain 
antithrombin (AT) activity between 15-35%. 
 
. 
Applicant-proposed indication(s)/ 
population(s) 
Indicated for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the 
frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and adolescent 
patients aged 12 years and older with hemophilia A or B with 
or without factor VIII or IX inhibitors. 
SNOMED CT code for proposed 
indication disease term(s)1 
41788008 
Regulatory action  
Approval 
Approved dosage (if applicable) 
The starting dose of fitusiran is 50 mg once subcutaneously 
every 2 months. The dose should be adjusted, if needed, to 
maintain AT activity between 15-35%. Monitor AT activity 
using an FDA-cleared test.  
Approved indication(s)/ 
population(s) (if applicable) 
Fitusiran is an antithrombin-directed small interfering 
ribonucleic acid indicated for routine prophylaxis to prevent or 
reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and 
pediatric patients aged 12 years and older with hemophilia A 
or B with or without factor VIII or IX inhibitors. 
SNOMED CT code for approved 
indication disease term(s)1 
41788008 
1 For internal tracking purposes only. 
Abbreviations: PDUFA, Prescription Drug User Fee Act; SNOMED CT, Systematized Nomenclature of Medicine Clinical Terms

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
I. Executive Summary 
1. Overview 
1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action  
The Applicant, Genzyme Corporation, submitted an NDA for fitusiran (trade name QFITLIA), 
an antithrombin (AT)-directed small interfering ribonucleic acid, for the following indication: 
routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric 
patients aged 12 years and older with hemophilia A or B with or without factor VIII or IX 
inhibitors. The proposed starting dosage is 50 mg once subcutaneously (SC) every 2 months. The 
dose should be adjusted, if needed, to maintain AT activity in the range of 15 to 35%. 
The Applicant conducted two adequate and well-controlled trials, in which fitusiran prophylaxis 
use at 80 mg once monthly (QM) demonstrated a substantial improvement in the annualized 
bleeding rate (ABR) compared to on-demand treatment in subjects with hemophilia A with 
inhibitors (HAwI) and hemophilia B with inhibitors (HBwI) (Study EFC14768), and subjects 
with hemophilia A without inhibitors (HA) and hemophilia B without inhibitors (HB) (Study 
EFC14769). During development, to mitigate the risk of thrombotic events, an antithrombin-
based dose regimen (AT-DR) targeting an AT activity range of 15 to 35% was implemented. 
Subjects in the single-arm extension study (Study LTE15174), who used the to-be-marketed 
fitusiran AT-DR, were compared to the on-demand control arms from the randomized controlled 
parent studies (Study EFC19768 and Study EFC14769). We concluded that this comparison was 
also an adequate and well-controlled investigation and that it confirmed that the efficacy of 
fitusiran is preserved with the AT-DR. 
Fitusiran has serious risks of thromboembolic events and gallbladder disease (including 
cholecystitis, cholelithiasis, and the need for cholecystectomy in some patients). These risks and 
mitigation recommendations will be described in a boxed warning. Hepatotoxicity is also a 
serious risk that will be described in a Warning and Precaution with recommendations for liver 
test monitoring. The incidence of these risks appears lower with the use of the AT-DR dose 
regimen. Contemporaneously with fitusiran’s approval, the Center for Devices and Radiological 
Health (CDRH) is clearing a companion diagnostic assay so healthcare providers can accurately 
assess AT activity and adjust the fitusiran dose and frequency of administration, as needed, to 
target AT activity of 15 to 35%, which is essential for the safe and effective use of fitusiran. 
The application was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team. Each discipline recommends 
approval, and the signatory authority concurs that the application should be approved. The 
overall benefit-risk profile is favorable as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework (Table 2). 
For detailed information supporting the basis for this approval, please refer to the detailed 
reviews included in this interdisciplinary assessment document, consult reviews (e.g., Drug-
Induced Liver Injury team consultation), CDRH assessment of the AT assay, and the product 
quality review. 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 25 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
 
4 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of 
Effectiveness 
Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with two or more adequate and well-
controlled clinical investigations. These included Study EFC14768 in subjects with HAwI and 
HBwI and Study EFC14769 in subjects with HA and HB as well as the comparison of subjects 
dosed with the AT-DR in Study LTE15174 with those in the on-demand control arms of Study 
EFC14768 and EFC14769. 
Study EFC14768 was a randomized, multicenter, open-label clinical study in 57 adult and 
adolescent males (aged ≥12 years) with hemophilia A or B with inhibitors who previously 
received on-demand treatment with bypassing agents (BPAs) for bleeding. Eligible patients were 
randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive fitusiran prophylaxis 80 mg SC QM (N=38) or BPA on 
demand for treatment of breakthrough bleeding episodes (N=19) for 9 months. In the fitusiran 
prophylaxis arm (80 mg QM), there was a 91% reduction in ABR for treated bleeds compared to 
the BPA on-demand arm (p value<0.0001) in the efficacy period, demonstrating that fitusiran 
prophylaxis at 80 mg QM provided a substantial reduction in bleeding compared to on-demand 
treatment with BPAs in subjects with HAwI and HBwI. 
Study EFC14769 was a randomized, multicenter, open-label clinical study in 120 adult and 
adolescent males (aged ≥12 years) with hemophilia A or B without inhibitors who previously 
received on-demand treatment with clotting factor concentrates (CFCs) for bleeding. Eligible 
patients were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive fitusiran prophylaxis 80 mg SC once monthly 
(N=80) or CFCs on demand to treat bleeding episodes (N=40) for 9 months. In the fitusiran 
prophylaxis arm (80 mg QM), there was a 90% reduction in ABR for treated bleeds compared to 
the factor on-demand arm (p value<0.0001) in the efficacy period, demonstrating that fitusiran 
prophylaxis at 80 mg QM provided a substantial reduction in bleeding compared to on-demand 
treatment with CFCs in subjects with HA and HB without inhibitors. 
Although fitusiran was originally developed as an 80 mg QM fixed dose regimen, the dose 
regimen was subsequently modified to an AT-DR after observing multiple thrombotic events and 
determining that the risk of thrombosis was increased with persistent AT levels <15%. The 
efficacy of fitusiran AT-DR treatment was assessed by comparing the fitusiran AT-DR treatment 
data from the long-term extension Study LTE15174 to the control data from studies EFC14768 
and EFC14769. Of the 227 enrolled subjects in the long-term extension study, 78 subjects had 
inhibitors (HAwI=59 and HBwI=19) and 149 subjects did not have inhibitors (HA=115 and 
HB=34). In subjects with inhibitors treated with fitusiran, there was a 73% reduction 
(p-value<0.0006) in the estimated ABR for treated bleeds in the primary efficacy period 
compared to those treated with BPA on demand from the control data. In subjects without 
inhibitors, there was a 71% reduction (p-value<0.0001) in the estimated ABR for treated bleeds 
in subjects treated with fitusiran in the primary efficacy period compared to those treated with 
CFCs on-demand from the control data. In summary, the AT-DR provides substantial reduction 
in bleeding compared to on-demand treatment, consistent with the efficacy data from the 80 mg 
QM dose regimen. 
Reference ID: 5560526

2. Benefit-Risk Assessment 
2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework 
Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework 
Dimension 
Evidence and Uncertainties 
Conclusions and Reasons 
Analysis of 
condition 
 
Hemophilia A (HA) is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations in 
the FVIII gene, which results in severe deficiency or absence of the clotting 
protein factor VIII. HA occurs in approximately 1 in 5,000 live male births. 
Patients are at increased risk for minor and serious bleeding, including life-
threatening spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. Disease manifestations 
include mucosal bleeding, easy bruising, soft tissue and muscle bleeds, 
hemarthrosis, and excessive bleeding with procedures or trauma. Bleeding 
can occur in vital organs (including the brain). Adults commonly experience 
hemophilic arthropathy due to recurrent joint bleeds, which is associated 
with signficant disease morbidity. 
 
Hemophilia B (HB) is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations in 
the FIX gene, which results in severe deficiency or absence of the clotting 
protein factor IX. HB occurs in approximately 1 in 30,000 live male births. 
Deficient factor IX levels result in an increased risk for minor and serious 
bleeding, including life-threatening spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. 
Disease manifestations of HB are the same as HA. 
 
One of the most serious complications of HA and HB is development of 
inhibitors to factors, which is associated with the use of factor replacement. 
Approximately 30-35% of patients with HA and 5-15% of patients with HB 
develop inhibitors. Patients with inhibitors often experience increased 
disease morbidity and mortality because the bleeding is more difficult to 
prevent or treat.  
Hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) 
with or without FVIII inhibitors and hemophilia 
B (congenital factor IX deficiency) with or 
without FIX inhibitors are serious chronic 
diseases with significant morbidity and 
mortality due to bleeding. Patients with 
inhibitors tend to have a worse disease course.

Conclusions and Reasons 
Current 
treatment 
options 
 
The goal of treatment in hemophilia is to prevent bleeding, improve joint 
function, relieve pain, and to assist the patient in continuing/resuming 
normal activities of daily living.  
 
Current treatment options for patients with hemophilia A or B without 
inhibitors include either episodic IV infusion of the missing factor (FVIII or 
FIX) in response to symptoms of a bleed (“on-demand”) or scheduled 
prophylactic factor (FVIII or FIX) administration to prevent bleeds from 
occurring. 
 
Prophylactic treatment in patients with severe hemophilia with replacement 
factor VIII or IX, has been proven to reduce the frequency of bleeding 
events and joint destruction; however, it requires lifelong IV infusion every 1 
to 2 weeks, resulting in treatment burden, incomplete adherence, and 
complications associated with indwelling catheters (e.g., infection, 
thrombosis). These products can also lead to inhibitor formation: 
– 
Neutralizing alloantibodies (inhibitors) develop with exposure to 
exogenous factor and block the activity of factor VIII or IX. 
Approximately 30% of patients with HA and 1-15% of patients with HB 
will develop inhibitors against infused FVIII or FIX. 
– 
Immune tolerance induction (ITI) is used to eradicate the inhibitor, but 
this is only sucessful in 60-80% of cases in patients with HAwI and 
15-30% of patients with HBwI. 
 
Available non-factor-replacement therapies for hemophilia A or B include: 
– 
Emicizumab (HEMLIBRA) is a bispecific factor-IXa- and factor-X-
directed antibody that was FDA-approved in 2017 for routine 
prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in 
adult and pediatric patients with hemophilia A with or without inhibitors. 
Emicizumab is administred weekly up to every 4 weeks by SC injection. 
Emicizumab has a boxed warning for thrombotic microangiopathy and 
thromoemoblism associated with concomitant use of activated 
prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC). 
– 
Marstacimab (HYMPAVZI) is a tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI) 
antagonist that was FDA-approved in 2024 for routine prophylaxis to 
prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and 
pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with HA and HB without 
inhibitors. Martascimab is a weekly SC injection. Marstacimab has a 
risk of hypersensitivity reactions, in addition to thromboembolic events.  
The effectiveness of available therapy for 
prophylactic treatment of bleeds varies. There 
is a need for additional safe and effective 
therapies to prevent or reduce bleeding in 
patients with HA and HB with and without 
inhbitors.  
 
Treatment with factor replacement can result in 
significant treatment burden, including need for 
IV cathethers, risks of infections and 
thrombosis, and development of inhibitors. 
There is also need for available therapies that 
can be dosed less frequently and by a more 
convenient route of administration (such as 
SC).

Conclusions and Reasons 
– 
Concizumab (ALHEMO) is a TFPI antagonist that was FDA-approved in 
2024 for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of 
bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients 12 years of age and 
older with HAwI and HBwI. Concizumab is a daily SC injection. 
Concizumab has a risk of hypersensitivity reactions, in addition to 
thromboembolic events. 
– 
Gene therapy options are FDA-approved for patients with HA and HB; 
however, not all patients are candidates.  
 
FDA-approved bypassing agents are used to prevent or control bleeding in 
patients with inhibitors. aPCC is indicated to control and prevent bleeding 
episodes in patients with HAwI and HBwI. Recombinant activated 
coagulation factor VII (rFVIIa) is indicated for the treatment of bleeding 
episodes in patients with HAwI and HBwI. Efficacy of these products can be 
variable, and they carry a boxed warning for thrombosis. 
Benefit 
 
Studies EFC14768 and EFC14769 were multicenter, open-label clinical 
trials, in which patients with HA or HB with or without inhibitors were 
randomized 2:1 to fitusiran 80 mg QM or on-demand therapy. Both studies 
showed a statistically significant reduction in ABR for treated bleeds with 
fitusiran compared to on-demand therapy.  
 
Study LTE15174 was a long-term extension study in which 227 subjects 
with HA or HB with or without inhibitors, who previously received fitusiran in 
EFC14768 or EFC14769, rolled over and received fitusiran AT-DR. Efficacy 
of fitusiran AT-DR was established by comparison of data from patients in 
the extension study LTE15174 with data from patients in the control arms of 
the parent studies.  
– 
In subjects with inhibitors treated with fitusiran prophylaxis, there was a 
substantial reduction in mean ABR for treated bleeds: 5.1 with 95% CI 
(2.8, 9.5) for AT-DR compared to 19.1 with 95% CI (11.8, 31.0) for on-
demand BPA, with a resultant 73% reduction in estimated ABR for the 
fitusiran AT-DR compared to on-demand BPA (p=0.0006).  
– 
In subjects without inhibitors treated with fitusiran prophylaxis, there 
was a substantial reduction in mean ABR for treated bleeds: 9.0 with 
95% CI (5.6, 14.5) for AT-DR compared to 31.4 with 95% CI (20.5, 
48.2) for on-demand BPA, with a resultant 71% reduction in estimated 
ABR for the fitusiran AT-DR compared to CFC on-demand (p<0.0001).  
Fitusiran AT-DR routine prophylaxis 
compellingly reduces the frequency of bleeding 
episodes in subjects with HA or HB with or 
without inhibitors when compared with on-
demand BPA (in those with inhibitors) or on-
demand CFCs (in those without inhibitors). 
 
The prevention and/or reduction of bleeding is 
a clinical benefit, expected to reduce the 
morbidity and mortality associated with 
hemophilia, including joint damage and 
hemorrhagic stroke. 
 
Fitusiran offers the additional convenience of 
less frequent dosing (every 1 to 2 months) by 
subcutaneous route.

Risk and risk 
management 
 
Fitusiran was originally developed as an 80 mg QM dose; however, due to 
serious events of thrombosis associated with persistent AT activity <15%, 
the dosing regimen was modified to an AT-DR, targeting AT activity 15-
35%.  
 
Fitusiran’s safety was evaluated primarily in 286 subjects who received 
fitusiran on the AT-DR. 
– 
There were no fatal adverse events on the AT-DR.  
– 
Serious adverse reactions occurred in 1.4% of subjects; the most 
common serious adverse reaction was cholecystitis (N=2).  
– 
Adverse reactions leading to permanent discontinuation of fitusiran 
occurred in 1.4% of subjects and included liver injury, postoperative 
deep vein thombosis, cerebral infraction, and pruritus.  
– 
Adverse reactions leading to dosage interruptions of fitusiran occurred 
in 0.7% patients and included increased serum transaminases.  
The safety data submitted were sufficient to 
characterize the toxicity profile of fitusiran.  
 
The major risks of fitusiran include thrombotic 
events, gallbladder events, and hepatotoxicity, 
all of which appear to be reduced with the AT-
DR compared to fixed-dosing. It is essential 
that patients maintain AT activity 15-35%, 
necessitating the contemporaneous approval of 
a companion diagnostic for measuring AT 
activity.

The most common adverse reactions (≥10%) were viral infection (29%), 
nasopharyngitis (26%), and bacterial infection (11%). Other reported 
adverse reactions (≥5%) included hepatic injury (8%), arthralgia (8%), 
prothrombin fragment 1.2 increased (7%), injection site reaction (6%), 
headache (5%), and cough (5%).  
 
There are several risks that should be included in the Warnings and 
Precautions section and Boxed Warning of the United States Prescribing 
Information (USPI). These risks were identified from the pooled safey 
population, which includes exposure to fitusiran at fixed doses and the AT-
DR (n=355).  
1. Thromboembolic events were an adverse event of special interest 
based on the mechanism of action of fitusiran and the thrombotic 
events observed in the fitusiran fixed dosing regimen. Thrombotic 
events were reported in 7 subjects (2.6%) receiving fitusiran 80 mg 
QM prophylaxis, including a fatal cerebral venous sinus thrombosis 
event, and in 4 subjects (1.4%) receiving the AT-DR regimen. The 
risk of thrombosis was greater in patients with persistent AT activity 
<15%, when bleed management guidelines were not followed in the 
post-operative setting, with an indwelling venous catheter, and with 
use of the 80 mg once monthly dosing (not the approved dosing). 
Monitoring of AT activity using an FDA-cleared test (approved 
contemporaneously as a companion diagnostic) and targeting AT in 
the range of 15-35% is recommended to reduce the risk of 
thrombosis.  
2. A risk of acute and recurrent gallbladder disease was identified. In 
the fitusiran development program, 17% of subjects receiving the 
fixed dose once monthly regimen experienced gallbladder events, 
11 of whom (24%) underwent cholecystectomy. Gallbladder events 
were reported in 3.8% of subjects receiving the AT-DR, one of 
whom underwent cholecystectomy. All but one of the patients who 
underwent cholecystectomy resumed treatment after surgery. One 
subject who started on fixed dosing experienced cholangitis and 
pancreatitis caused by gallstone disease more than a year after 
cholecystectomy while receiving AT-DR dosing.  
3. Hepatoxocity was identified as a risk with the use of fitusiran. On 
the 80 mg once a month fixed dose regimen, alanine transaminase 
(ALT) and aspartate transaminase (AST) elevations above 3 times 
and need to follow bleeding management 
guidelines).  
 
Fitusiran has a unique and unexpected risk of 
acute and recurrent gallbladder disease, which 
resulted in the need for surgical intervention in 
some subjects. Even routine surgeries in 
patients with hemophilia can be challenging 
due to bleeding complications. It is critical that 
prescribers are aware of this risk before 
prescribing fitusiran in order to identify 
signs/symptoms early and to consider whether 
fitusiran is the appropriate option for their 
patient. Therefore, this risk will be included in a 
Boxed Warning, together with mitigation 
strategies (e.g., considering alternative 
treatment for hemophilia in patients with a 
history of symptomatic gallbladder disease; 
considering interruption or discontinuation of 
fitusiran if gallbladder disease occurs).  
 
The risk of hepatotoxicity will be described in a 
Warning and Precaution, together with 
mitigation strategies (e.g., monitoring liver 
tests, potential need to interrupt or discontinue 
fitusiran). 
 
We will also require enhanced 
pharmcovigilance for thrombotic events, 
hepatotoxicity, and gallbladder disease 
(including cholelithiasis and cholecystitis) to 
further monitor these risks in the postmarketing 
setting.

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk 
Hemophilia is a rare, serious, and chronic hematologic disorder cause by an inherited deficiency 
in factor VIII (hemophilia A) or factor IX (hemophilia B), leading to an increased risk of minor 
and life-threatening bleeding. Therapy is either given on demand, in which the replacement 
factor or BPA is given at the time of bleeding, or as prophylaxis, in which the therapy is 
continuously administered to prevent bleeding from occurring. Treatment with clotting factor 
concentrate can lead to inhibitor development, a serious complication in which bleeding is more 
difficult to manage. Newer treatments are available for patients depending on the hemophilia 
subtype and presence or absence of inhibitors, including emicizumab, marstacimab, and 
concizumab. Despite available therapies, new treatment options are needed. 
The efficacy data for fitusiran prophylaxis demonstrate a large, compelling reduction in ABR 
using AT-DR compared to on-demand treatment with BPA (in those with hemophilia A or B 
with inhibitors) and compared to CFC (in those with hemophilia A or B without inhibitors). The 
prevention of bleeding, a clinical benefit, is expected to reduce the morbidity and mortality 
associated with hemophilia. Fitusiran has the added benefit of every 1- to 2-month SC dosing, 
based on the AT level; a less frequent administration of the drug is beneficial for patients who 
may take it lifelong. 
The risks of fitusiran can adequately be described with labeling. Thrombotic events and acute 
and recurrent gallbladder disease will be included in a Boxed Warning and the risk of 
hepatotoxicity will be included in the Warnings and Precautions. The incidence of these three 
risks was lower with the AT-DR compared to the non-approved fixed-dosing regimen; therefore, 
monitoring of AT activity and targeting AT activity in the range of 15-35% is essential for the 
safe and effective use of fitusiran. An FDA-cleared AT activity assay is being 
contemporaneously approved with fitusiran as a companion diagnostic. These risks are further 
mitigated with instructions in labeling (e.g., need to follow bleed management guidelines to 
reduce thrombotic risks; need to consider alternative hemophilia therapy in patients with a 
history of symptomatic gallbladder disease; need to monitor liver tests while on treatment) and 
will be communicated to patients in a Medication Guide. 
The large benefit of fitusiran on reducing bleeding outweighs the identified risks when the 
mitigation approaches described in labeling are followed, providing another useful treatment 
option for patients with this serious, life-threatening disease. Labeling recommends use of 
fitusiran by healthcare providers experienced in the treatment of hemophilia, who are the most 
likely prescribers. These prescribers are well-versed in managing these complex diseases and 
determining the best treatment option for their specific patients considering the benefits and risks 
of the available therapies.

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment 
3. Introduction 
Fitusiran (proprietary name QFITLIA), a new molecular entity, is a double-stranded small 
interfering ribonucleic acid (siRNA) that causes degradation of antithrombin (AT) messenger 
RNA (mRNA) through RNA interference, reducing plasma AT levels. AT is a serine protease 
inhibitor that inactivates thrombin (factor IIa) and factor Xa, along with other enzymes in the 
coagulation system. Lower plasma AT protein levels lead to increases in thrombin generation 
and an improved ability to form stable clots. Fitusiran acts independently from factor VIII 
(FVIII) and factor IX (FIX), and thus would not be expected to result in the formation of factor-
neutralizing antibodies. 
The Applicant proposes the following indication: for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the 
frequency of bleeding episodes in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with 
hemophilia A (HA) (congenital factor VIII deficiency) with or without factor VIII inhibitors, or 
hemophilia B (HB) (congenital factor IX deficiency) with or without factor IX inhibitors. 
The proposed dosing regimen is 50 mg subcutaneously once every 2 months, with dose 
adjustments to maintain AT activity between 15 and 35% using an FDA-authorized AT activity 
assay. 
Disease Background 
Congenital hemophilia is a rare X-linked bleeding disorder caused by deficiencies of FVIII in 
HA and FIX in HB. Patients with HA or HB have a deficiency in the prothrombinase complex, 
essential for the conversion of prothrombin to thrombin, which is crucial for hemostasis (Sidonio 
et al. 2023). HA and HB occur in all racial and ethnic groups and are due to missense, nonsense, 
or deletions in F8 or F9, respectively. The prevalence of HA is estimated at 24.6 cases per 
100,000 male births, while HB prevalence is estimated at 5 cases per 100,000 male births; in 
both HA and HB, approximately 30 to 40% of cases are sporadic, or born to a noncarrier mother 
(Seaman et al. 2021; Sidonio and Malec 2021). The severity of hemophilia is correlated with 
residual factor levels, with mild defined as between 5 and less than 40% FVIII or FIX activity, 
moderate as between 1 to less than 5% activity, and severe as less than 1% activity (Srivastava et 
al. 2020). Severe disease occurs in roughly 30% of HB and 40% of HA. Inhibitors to FIX occur 
in 5 to 15% of severe HB, whereas inhibitors to FVIII occur in 30% of severe HA. Differences in 
severity and inhibitor development between HA and HB are related to the differences in the size 
of the respective genes, and the types of genetic lesions that differ between HA and HB 
(Castaman and Matino 2019). 
Clinical hallmarks of severe hemophilia are spontaneous or traumatic joint, soft tissue, and 
muscle hemorrhages (Sidonio et al. 2023), and prolonged and potentially fatal hemorrhage after 
trauma or surgery. In patients with HA or HB, neutralizing antibodies known as FVIII or FIX 
inhibitors, respectively, may develop, rendering exogenous factor replacement ineffective. The 
main risk factors for inhibitor development are hemophilia severity, F8/F9 genotype, and 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 34 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
 
13 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
cumulative exposure to FVIII and FIX concentrates (Rezende et al. 2024). Individuals with 
hemophilia with inhibitors are more difficult to treat in response to bleeding events, leading to a 
higher rate of progressive joint disease from frequent joint bleeds and increased risk of death 
from uncontrolled bleeding. 
The goal of treatment in severe hemophilia is to prevent or minimize the occurrence of bleeding 
to improve joint function, alleviate pain, and help the patient to continue/resume regular daily 
activities. Treatment with replacement factor can either be in response to a bleeding event (“on-
demand”) or scheduled to prevent bleeds by maintaining factor levels in a protective range 
(prophylactic treatment). There is significant evidence that prophylactic treatment in children 
with severe hemophilia prevents bleeding episodes and the associated joint damage (Manco-
Johnson et al. 2007). In individuals with severe hemophilia, an early start of treatment with long-
term prophylaxis is reported to reduce bleeding frequency and subsequent chronic joint disease 
and result in better quality of life, and is recommended by international hemophilia care 
organizations (Srivastava et al. 2020; Rezende et al. 2024). The choice of regimens (i.e., on-
demand or prophylaxis) is multifactorial and may be based on frequency and severity of 
bleeding, patient preference and lifestyle, and regional/geographic standard of care. The World 
Federation of Hemophilia strongly recommends prophylactic treatment for patients with severe 
hemophilia A or B with or without inhibitors to prevent bleeds at all times. Prophylaxis should 
be individualized (adjusting prophylaxis dose/frequency or both), taking into consideration 
patient bleeding phenotype, joint status, individual pharmacokinetics, and patient self-assessment 
and preference (Konkle et al. 2019). 
The treatment options for severe hemophilia have advanced from plasma-derived factor 
replacement to recombinant factor replacement, extended half-life recombinant factor 
replacement, non-factor-replacement therapy, and gene therapy. Annualized bleeding rate (ABR) 
has become the primary outcome measure to demonstrate the success of treatment products for 
severe hemophilia (Konkle et al. 2019). ABR is a clinically meaningful endpoint, as a reduction 
in bleeding events is important to patients by decreasing risk of mortality and organ and tissue 
damage, including joint damage, and allowing greater activity that might otherwise be limited 
due to risk of trauma and bleeding. 
Current Therapeutic Options 
The current prophylaxis treatment options for patients with severe HA or HB without inhibitors 
include: 
1. Prophylactic treatment with replacement factors, which has been proven to reduce the 
frequency of bleeding events; however, this approach requires frequent IV infusion, resulting 
in high treatment burden, incomplete adherence, complications associated with indwelling 
catheters (e.g., infection, thrombosis) and can lead to the development of factor inhibitors. 
Replacement factor therapy includes both plasma-derived factor concentrates and 
recombinant factor concentrates. 
2. Non-factor-replacement therapies have been designed to provide an alternative to 
conventional replacement treatment and either increase thrombin generation through the 
inhibition of naturally occurring anticoagulants, or by mimicking the action of factor VIII. 
These agents are designed to work in the prophylaxis context, are not indicated for 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 35 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
 
14 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
management of acute bleeding episodes, and are typically dosed subcutaneously on either 
weekly or monthly schedules. 
a. Emicizumab (Hemlibra) subcutaneous (SC) injection is a bispecific FIXa- and factor 
X (FX)-directed antibody, approved for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the 
frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with HA with and 
without inhibitors. The emicizumab product label (Genentech 2024) contains a boxed 
warning that states cases of thrombotic microangiopathy and thrombotic events were 
reported when, on average, a cumulative amount of >100 U/kg/24 hours of activated 
prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC) was administered for 24 hours or more to 
patients receiving emicizumab prophylaxis. 
b. Marstacimab (Hympavzi) is a tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI) antagonist 
indicated for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding 
episodes in adult and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with HA or HB 
without inhibitors. Dosing is weekly SC. Thromboembolic events may occur and are 
listed in warnings and precautions (Pfizer Laboratories Div Pfizer Inc 2025). 
3. Adeno-associated virus (AAV)-based gene therapy introduces an AAV vector that inserts a 
functional copy of the missing clotting factor gene into patients’ hepatocytes so that clotting 
factor can be expressed (Deshpande et al. 2024). Advantages of gene therapy include a one-
time infusion and factor expression levels that provide excellent prophylaxis. Although these 
therapies appear to have few serious adverse events, a notable toxicity of hepatotropic AAV-
based gene therapy is liver toxicity, which can result in loss of transgene expression of factor. 
The current prophylaxis treatment options for patients with severe HA or HB without inhibitors 
is detailed in Table 3.

The current prophylaxis treatment options for patients with severe hemophilia A with inhibitors 
(HAwI) or hemophilia B with inhibitors (HBwI) include: 
4. Non-factor-replacement therapies 
a. Emicizumab (Hemlibra) is also approved for HAwI; see description of the product 
above.  
b. Concizumab (Alhemo) is a TFPI antagonist indicated for routine prophylaxis to 
prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients 
12 years of age and older with HA or HB with inhibitors. Alhemo is dosed daily via 
SC injection. Thromboembolic events may occur and are listed as a warning in the 
label (Novo Nordisk 2024). 
5. Bypassing agents, including recombinant activated factor VII or activated prothrombin 
complex concentrates, are limited by short half-lives requiring daily IV infusion and by cost. 
Bypassing agents can be used for prophylaxis in both HA and HB with inhibitors. Bypassing 
agents are also associated with thrombotic risk.

3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review 
Table 5 provides an overview of the clinical studies conducted to support the benefit-risk 
assessment of fitusiran for subjects with HA and HB with and without inhibitors.  
Results from subjects with HA and HB with and without inhibitors from the parent studies 
EFC14768 and EFC14769, and the extension study LTE15174 formed the primary basis for the 
assessment of efficacy. The primary assessment of efficacy for the fitusiran 80 mg QM dosage 
was based on the parent studies, EFC14768 and EFC14769. The assessment of efficacy for the 
AT-DR was based on a comparison of efficacy data from subjects who received the AT-DR 
during the single arm extension study LTE15174 to those who used on demand treatment during 
the parent studies EFC14768 and EFC14769. 
The assessment of risk for the fitusiran AT-DR was primarily based on pooled analyses of 
subjects who received the AT-DR from Studies LTE15174, LTE14762, and EFC15110 (N=286). 
Additionally, assessment of risk was based on data from a pooled safety set comprising all 
adverse events occurring in subjects with HA or HB with or without inhibitors while receiving 
any dosage of fitusiran in a clinical study (N=335). 
See Figure 20, Section 12 for the timeline of clinical studies in relation to AT-DR across the 
fitusiran development program.

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, 
and Clinical Pharmacology 
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential 
Effectiveness 
Hemophilia A and hemophilia B are inherited X-linked genetic bleeding disorders characterized 
by a deficiency in blood clotting factors in the intrinsic coagulation pathway, which causes an 
increased risk of bleeding in affected patients. Antithrombin (AT) is a liver-expressed 
anticoagulant protein that is the major endogenous inhibitor of the coagulation factor thrombin. 
Fitusiran (AD-57213 or ALN-AT3SC) is a synthetic double-stranded small interfering RNA 
(siRNA), designed to suppress liver production of AT as a strategy to rebalance the coagulation 
cascade by increasing thrombin generation to improve hemostasis in individuals with 
hemophilia. Fitusiran contains a conjugated triantennary N-acetylgalactosamine (GalNAc) sugar 
ligand that binds to the asialoglycoprotein receptor (ASPGR) on hepatocytes (Cummings and 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 44 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
23 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
McEver 2009). Upon binding, the ASPGR-fitusiran complex is internalized and processed in 
endosomes, where fitusiran is loaded onto the RNA-induced silencing complex (RISC), which 
guides fitusiran to the target AT mRNA sequence (transcribed from the SERPINC1 gene) and 
utilizes RNA interference mechanisms to specifically target/degrade AT mRNA. Reduced AT 
mRNA levels result in lowered plasma AT protein levels, increased thrombin generation 
potential, and improved ability to form stable clots (Figure 1). 
Figure 1. Mechanism of Action of Fitusiran 
 
Source: Applicant’s Application Orientation Meeting 
Coagulation model depicting two separate initiations, intrinsic (contact) and extrinsic pathways, which ultimately merge at the level 
of factor Xa (common pathway). In hemophilia A and B, the lack of FVIII and FIX, respectively, leads to a decrease in thrombin 
potential, resulting in a bleeding phenotype. 
Abbreviations: F, factor 
5.1.1. In Vitro Characterization of Fitusiran 
To establish the proof of concept for fitusiran as a potential treatment for hemophilia, a series of 
in vitro and in vivo pharmacology studies was conducted by the Applicant. The ability of 
fitusiran to reduce AT mRNA, protein, and activity levels was characterized in vitro using 
HepG2 cells (immortalized human liver cells) and evaluated in vivo using various wild-type 
(WT) species, transgenic mouse models of hemophilia A (HA) and B (HB), an HA dog model 
(genetic), and an HA monkey model (anti-FVIII antibody). The specificity of fitusiran for the AT 
mRNA was evaluated using bioinformatics methodology and in vitro in HepG2 cells. 
5.1.2. In Vitro Pharmacological Activity of Fitusiran 
Addition of fitusiran to HepG2 cells in vitro resulted in a dose-dependent inhibition of AT 
mRNA levels with a half maximal inhibitory concentration of 49.6pM. There was no appreciable 
off-target inhibition detected for any of the eight potential off-target transcripts identified by 
bioinformatics analyses and tested in HepG2 cells at up to 10nM fitusiran, which supported the 
specificity of fitusiran for the AT mRNA. Refer to Section 13 for additional details on these 
studies. 
Reference ID: 5560526
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

===== PAGE 45 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
24 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
5.1.3. In Vivo Proof of Concept Animal Model Data 
Fitusiran is pharmacologically active in mice, rats, dogs, and cynomolgus monkeys, as the AT 
mRNA target sequence is fully conserved (mice, rats, monkeys) or highly conserved (dogs) with 
the human sequence. AT levels were assessed following single and repeated subcutaneous 
administrations of fitusiran in WT mice and healthy monkeys, and in mouse, monkey, and dog 
models of hemophilia. In WT mice, single SC doses of fitusiran >10 mg/kg led to dose-
dependent AT suppression exceeding 80%. Consistent with the long tissue residence typical of 
siRNA drugs, AT mRNA suppression was maintained for at least 28 days postdosing. A single 
SC dose of fitusiran up to 2 mg/kg in healthy monkeys resulted in AT protein reductions of up to 
70%. Further AT reductions up to 80% were observed following 6 months of repeated monthly 
dosing. Consistent with the intended therapeutic mechanism, reduction of AT was associated 
with increased thrombin generation. These data confirmed the ability of fitusiran to hit the 
intended target in vivo and suggested that a less frequent dosing regimen might be suitable for 
the intended hemophilia patient population. 
Mouse models of hemophilia have been generated by disruption of the genes encoding clotting 
factor VIII (modeling hemophilia A) or factor IX (modeling hemophilia B). These models 
exhibit bleeding tendencies similar to those observed in humans with hemophilia (Sabatino et al. 
2012). A single SC administration of fitusiran up to 30 mg/kg in hemophilia A (factor VIII 
knockout) mice resulted in a dose-dependent suppression of AT expression of >80% at 
≥10 mg/kg when compared to untreated HA mice. Similarly, a single-dose SC administration of 
fitusiran up to 30 mg/kg fitusiran in hemophilia B (factor IX knockout) mice resulted in a dose-
dependent suppression of AT of up to 90% compared to untreated HB mice. A dose-related 
increase in endogenous thrombin up to 155% was observed at 30 mg/kg in HB mice when 
compared to control HB mice, predicting an improved ability of the hemophilia mice to form 
clots. Fitusiran activity was sustained for the duration of a 26-week repeat-dose study in HA 
mice; once-weekly administration of fitusiran up to 30 mg/kg/week resulted in a significantly 
decreased activated partial thromboplastin time of up to 60% and up to 90% suppression of 
plasma AT protein levels compared to control HA mice. Studies in mouse hemophilia models 
provided important proof of concept data and supported the initiation of clinical development of 
fitusiran for the treatment of hemophilia. The HA and HB mouse proof of concept data were 
further corroborated by studies in a genetic dog model of HA and in an HA monkey model 
where hemophilia was induced using an anti-FVIII monoclonal antibody (MAb). Details of these 
studies can be found in Section 13. 
Functional improvements in hemostatic endpoints in HA (factor VIII knockout) mice were 
assessed using the microvessel laser injury method to compare the ability to form clots in 
fitusiran-treated versus untreated animals. HA mice were given a single SC administration of 
fitusiran up to 30 mg/kg prior to injury. Ten days later, the cremaster muscle was injured by laser 
to induce bleeding. A dose-related increase in the accumulation of platelets and fibrin and 
improved thrombus formation within 3 minutes were observed in fitusiran-treated HA mice, 
while no clotting was detected in PBS-treated HA mice. Stable thrombus formation was similarly 
demonstrated in HB (factor IX knockout) mice using the same methodology. These studies in 
clinically relevant mouse models link the fitusiran mechanism of action data (decreased AT 
mRNA, protein expression, and activity) with the desired clinical outcome of reduced clotting 
times in the presence of injury and suggest that fitusiran can be used prophylactically in the 
clinic (as opposed to use as a rescue treatment). 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 46 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
25 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Overall, fitusiran administration in healthy animals and hemophilia animal models resulted in 
dose-dependent decreases in AT mRNA, protein, and activity; decreased activated partial 
thromboplastin time; and increased endogenous thrombin formation, which supported the 
proposed mechanism of action. Fitusiran caused sustained reductions of AT mRNA that were 
anticipated based on the long tissue residence times observed with other siRNA drugs in animals 
and humans; these data supported the development of less frequent clinical dosing regimens 
(e.g., once every 8 weeks). Administration of fitusiran prior to laser injury in hemophilia mice 
led to consistent clot formation at the injury site, demonstrating the desired functional 
improvement in clinically relevant hemophilia models with real world implications; these data 
support a plausible mechanism of action by which fitusiran may prevent or reduce the frequency 
of bleeding episodes. Overall, the results from the studies support the therapeutic rationale for 
the use of fitusiran as a potentially effective treatment for patients with hemophilia A or B.

5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics

Characteristic 
Drug Information 
 
Pharmacologic Activity 
Established pharmacologic 
class (EPC) 
Fitusiran is an antithrombin-directed small interfering ribonucleic acid  
Mechanism of action 
Fitusiran is a double-stranded small interfering ribonucleic acid (siRNA) that causes degradation of antithrombin (AT) 
messenger RNA through RNA interference, which results in a reduction of plasma AT levels.  
Active moieties 
Fitusiran siRNA solution for SC administration 
QT prolongation 
A thorough QT (TQT) study was not conducted in humans with fitusiran because data from nonclinical and clinical 
studies provide the totality of evidence that fitusiran is not associated with QTc prolongation up to 80 mg QM.

In the majority of subjects, fitusiran concentrations in plasma were below the LLOQ (0.2 ng/mL) by 24 to 48 hours 
postdose. Model-derived individual steady-state exposures for subjects with hemophilia from four phase 3 studies 
are provided in Appendix 11 of the Population Pharmacokinetic Study Report POH1086. 
Range of effective dose(s) 
or exposure 
The starting dose is 50 mg Q2M. If needed, the starting dose can be escalated or de-escalated depending on 
whether the subject’s AT activity is >35% or <15%, respectively. The modified doses include 50 mg QM, 20 mg 
Q2M, 20 mg QM, 10 mg Q2M, or 10 mg QM.  
Maximally tolerated dose 
or exposure 
No maximum tolerated dose was identified. The highest dose studied was 80 mg QM. 
Dose proportionality 
Fitusiran exhibited a dose-proportional increase in plasma exposure over the dose range of 20 to 50 mg following 
single-dose SC administration. 
Accumulation 
No accumulation of fitusiran was observed following multiple once weekly (QW) or QM doses, as fitusiran is short-
lived in plasma. 
Time to achieve steady 
state 
From a pharmacodynamic standpoint, regardless of the dose regimen, >99% of subjects are expected to reach 
steady state AT activity levels within 23 weeks following initiation of fitusiran dosing or following dose escalation or 
de-escalation.

Characteristic 
Drug Information 
Bridge between to-be-
marketed and clinical 
trial/study formulations 
A single-dose vial/syringe formulation was used in phase 1-3 studies, but a prefilled pen (PFP) was introduced 
during long-term extension Study LTE15174 for administration of 50-mg doses in recognition that some subjects 
might prefer its convenience. The Applicant intends to market both vial/syringe and PFP presentations (each single-
dose vial is designed to deliver 20 mg of fitusiran in 0.2 mL, or 10 mg of fitusiran in 0.1 mL, and each PFP is 
designed to deliver a 50-mg fixed dose of fitusiran in 0.5 mL). The statistical comparisons show that the resulting PK 
parameters (Cmax, AUC0-t, AUC0-infinity) and observed annualized bleeding rate (ABR) are comparable between these 
two presentations (see Section 6.1.4.4).  
 
Absorption 
Bioavailability 
Fitusiran is absorbed rapidly following SC administration. Absolute bioavailability is not known.

Metabolic pathway(s) 
Fitusiran is metabolized by endo- and exonucleases to oligonucleotides of varying lengths, which are further 
metabolized to oligonucleotides of even shorter lengths. 
Primary excretion 
pathways (% dose) 
The mean fraction of fitusiran dose excreted in urine (0 to 24 hours postdose) after a 50-mg dose is 14.6%.

Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations 
Body weight 
Based on PopPK analysis, body weight and age were identified as statistically significant covariates in subjects with 
hemophilia, but the magnitude of the effect (<25% on fitusiran PK) is not considered to be clinically relevant (fitusiran 
effect is driven by fitusiran concentrations in liver rather than plasma).  
Age 
See above  
Renal impairment 
No dedicated study has been conducted to evaluate the impact of renal impairment. The PopPK analysis using 
clinical trial data from subjects with estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) >45 mL/min/1.73 m2 identified eGFR 
as a statistically significant covariate in subjects with hemophilia, but the magnitude of the effect (<25% on fitusiran 
plasma PK) is not considered to be clinically relevant (fitusiran effect is driven by fitusiran concentrations in liver, 
rather than plasma). 
Hepatic impairment 
No dedicated study has been conducted to evaluate the impact of hepatic impairment. There was no clinical 
experience of fitusiran in subjects with hepatic impairment, as the clinical development program excluded subjects 
with clinically significant liver disease, or with ALT and/or AST >1.5× upper limit of normal reference range (ULN) or 
total bilirubin > ULN. Given the risk for hepatotoxicity, the review team recommends “avoid use” in patients with all 
degrees of hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A, B and C). 
 
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator) 
Inhibition/induction of 
metabolism 
At clinically relevant concentrations, fitusiran is unlikely to be an inhibitor of CYP1A2, CYP2A6, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, 
CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4/5, or to be an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4. 
Inhibition/induction of 
transporter systems 
At clinically relevant concentrations, fitusiran is unlikely to be an inhibitor of major efflux transporters, including P-gp, 
BCRP, and BSEP, or major uptake transporters. 
 
Immunogenicity (if Applicable) 
Bioanalysis 
Antidrug antibodies (ADAs) against fitusiran were assayed using ELISA. No assay for neutralizing antibody (NAb) 
was developed.  
Incidence 
During fitusiran treatment (up to 250 weeks) in four pivotal studies, 10 out of 290 adults with hemophilia (3.4%) 
developed ADAs. All subjects had low ADA titers and the majority of subjects had transient ADAs.  
Clinical impact 
There was no identified clinically significant effect of ADAs on pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, safety, or 
effectiveness of fitusiran.

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit) 
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential 
Effectiveness 
6.1.1. Applicant’s Proposed Dosing Regimen 
The proposed starting dose of fitusiran is 50 mg once subcutaneously every 2 months (Q2M) and 
then, if needed, the dose can be escalated or de-escalated to maintain the AT activity in the range 
of 15 to 35%. The doses include 50 mg QM, 20 mg Q2M, or 20 mg QM based on AT activity 
levels. AT activity must be measured using an FDA-cleared AT activity assay (under review by 
the Center for Devices and Radiological Health as a Companion Diagnostic). No dose 
adjustment from 50 mg Q2M is needed if AT activity levels are maintained between 15 to 35%. 
AT activity should be measured prior to initiation of fitusiran 
 
 After a dose modification, AT measurements should be restarted. 
The Agency made recommendations to add a 10 mg Q2M dose (described in Section 6.1.4.1) 
and also to update the frequency and timing of AT activity measurement (described in 
Section 6.1.4.2) in Section 2 of the USPI. 
6.1.2. Selection of Initial Dosing Regimen for Parent 
Phase 3 Studies 
Selection of the Initial 80 mg QM Dose Regimen 
The Applicant’s selection of the initial 80 mg QM dosing regimen for the phase 3 study was 
based on the results of phase 1 study TDR14767 (ALN-AT3SC-001) as well as modeling and 
simulation. Due to an observed disconnect between fitusiran plasma pharmacokinetic (PK) and 
AT activity, predicted fitusiran concentrations in the liver RISC (based on allometrically scaled 
total liver PK parameters from rat liver PK) and observed clinical PD values (AT activity) were 
utilized for the development of a population kinetic-pharmacodynamic (PopK-PD) model. The 
Applicant’s PopK-PD modeling and simulation analyses appeared reasonable for guiding dosing 
regimen development. Refer to Section 14 Pharmacometrics Assessment for detailed evaluation 
of this model. 
The phase 1 study TDR14767 (ALN-AT3SC-001) evaluated once-weekly (QW) and QM 
regimens, both as body-weight-based (mg/kg) doses and as fixed (mg) doses. The tolerability 
and safety of fitusiran in the phase 1 study were evaluated in 3 healthy subjects after a single SC 
administration of 0.03 mg/kg, and in 42 subjects with hemophilia A or B after either three QW 
doses ranging from 0.015 to 0.075 mg/kg, three QM doses ranging from 0.225 to 1.8 mg/kg, or 
three QM doses of 50 or 80 mg. A dose-dependent reduction in AT activity from baseline was 
observed at all dose levels (Figure 2). Among tested doses, 50-mg and 80-mg fitusiran doses 
demonstrated generally similar safety profiles and reduction in AT activity. 
Reference ID: 5560526
(b) (4)

===== PAGE 51 =====
NDA 219019

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
In addition to the observed dose-AT-activity relationship, the Applicant conducted an 
exploratory PopK-PD analysis to evaluate reduction in AT activity over a fitusiran dose range of 
1 to 140 mg. The results showed that with doses greater than 80 mg fitusiran, only a small 
incremental change in AT activity was predicted (Figure 3).

Based on evaluation of the mean annualized bleeding rate (ABR) by AT activity quartile 
observed in the phase 1 study TDR14767 (ALN-AT3SC-001), it was determined that an AT 
activity reduction of >75% showed maximal effect in controlling bleeding, as shown in Figure 4 
below. 
Figure 4. Mean (±SEM) Annualized Bleed Rate by AT Quartile in Phase 1 Study TDR14767 
 
PopK-PD simulations to predict AT reduction at steady state indicated that 50 mg QM dosing 
would result in 87% of subjects achieving >75% AT reduction, while 80 mg QM dosing would 
result in 98% of subjects achieving >75% AT reduction (POH1133). 
Based on the totality of available observed and simulated data, the Applicant chose the initial 
80 mg QM fitusiran dose for the phase 3 studies (EFC14768, EFC14769, EFC15110), with the 
goal of achieving maximal pharmacodynamic (PD) activity (AT reduction) with low intersubject 
variability. 
6.1.3. Development of AT-DR 
The fitusiran phase 3 program was initiated at a fixed dose regimen of 80 mg QM, and later 
adjusted to an AT-based dosing regimen (AT-DR). 
The efficacy and mean AT levels with fitusiran 80 mg QM treatment compared with on-demand 
treatment with BPAs or CFCs, or compared with prior BPA/CFC prophylaxis are summarized in 
Table 8 below. 
Reference ID: 5560526

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Table 8. Efficacy and Mean AT Levels of the 80 mg QM Regimen in Three Parent Phase 3 Studies 
Source: Applicant’s Orientation Meeting Slides 
Abbreviations: ABR, annualized bleeding rate; AT, antithrombin; BPA, bypassing agent; CFC, clotting factor concentrate; HA/B, 
hemophilia A or B; SD, standard deviation  
One potential concern with the 80 mg QM dose is that the mean AT levels are close to 10% with 
fitusiran treatment. Clinical data suggested that the risk of serious thrombotic events may be 
greater with persistent AT levels <10%. 
In October 2020, when the phase 1/2 study (LTE14762), the three parent phase 3 studies 
(EFC14768, EFC14769, EFC15110), and the phase 3 long-term extension (LTE) study 
(LTE15174) were ongoing, the Applicant placed a voluntary pause on ongoing clinical trials to 
evaluate the risk of the 80 mg QM regimen, due to concerns regarding thrombotic events 
potentially related to fitusiran. 
After analyzing the role of AT reduction and associated risk of thrombotic events as well as 
bleeding episodes, the Applicant revised the dosing regimen to AT-DR with a target AT activity 
of 15 to 35%. The 15% AT activity was set as the lower threshold because clinical data as of 
October 2020 suggested that the risk of serious thrombotic events may be greater with AT levels 
<10%, while AT activity from 15% to 25% was deemed to preserve efficacy. The AT activity 
35% was set as the upper threshold because data as of October 2020 suggested that AT levels 
<25% (>75% AT reduction) showed maximal effect in controlling bleeding, and the upper 
bounds of the 95% and 90% confidence intervals (CIs) of effective AT levels (25%) were 35.5% 
and 34.2%, respectively. 
With the identification of AT target thresholds (15 to 35% of normal AT activity), the Applicant 
performed modeling and simulation to identify the doses to achieve this target range. Based on 
the simulation results, the starting dose was revised to 50 mg Q2M (instead of 80 mg QM), with 
an option to sequentially escalate to 50 mg QM and 80 mg QM in case of AT activity >35% and 
options to decrease the dose or discontinue fitusiran for AT levels less than 15%. This revised 
AT-based dosing regimen was implemented in December 2020 across studies (except EFC14768 
and EFC14769, which were nearly complete at the time of the voluntary dosing pause). After 
implementation of the AT-DR, the observed number of subjects who had to discontinue fitusiran 
treatment due to AT activity levels <15% exceeded predictions. Therefore, based on additional 
simulations, the lower dose of 20 mg Q2M was identified, with the option to escalate to 20 mg 
QM in case of AT activity >35%. This lower dose regimen was subsequently implemented in the 
Reference ID: 5560526

long-term extension part of Study LTE15174. The final AT-DR employed in Study LTE15174 is 
depicted in Figure 5 below. 
Figure 5. Final Revised AT-Based Fitusiran Dose Regimens (LTE15174) 
 
Abbreviations: AT, antithrombin; LTE, long-term extension; Q2M, every 2 months; QM, once monthly; SS, steady state 
During the parent phase 3 studies and LTE15174, the Applicant found that escalation to 80 mg 
QM did not seem necessary, because none of the subjects who started dosing at 50 mg Q2M 
required dose escalation beyond 50 mg QM up to 80 mg QM. Therefore, in the final proposed 
AT-DR, the Applicant kept the AT-DR implemented since December 2020, but removed the 
option of escalating to 80 mg QM.

AT-DR Is Supported by Positive Efficacy and Safety Results 
The efficacy and safety evaluation supporting the AT-DR is discussed in detail in Section 6.2 and 
Sections 7.4 to 7.6.

As shown in Figure 6 below, in Study LTE15174, the target AT range of 15 to 35% could be 
achieved with the AT-DR, and 94% of subjects required 0 or 1 dose adjustment to achieve the 
target AT activity range.

6.1.4.1. Recommendation for Adding 10 mg Q2M for Patients 
Whose AT Level Drops Below 15% 
The review team simulated AT activity at Week 76 (close to trough) for two regimens: 10 mg 
Q2M and 15 mg Q2M. Table 10 shows that approximately 6% (out of the 7% discontinued due 
to AT <15%) of virtual subjects could remain on treatment with the addition of a 10 mg Q2M 
regimen. The 10 mg Q2M regimen is expected to result in relatively more patients remaining on 
treatment than 15 mg Q2M for patients with at least one AT activity <15% following 20 mg 
Q2M. The lower dose option of 10 mg Q2M will be an added dosage option in product labeling.

6.1.4.2. Changes to Proposed AT Monitoring Plan for Dose 
De-Escalation 
The review team revised the Applicant’s proposed AT monitoring and dose adjustment 
instructions (see Section 14.5.4 Evaluation of AT monitoring program). Specifically, changes 
were made to measure AT activity at Weeks 4 (Month 1), 12 (Month 3), and 20 (Month 5) that 
coincide with the lowest (nadir) AT activity (4 weeks post-dose) following Q2M or QM dosing 
to aid in dose de-escalation decisions. A measurement at Week 24 (Month 6) was recommended 
to capture the pre-dose AT activity to aid in dose escalation decisions. The revised instructions 
are shown below: 
Measure AT activity prior to initiation of QFITLIA using an FDA-authorized test. Do not initiate 
QFITLIA dosing if AT activity is <60% at baseline, 
 
 
Measure AT activity at Weeks 4 (Month 1), 12 (Month 3), 20 (Month 5) and 24 (Month 6) 
following the starting dose and after any dose modification. 
 If any AT activity is <15%, a dose reduction is required, the lower dose should be initiated 3 
months after the prior dose. 
 If AT activity is >35% after 6 months, or if the subject has not achieved satisfactory bleed 
control, dose escalation should be considered. 
Once the subject’s target dose is identified based on AT activity 15 to 35%, 
 
measure AT activity annually. Additional measurements can be considered

6.1.4.3. Dosing in Adolescents 
The use of fitusiran in adolescents with hemophilia A and B is supported by the three 
randomized phase 3 studies (EFC14768, EFC14769, EFC15110) and one open-label long-term 
extension study (LTE15174). A total of 60 adolescents were evaluated in the clinical studies. The 
dosing used in adolescents was the same as used in adults. No differences in efficacy were 
observed between the adolescent subgroup and the overall population (see Sections 6.2.1.5 and 
6.2.2.5, efficacy subgroup analysis). In general, the adverse reactions in fitusiran-treated 
adolescent subjects were similar in type to those seen in adult subjects with hemophilia A or B, 
with or without inhibitors (see Section 7.5.2.9 safety subgroup analysis). 
The PopPK analysis also showed that age (12 to 71 years) did not have a significant effect on the 
PK of fitusiran (Table 11). 
Table 11. Mean (SD) [CV%] Empirical Bayes Fitusiran Exposure Estimates (Dose-Normalized) By 
Age 
 
Source: Summary of Clinical Pharmacology, Table 27 
Therefore, the Applicant’s proposal for dosing in adolescent subjects, i.e., ≥12 age <18 years, 
using the AT-DR is acceptable. 
6.1.4.4. Bridging of Phase 3 to the To-Be-Marketed Product 
Presentations 
The vial/syringe formulation was used in phase 1 to 3 studies, but a prefilled pen (PFP) was 
introduced during LTE15174 for administration of 50-mg doses in recognition that some subjects 
might prefer its convenience. The Applicant proposes both vial/syringe and PFP formulations in 
the label (each PFP is designed to deliver a fixed dose of 50 mg of fitusiran in 0.5 mL, and each 
vial is designed to deliver 20 mg of fitusiran in 0.2 mL). Patients on 50 mg having to decrease 
the dose to 20 mg should switch from the PFP to vial/syringe presentation. A scientific bridge 
between PFP and vial/syringe presentations was established from a PK and efficacy perspective. 
From a PK perspective, comparison of fitusiran PK administered via the PFP and vial/syringe 
was performed as part of phase 3 study LTE15174. In Study LTE15174, 12 subjects were first 
administered the 50-mg fitusiran dose using the glass vial with syringe, and then the next dose of 
50 mg was administered with PFP. Eleven subjects completed both glass vial with syringe and 
PFP dose administrations. As shown in Table 12 below, the two formulations were bioequivalent 
with the 90% CIs of maximum plasma concentration, area under the concentration-time curve to

6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To 
Demonstrate Efficacy 
In support of the proposed indication, the Applicant initially conducted three phase 3 parent 
studies (i.e., EFC14768 [ATLAS-INH], EFC14769 [ATLAS-A/B], and EFC15110 [ATLAS-
PPX]) using a fixed fitusiran dose of 80 mg monthly to evaluate the efficacy of fitusiran in adults 
and adolescents with hemophilia A or B with or without inhibitors. Study EFC14768 was a 
randomized, open-label, controlled trial in subjects with hemophilia A or B with inhibitors. 
Study EFC14769 was a randomized, open-label, controlled trial in subjects with hemophilia A or 
B without inhibitors. Study EFC15110 was a single-arm trial in subjects with hemophilia A or B 
with or without inhibitors who were switched to fitusiran from factor or BPA prophylaxis.  
In October 2020, the Applicant received reports of nonfatal vascular thrombotic events in 
subjects on fitusiran in the ongoing phase 3 program. Pending further assessment of these events, 
and in accordance with the recommendations of an external, independent Data Monitoring 
Committee and Scientific Steering Committee, the Applicant decided to voluntarily pause dosing 
and enrollment in all ongoing phase 3 clinical studies using the 80 mg QM regimen as of 
October 30, 2020, and notified the Agency on November 2, 2020. The original statistical analysis

plan for summary of clinical efficacy/integrated summary of effectiveness was submitted on

The Applicant proposed a revised dose regimen and restarted the evaluation of the drug in a 
long-term extension study (LTE15174 [ATLAS-OLE]). The fitusiran treatment period of the two 
parent phase 3 studies EFC14768 and EFC24769, was completed as of January 26, 2021. 
Following completion of these parent studies, subjects rolled over into the long-term extension 
study. Subjects who have not completed study EFC15110 will also roll into the long-term 
extension study after study completion. The revised dose (AT-DR) in the long-term extension 
study consists of a starting dose of 50 mg Q2M, with options to escalate to 50 mg QM and 80 mg 
QM, and to de-escalate to 20 mg Q2M and 20 mg QM. The dose administered is determined on 
an individual basis in order to maintain AT level between 15 to 35%. 
When this new dosing regimen was implemented, Studies EFC14768 and EFC14769 were near 
completion; thus, the AT-DR was not implemented in either of these studies. In switching to 
Study EFC15110, the 50 mg Q2M dose was implemented in 2 subjects. The AT-DR was fully 
implemented in the extension Study LTE15174. Due to the change in dosing regimen, efficacy of 
fitusiran AT-DR was established by a comparison of data from subjects in the extension study 
LTE15174 with the data from subjects in the control arms of the parent studies.

ATLAS-INH (EFC14768) 
ATLAS-INH (EFC14768) was a multicenter, randomized, open-label study in male subjects 
aged ≥12 years, with hemophilia A or B and inhibitory antibodies to factor VIII (FVIII) or factor

administered SC as prophylaxis once monthly or on-demand BPAs. Fitusiran-treated subjects

could also receive on-demand BPAs for treatment of breakthrough bleeding episodes. On-
demand use of BPAs was defined as the use of these agents, as needed, for episodic bleeding, 
and not as a regular regimen intended to prevent spontaneous bleeding. For subjects in the BPA 
on-demand arm (i.e., those not receiving fitusiran), bleeding management therapy with BPAs 
was managed based on the local standard practice for treating hemophilia subjects with 
inhibitors, as routinely administered by the physician. Randomization was stratified by the 
number of bleeding episodes in the 6 months prior to screening (≤10 versus >10). All subjects 
were treated for 9 months, and upon completion, eligible for participation in an open-label 
extension study (Study LTE15174). See a detailed description of the study design and bleed 
management guidelines in Section 15.1.

Study LTE15174 (ATLAS-OLE) 
Study LTE15174 (ATLAS-OLE) was a multicenter, open-label extension study in male subjects 
aged ≥12 years, with hemophilia A or B, with or without inhibitory antibodies to FVIII or FIX. 
The study included subjects who had completed any of the fitusiran phase 3 hemophilia A or B 
studies (Study EFC14768, Study EFC14769, and Study EFC15110, also referred to as “parent” 
studies) to continue to be evaluated for long-term safety and efficacy. Additionally, this study 
collected efficacy data for the AT-DR. Initially, subjects received a fixed dose of fitusiran 80 mg 
QM. Following the dosing pause for cases of thrombotic events in October 2020, subjects were 
switched to the AT-DR. The estimated total time on the study for a subject who started fitusiran 
treatment before the 2020 dosing pause is up to 90 months, and up to 62 months for a subject 
who started fitusiran treatment after the lifting of the pause. Bleeding episodes were treated per 
Bleed Management Guidelines and management during surgery is outlined in Section 15. Use of 
prothrombin complex concentrates and emicizumab were not permitted during the study. A 
detailed study design is described in Section 15.3. For Pool A, the fitusiran-treated subjects who 
were used from LTE15174 were those with HAwI or HBwI who were originally randomized to 
fitusiran in Study EFC14768 and who received AT-DR during Study LTE15174.

6.2.1.2. Objectives and Endpoints, Pool A 
Objectives 
The primary objective was to evaluate the efficacy of fitusiran treatment at the revised AT-DR 
compared to on-demand treatment with BPAs, as determined by the frequency of bleeding 
episodes. 
Endpoints 
The primary efficacy endpoint was annualized bleeding rate (ABR) for treated bleeds in the 
fitusiran primary efficacy period compared to the control period (refer to Table 14 in Section 6.2 
for the definition of analysis periods). The number of bleeding episodes was annualized for each 
subject using the following formula: ABR for treated bleeds ="total number of bleeding 
episodes"/"total number of days in the respective period" × 365.25. Treated bleeds were defined 
as bleeding episodes that required administration of factor concentrates or BPA infusion for 
management, e.g., hemarthrosis, muscle, or mucosal bleeding. 
The key secondary endpoints are listed below: 
1. Annualized treated spontaneous bleeding rate (AsBR) in the primary efficacy period and the 
control period. 
2. Annualized treated joint bleeding rate (AjBR) in the primary efficacy period and the control 
period. 
3. Change in Haemophilia Quality of Life Questionnaire for Adults (Haem-A-QoL) Physical 
Health domain score in the primary efficacy period and the control period. 
4. Time to first treated bleeding episode in the primary efficacy period and control period (this 
endpoint was not included in the testing hierarchy of the parent Study EFC14768). 
Haem-A-QoL Physical Health Domain Score 
The Haem-A-QoL Physical Health domain is a 5-item domain in the 46-item Haem-A-QoL, a 
patient-reported outcome (PRO) instrument. The Physical Health domain is designed to assess 
patient-reported hemophilia-related symptoms (painful swellings and presence of joint pain) and 
physical functioning (pain with movement and difficulty walking far). Each item is rated on a 
5-point verbal rating scale ranging from “Never” to “All the time.” The recall period is the 
previous 4 weeks. The Haem-A-QoL was administered by paper at baseline, and month 9. 
Endpoints for Study EFC14768 are described in Section 15.1. 
6.2.1.3. Eligibility Criteria, Pool A 
Eligibility criteria for Study EFC14768 are described below. Study LTE15174 included subjects 
who completed a previous parent phase 3 clinical study of fitusiran and had central AT activity 
level ≥22% (prior to dosing). 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 64 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
43 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Study EFC14768 
Key Inclusion Criteria 
1. Males ≥12 years of age. 
2. Severe hemophilia A or B with inhibitors evidenced by: 
a. A central laboratory measurement or documented medical record evidence of FVIII 
<1% or FIX level ≤2% at Screening. 
b.  On-demand use of BPAs to manage bleeding episodes for at least the last 6 months 
prior to Screening, and meeting one of the following Nijmegen-modified Bethesda 
assay results criteria: 
i. 
Inhibitor titer of ≥0.6 BU/mL at Screening, or 
ii. 
Inhibitor titer of <0.6 BU/mL at Screening with medical record evidence of 2 
consecutive titers ≥0.6 BU/mL, or 
iii. 
Inhibitor titer of <0.6 BU/mL at Screening with medical record evidence of 
anamnestic response. 
3. A minimum of 6 bleeding episodes requiring BPA treatment within the last 6 months prior to 
Screening. 
Key Exclusion Criteria 
1. Known coexisting bleeding disorders other than hemophilia A or B, e.g., Von Willebrand’s 
disease, additional factor deficiencies, or platelet disorders. 
2. Current participation in immune tolerance induction therapy. 
3. Current use of BPAs as regularly administered prophylaxis designed to prevent spontaneous 
bleeding episodes. 
4. AT activity <60% at Screening, as determined by central laboratory measurement, in order to 
exclude patients with underlying congenital AT deficiency and to decrease the risk of 
excessively low AT activity levels. 
5. Presence of clinically significant liver disease. 
6. Estimated glomerular filtration rate ≤45 mL/min/1.73 m2 (using the Modification of Diet in 
Renal Disease formula). 
7. Coexisting thrombophilic disorder. 
8. History of arterial or venous thromboembolism. 
6.2.1.4. Statistical Analysis Plan, Pool A  
Analysis of the Primary Endpoint 
The primary endpoint was analyzed using a negative binomial model with the number of 
bleeding episodes as the response variable, and treatment arm and randomization strata of 
number of bleeds in the 6 months prior to study (≤10, >10) as fixed effects. The logarithm of the 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 65 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
44 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
duration (in years) that each subject spent in each study period matching the bleeding episode 
data being analyzed (i.e., treatment period from Study EFC15174 and control period from Study 
EFC14768) were included as an offset variable to account for unequal follow-up time due to 
different durations of the treatment and control periods, early withdrawal, surgery, etc. 
Analysis of Key Secondary Endpoints 
Annualized Treated Spontaneous Bleeding Rate 
The same primary analysis method for the primary endpoint was used for this key secondary 
endpoint. 
Annualized Treated Joint Bleeding Rate 
The same primary analysis method for the primary endpoint was used for this key secondary 
endpoint. 
Change in Haem-A-QoL Physical Health Domain Score 
Change from baseline in Haem-A-QoL Physical Health domain score (in subjects ≥17 years of 
age) in the control period and primary efficacy period were analyzed using a mixed model for 
repeated measure analysis (MMRM) with robust sandwich covariance matrix, with fixed effects, 
visit of study period (one postbaseline in control period and two postbaselines in primary 
efficacy period), the randomization strata of number of bleeding episodes in the 6 months prior 
to EFC14768 study entry, and baseline Haem-A-QoL Physical Health domain score. We show 
the Haem-A-QoL Physical Health results for completeness but do not consider the findings 
reliable for reasons discussed later in this review. 
Time to First Treated Bleeding Episode 
The risk of bleeding in the primary efficacy period and control period was compared using a Cox 
proportional hazards regression model. For intent-to-treat (ITT) Pool A, treatment arm and 
randomization strata of number of bleeding episodes (≤10, >10) in the 6 months prior to 
EFC14768 study entry were fixed effects.  
Multiple Testing Strategy 
To control the familywise error rate at an alpha level of 0.05 in the testing of the primary and key 
secondary endpoints for each pool, a hierarchical testing approach was used. For ITT pool A, the 
endpoints were tested in the following order (Table 16 below):

If testing of any of the endpoints was not statistically significant, formal testing of subsequent 
endpoints would be stopped and the null hypothesis for subsequent tests would not be rejected. 
Note that Endpoints #1 to #4 in Table 9 were prespecified primary and key secondary endpoints 
in the protocol of the parent study EFC147689. However, the endpoint of the time to first treated 
bleeding episode (Endpoint #5) in the primary efficacy period and control period was not 
prespecified as a key secondary endpoint. Therefore, the review team does not consider it as a 
prespecified multiplicity-adjusted endpoint for the Pool A analysis. As a result, the endpoint of 
time to first treated bleeding episode in the primary efficacy period will not be used to support a 
labeling claim of fitusiran AT-DR. 
Handling of Intercurrent Events and Missing Data 
Inclusion of bleeding events during the intercurrent events, such as perioperative period, after 
fitusiran treatment discontinuation, or use of other medications influencing hemostasis 
(Table 17) could significantly bias the evaluation of the effect of fitusiran and make treatment 
comparisons difficult to interpret. To avoid potential selection bias, a combination of on-
treatment and hypothetical strategies were used to deal with intercurrent events (Table 18).

interruption 
periods 
a) If the gap between the last dose date of the parent study 
(EFC14768/EFC14769/EFC15110) on a new dose regimen and the first

additional dosing interval (28 days). Such a pause in dosing was expected 
to result in antithrombin (AT) recovery to a level that would no longer

period of major

period of minor 
surgery

control period for Pool C was prohibited.

VI. Subjects exit

Subjects exit the program before Dose Restart Day 1 of fitusiran with a new dose

Source: Summary of Clinical Efficacy Hemophilia A and B, Table 5. 
Abbreviations: BPA, bypassing agent; Q2M, every 2 months; QM, once monthly 
The primary analysis estimand was based on a combination of on treatment and hypothetical 
strategies. 
For subjects who were followed up in either the control or treatment period, the on-treatment 
strategy was considered of greatest relevance in assessing the primary objective. Bleeding 
episodes during intercurrent events I(a), I(b), I(d), I(e), and II through V were excluded from this 
analysis. 
The hypothetical strategy for subjects who received at least one dose of the AT-DR but 
permanently discontinued treatment during the dose adjustment period due to low AT level 
(intercurrent event I(c)) was used to address the scientific question concerning the outcomes if 
these subjects had continued the treatment and been followed until the end of the primary 
efficacy period. The current clinical development plan under the AT-DR was to evaluate the 
safety and efficacy when the AT level was between 15 and 35%. Per protocol, subjects who had 
two AT levels below 15% were required to de-escalate to a lower dose or permanently 
discontinue fitusiran treatment to mitigate vascular thrombotic risk. Available clinical data 
suggested that lower AT levels were associated with improved ABR. For those subjects who 
permanently discontinued fitusiran treatment due to AT levels below 15%, directly using 
bleeding data from such subjects may create a bias to artificially improve the overall fitusiran 
ABR. Rather, it was hypothesized that those subjects may have an ABR similar to other subjects 
with AT levels at 15 to 35%. Therefore, the approach of missing at random was adopted. 
The hypothetical strategy for subjects who exit the program before Restart Day 1 of fitusiran 
with the AT-DR (intercurrent event VI) was considered to address the scientific question 
concerning the outcomes if these subjects had remained in the program, received the new 
regimen, and were followed until the end of the primary efficacy period.

On-treatment

Abbreviations: ISE, integrated summary of effectiveness; SAP, statistical analysis plan 
Handling of Missing Data in the Primary Analysis 
For subjects who received at least one dose of fitusiran after dose pause and permanently 
discontinued treatment during the dose adjustment period, if the permanent treatment 
discontinuation was not due to low AT level, the dose adjustment period was counted as the 
primary efficacy period, available bleeding episode data starting from the Dose Restart Day 1 
visit to end of study was used, and intercurrent events I(a), I(b), I(d), I(e), and II to V (Table 17) 
would apply.
