Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
No imputation of missing data in the control period was performed for subjects who withdrew in 
the control period. 
For the primary efficacy period, unobserved data due to intercurrent event I(c) and VI were 
addressed as follows: 
 For subjects who received no dose of fitusiran under the AT-DR but at least one dose of 
fitusiran (i.e., 80 mg QM) before dose pause, or who received no dose of fitusiran under 
either the initial regimen (i.e., 80 mg QM) or AT-DR, the unobserved bleeding data during 
the primary efficacy period (Dose Restart Day 169 to Dose Restart Day 364) were imputed 
via a multiple imputation (MI) method with a missing at random (MAR) assumption based 
on observed bleeding data from Dose Restart Day 169 to Dose Restart Day 364 for subjects 
who were randomized to the fitusiran arm in EFC14768/EFC14769 study or exposed to 
fitusiran in EFC15110 study, and also dosed after the dose adjustment period, with on-
treatment strategy applied to intercurrent events I(a), I(b), I(d), I(e), II to V. 
 For subjects who permanently discontinued fitusiran during the dose adjustment period due 
to low AT level, the unobserved bleeding data during the primary efficacy period were 
imputed via MI method with MAR assumption based on observed bleeding data from the 
primary efficacy period for subjects who were randomized to the fitusiran arm in 
EFC14768/EFC14769 study or exposed to fitusiran in EFC15110 study, and also dosed with 
20 mg of fitusiran during the primary efficacy period, with on-treatment strategy applied to 
intercurrent events I(a), I(b), I(d), I(e), II to V. 
6.2.1.5. Results of Analyses, Pool A 
Subject Disposition 
ITT Pool A was comprised of fitusiran-treated subjects in Study LTE15174 and on-demand 
BPA-treated subjects in Study EFC14768 maintaining the original randomization either to 
fitusiran or on-demand BPA. Thirty-five (92.1%) subjects were enrolled and entered the fitusiran 
prophylaxis arm in the treatment period (dose resumption Day 1 to Day 364). There were 31 
(81.6%) subjects who completed the study period and 4 (10.5%) who did not. Four (10.5%) 
subjects discontinued the study because more than 1 AT measured <15% (Table 19).

number of subjects with given characteristic 
The small sample size of HBwI subjects (N=12) compared to HAwI subjects (N=45) is expected 
as HA is more common than HB, with a prevalence of one in 5,000 male live births compared to 
one in 30,000, respectively (Castaman and Matino 2019). 
The majority of subjects enrolled in Pool A (nearly 90%) were not from the United States. This 
is expected, as standard of care in the United States has shifted to use of prophylaxis factor 
replacement for bleeding, rather than on-demand factor therapy. The efficacy outcomes in ex-
U.S. patients are expected to be the same as U.S. patients, as there are no known demographic 
factors that should alter the response to study drug. In addition, when treating patients with 
hemophilia, clinical practice can vary; however, the pathophysiology of hemophilia does not 
differ based on country. Therefore, based on the mechanism of action of fitusiran, there is no 
rationale for a difference in efficacy based on race or geographic region. 
Additional baseline characteristics are described in Section 16.1. 
Analysis of the Primary Endpoint 
Pool A met its primary efficacy objective of superiority of fitusiran prophylaxis over BPA on 
demand in reducing ABR for treated bleeds. The estimated ABR was 19.1 (95% CI: 11.8, 31.0) 
for the BPA on demand group and 5.1 (95% CI: 2.7, 9.5) for the fitusiran prophylaxis (AT-DR) 
group, with a rate ratio of 0.27 (95% CI: 0.13, 0.57; p=0.0006). Results for the primary efficacy 
endpoint analysis are summarized in Table 21. There was no pre-planned formal comparison 
between the fitusiran 80-mg dose and the AT-DR. The parent study efficacy results for the 
80 mg QM dose are also included in the table for reference. The Applicant’s primary analysis 
results were confirmed by the FDA statistical review team.

In additional to a nonsignificant p-value, the FDA review team had concerns about the 
interpretability for the PRO data of Haem-A-QOL Physical Health domain score given the lack 
of baseline values and other limitations. For details, please refer to Section 16.5. The fourth key 
secondary endpoint of the time to first treated bleeding episode should be considered exploratory 
only, since the prior endpoint in the testing hierarchy, Haem-A-QOL Physical Health domain 
score, failed to yield a significant p-value. Further, this endpoint was not proposed in the parent 
study EFC14768. In addition, while it is clearly beneficial to reduce the number of treated 
bleeds, it is not clear whether reducing the time to first treated bleed is a clinically meaningful 
endpoint.
 
Subgroup Analysis of the Primary Endpoints 
Subgroup analyses were conducted to assess the potential for differences in the treatment effect 
of fitusiran prophylaxis compared to BPA on-demand across various demographic and clinical 
characteristic groups. Overall, the treatment effect was consistent across baseline subgroups, 
including hemophilia type, prior bleeding events, age group, race, and ethnicity. Of note, these 
subgroup analyses are limited by the small numbers of subjects, so the results should be 
interpreted with caution.

The subgroup analyses described above should be interpreted with caution given the small 
sample size. For example, the CI for HBwI population is wide and crosses 1; however, based on 
the mechanism of action of the drug (which acts independent of factors VIII and IX) and the 
pathophysiology of the disease, it is not expected for patients with HAwI and HBwI to respond 
differently to fitusiran. Likely, the difference in ABR is due to small sample size in the subgroup 
(N=9). With less than 10% of data from U.S. subjects (i.e., 5/57), the mean rate ratio (RR; 95% 
CI) was not estimable for this geographic region, although the observed mean (±SD) ABR for 
treated bleeds on fitusiran for the five U.S. subjects in the ITT Pool A was similar to the overall 
population: 4.8 (5.53) versus 4.4 (5.45).

6.2.2.1. Design, Pool B 
The control arm for Pool B was from Study ATLAS-A/B (EFC14769) described below. The 
treatment arm was from the extension Study ATLAS-OLE (LTE15174). For Pool B, the 
fitusiran-treated subjects who were included from LTE15174 were those with hemophilia A or B 
without inhibitors who were originally randomized to fitusiran in Study EFC14769 and who 
received AT-DR during Study LTE15174. 
ATLAS-A/B (EFC14769) was a multicenter, randomized, open-label study in male subjects aged 
≥12 years, with hemophilia A or B without inhibitory antibodies to FVIII or FIX, who were not 
receiving prophylactic therapy. The study design is shown in Figure 11. Subjects were 
randomized in a 2:1 ratio to either fitusiran 80 mg SC as prophylaxis once monthly or to on-
demand factor concentrates. The study allowed use of on-demand factor concentrates in the 
fitusiran-treated subjects for treatment of breakthrough bleeding episodes. On-demand use of 
factor concentrates is defined as the use of these agents, as needed, for episodic bleeding, and not 
on a regular regimen intended to prevent spontaneous bleeding. For subjects in the fitusiran 
treatment arm who developed breakthrough bleeding episodes, bleeding management guidelines 
were followed as outlined in Section 15. Randomization was stratified by the number of bleeding 
episodes in the 6 months prior to Screening (≤10 versus >10) and hemophilia type (hemophilia A 
or B). All subjects were treated for 9 months and upon completion, eligible for participation in an 
open-label extension study. See a detailed description of the study design in Section 15.2. 
Reference ID: 5560526

NDA 219019

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

The Study design for Study ATLAS-OLE (LTE15174) is described above in Section 6.2.1.1. 
6.2.2.2. Objectives and Endpoints, Pool B 
Objectives 
The primary objective was to evaluate the efficacy of fitusiran treatment with the revised AT-DR 
compared to factor on demand, as determined by the frequency of bleeding episodes. 
The secondary objective was to evaluate the efficacy of fitusiran compared to factor on demand, 
as determined by the frequency of treated spontaneous bleeding episodes, the frequency of 
treated joint bleeding episodes, health-related quality of life in subjects ≥17 years of age, and 
time to first treated bleeding episode. 
Endpoints 
The primary efficacy endpoint was ABR of treated bleeds in the fitusiran primary efficacy period 
compared to the control period (Table 14). 
The key secondary endpoints are listed below: 
1. Annualized treated spontaneous bleeding rate (AsBR). 
2. Annualized treated joint bleeding rate (AjBR). 
3. Change in Haemophilia Quality of Life Questionnaire for Adults (Haem-A-QoL) Physical 
Health domain score. This instrument is described in Section 6.2.1.2. 
 
 
4. Time to first treated bleeding episode (not proposed in parent Study EFC14769). 
Endpoints for Study EFC14769 are described in Section 15.2.

6.2.2.3. Eligibility Criteria, Pool B  
Eligibility criteria for Study EFC14769 are described below. Study LTE15174 included subjects 
who completed a previous parent phase 3 clinical study of fitusiran and had central AT activity 
level ≥22% (prior to dosing). 
ATLAS-A/B (EFC14769) 
Key Inclusion Criteria 
1. Males ≥12 years of age. 
2. Severe hemophilia A or B without inhibitors evidenced by: 
a. A central laboratory measurement or documented medical record evidence of FVIII 
<1% or FIX level ≤2% at Screening. 
b. On-demand use of factor concentrate to manage bleeding episodes for at least the last 
6 months prior to Screening, and meeting each of the following criterion: 
i. 
Nijmegen modified Bethesda assay inhibitor titer of <0.6 BU/mL at Screening. 
ii. 
No use of BPAs to treat bleeding episodes for at least the last 6 months prior to 
Screening. 
iii. 
No history of immune tolerance induction therapy within the last 3 years prior to 
Screening. 
3. A minimum of 6 bleeding episodes requiring factor concentrate treatment within the last 
6 months prior to Screening. 
Key Exclusion Criteria 
Key exclusion criteria are the same as Pool A as described in Section 6.2.1.3. 
6.2.2.4. Statistical Analysis Plan, Pool B 
Analysis of the Primary Endpoint 
The primary endpoint was analyzed using a negative binomial model with the number of 
bleeding episodes as the response variable, and randomization arm, the randomization strata of 
number of bleeding episodes in the 6 months prior (≤10, >10), and hemophilia type as fixed 
effects. The logarithm of the duration (in years) that each subject spent in each study period 
matching their bleeding episode data (i.e., treatment period from Study EFC15174 and control 
period from EFC14769) was included as an offset variable to account for unequal follow-up time 
due to different durations of the treatment and control periods, early withdrawal, surgery, etc. 
Analysis of Key Secondary Endpoints 
Annualized Treated Spontaneous Bleeding Rate 
The same primary analysis for the primary endpoint was used for this key secondary endpoint. 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 78 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
57 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Annualized Treated Joint Bleeding Rate 
The same primary analysis for the primary endpoint was used for this key secondary endpoint. 
Change in Haem-A-QoL Physical Health Domain Score 
Change from baseline in Haem-A-QoL Physical Health domain score (in subjects ≥17 years of 
age) in the control period and primary efficacy period was analyzed using an MMRM with 
robust sandwich covariance matrix, with fixed effects, visit of study period (one postbaseline in 
control period and two postbaselines in primary efficacy period), randomization strata of number 
of bleeding episodes in the 6 months prior to EFC14769 study entry, randomization strata of 
hemophilia type, and baseline Haem-A-QoL Physical Health domain score. We show the Haem-
A-QoL Physical Health results for completeness but do not consider the findings reliable because 
of several limitations discussed in Section 16.5. 
Time to First Treated Bleeding Episode 
The risk of bleeding in the primary efficacy period and control period was compared using a Cox 
proportional hazards regression model. For ITT Pool B, treatment arm and randomization strata 
of number of bleeding episodes in the 6 months prior to EFC14769 study entry were fixed 
effects. 
Multiple Testing Strategy 
To control the familywise error rate at the alpha level of 0.05 in the testing of the primary and 
key secondary endpoints for each pool, a hierarchical testing approach was used. For ITT Pool B, 
the endpoints were tested in the order based on primary analysis of the endpoints as described in 
Table 10. If testing of any of the endpoints was not statistically significant, formal testing of 
subsequent endpoints would be stopped and the null hypothesis for subsequent tests would not be 
rejected. 
Handling of Intercurrent Events and Missing data 
The handling method of intercurrent events and missing data for Pool B were the same as 
described in Pool A. See Section 6.2.1.4 for further details. 
6.2.2.5. Results of Analyses, Pool B

ITT Pool B was comprised of subjects in Study LTE15174 and Study EFC14769 maintaining the 
original randomization either to fitusiran or on-demand factor replacement. Subject disposition is 
described in Table 28 below.

The small sample size of HB subjects (N=27) compared to HA subjects (N=93) is expected as

The majority of subjects enrolled in Pool B (nearly 80%) were not from the United States. This is 
expected as standard of care in the United States has shifted to use of prophylaxis factor 
replacement for bleeding, rather than on-demand factor therapy. The efficacy outcomes in ex-
U.S. patients are expected to be similar to those in U.S. patients as there are no known 
demographic factors that should alter the response to study drug. In addition, when treating 
patients with hemophilia, clinical practice can vary; however, the pathophysiology of hemophilia

Analysis of the Primary Endpoint 
The study demonstrated a statistically significant reduction in ABR for treated bleeds with 
fitusiran prophylaxis compared to factor on-demand treatment in the primary efficacy period. 
The estimated ABR was 31.4 (95% CI: 20.5, 48.2) for the factor on-demand group and 9.0 (95% 
CI: 5.6, 14.5) for fitusiran prophylaxis with AT-DR. Results for the primary efficacy endpoint 
analysis are summarized in Table 30. There was no pre-planned formal comparison of ABR for 
treated bleeds between subjects who received fitusiran 80 mg prophylaxis and AT-DR fitusiran 
prophylaxis. The comparison results of 80 mg QM vs. factor on demand from the parent study 
are also presented in the table for reference. The Applicant’s primary analysis results were 
confirmed by the FDA statistical review team.

reference approach, which is a conservative control.

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues 
6.3.1. Demonstration of Benefit of Fitusiran: 
Antithrombin-Based Dose Regimen Efficacy Data 
From the Long-Term Extension Study Compared 
to Control Data From Randomized Parent Studies 
Issue 
The parent randomized controlled phase 3 studies were nearly completed when the Applicant 
switched to the AT-DR, the to-be-marketed dose of fitusiran. To assess the efficacy of the AT-
DR, the Applicant compared the efficacy data from the long-term extension study (LTE15174), 
which implemented the AT-DR, to the control arms of the parent studies (EFC14768 and 
Reference ID: 5560526
  
 
 
 
   
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
  
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 

===== PAGE 84 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
63 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
EFC14769). The review team examined whether this approach is adequate to establish the 
effectiveness of the AT-DR. 
Background 
Dosing in the phase 3 studies, EFC14768, EFC14769, and EFC15110 (referred to as parent 
studies in the rest of the section), was initiated with fitusiran at 80 mg QM. To mitigate the risk 
of thrombotic events, the AT-DR targeting an AT activity range of 15 to 35% was implemented 
when the parent studies were nearly completed. The AT-DR was fully implemented in the 
extension study, LTE15174. 
The efficacy of fitusiran AT-DR treatment was assessed by comparing the fitusiran AT-DR 
treatment data from the long-term extension study LTE15174 to the control data from the parent 
studies (see Figure 7. This approach was discussed during the IND stage of development. At the 
Type C Guidance Meeting on September 18, 2023, and in a May 15, 2024, information request, 
the Agency raised the concern whether the time lag (approximately 2 years) between the parent 
studies and the extension study LTE15174 introduces additional confounding factors and 
ultimately reduces the confidence that this approach can establish effectiveness for the proposed 
AT-DR for fitusiran. 
Assessment 
The Applicant and the FDA clinical and statistical review team conducted thorough evaluations 
to assess the comparability between the control arm data from the parent studies and the fitusiran 
AT-DR data from the extension study. 
Pathophysiology 
The pathophysiology of severe hemophilia allows for a comparison between control and 
intervention groups across time. Congenital severe hemophilia typically presents in infancy and 
throughout the lifespan of a given patient. The clinical hallmark of severe hemophilia is 
spontaneous bleeding that requires lifelong treatment, including both prophylaxis and on-demand 
therapies for breakthrough bleeding events. In the control on-demand group, episodic treatment 
with CFC or BPA does not alter the course of the disease, and for that reason, the individual 
bleeding phenotype is not anticipated to change in the absence of prophylactic intervention over 
the study duration. Therefore, the use of the control group in the parent studies is adequate and 
not impacted by the time lag. 
Baseline Characteristics 
The parent studies’ baseline characteristics, including medical and surgical history at time of 
enrollment, concomitant medications, and prior hemophilia-directed therapies (e.g., CFC/BPA, 
prohibited medications, bleed management, etc.), were comparable between the active and 
control arms. No significant changes were observed in reported medical history or concomitant 
medications within the relatively short timeframe in which subjects were enrolled in the fitusiran 
parent and extension clinical studies. Bleed management guidelines were followed in all studies. 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 85 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
64 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Carryover Benefit From Prior Treatment With Fitusiran (i.e., Fitusiran Original Dosing 
Regimen of 80 mg QM) 
Influence From Prior Exposure to Fitusiran 
In the extension study LTE15174, among 11 participants who were never exposed to the original 
dose regimen, the estimated mean (95% CI) annualized bleed rate (ABR) for treated bleeds was 
lower than the overall AT-DR participants [1.49 (0.58, 3.85) vs. 6.41 (5.31, 7.74)] with 
nonoverlapped CI, suggesting that there was no positive influence from prior exposure to the 
original dose regimen for the estimated efficacy for AT-DR. In addition, the primary efficacy 
assessment occurred after a 6-month period in which study participants restarted fitusiran, 
targeting AT levels of 15-35%. By the end of the 6-month period, the majority of study 
participants reached AT-steady state and were on a stable dose. Therefore, as participants were 
on a steady state of fitusiran, it is unlikely that the prior exposure to fitusiran at the 80mg fixed 
dose had an influence on efficacy outcomes. 
Imbalanced Dropout Rate Between Treatment Arms 
The dropout rate is 15% to 22% higher in the fitusiran AT-DR treatment arm from the extension 
study LTE15174 in subjects originally randomized to fitusiran in the parent studies compared to 
the control arm from parent studies. The time lag between the extension study and the parent 
studies may have a considerable contribution to the imbalanced dropout rate between arms 
(please refer to the disposition tables for Pools A, B and C in Section 6.2 for further 
information). Per the FDA statistical review team’s recommendation, the Applicant conducted 
several sensitivity analyses for ABR in Pools A and B to impute the missing data due to different 
reasons, including a copy reference approach, which is a conservative control-based imputation 
that imputes unobserved bleeding data during the primary efficacy period (Dose Restart Day 169 
to Dose Restart Day 364) using the control period data distribution in the corresponding parent 
study. These analyses were consistent with the primary analysis (for details, see the sensitivity 
analysis results for the primary endpoint in Section 6.2.1.5 and 6.2.2.5), showing that the primary 
efficacy results for the primary efficacy endpoint ABR for treated bleeds are robust. The missing 
data and dropouts did not affect the final efficacy conclusion. Additional reassurance is provided 
by the large treatment effect size in which the p-value of the primary efficacy endpoint (ABR for 
treated bleeds) was <0.001, for both subjects with and without inhibitors. 
Impact of Unknown and Known Confounders 
Randomization 
Although known confounders appear to be generally balanced between the two treatment arms in 
the pooled data (please refer to the baseline demographics and clinical characteristics tables in 
Section 6.2), unknown confounders can only be balanced through randomization. To balance the 
treatment and control groups for the unknown confounders, the Applicant followed the FDA 
statistical review team’s recommendation to maintain the original randomization used in the 
parent studies for the pooled analyses, i.e., the fitusiran new dose regimen arm should only 
include subjects who were randomized to the fitusiran arm in the parent study. The efficacy 
analyses were conducted according to the intent-to-treat (ITT) principle, preserving the 
randomization in the parent studies. 
Reference ID: 5560526

extension study LTE15174 were compared with their own control data from the parent studies 
(Table 36).The results of the analysis reached a conclusion consistent with the primary analysis.

Conclusion 
In summary, the review team determined that the approach of comparing efficacy data from the 
long-term extension study which used the AT-DR, to the control arms in the parent studies was 
acceptable for establishing the effectiveness of the AT-DR. 
7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management) 
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on 
Nonclinical Data 
The safety of fitusiran was assessed in good laboratory practice repeat-dose toxicology studies in 
healthy animals (rats and monkeys) and in animal models of hemophilia (mice, dogs, and 
monkeys). Animal studies were conducted using the subcutaneous route of administration, which 
is consistent with the intended clinical route. To ensure continuous exposure and account for 
differences in tissue exposure half-lives, animals were generally dosed more often (once weekly) 
than the clinical dosing schedule (once per month or once every other month). Small interfering 
RNA drugs, including fitusiran, show prolonged tissue residence times (e.g., weeks to months), 
but have short intermittent plasma exposure (e.g., hours). Because of this, the relationship of 
fitusiran blood exposures (i.e., AUC) to tissue levels remains uncertain. To account for this, 
animal-to-human safety margins described herein are based on dose comparisons (instead of 
plasma exposures), which have been corrected to reflect the total fitusiran dose over time (i.e., 
mg/kg/month) and further adjusted for body surface area (BSA) differences between species. 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 88 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
67 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Fitusiran was pharmacologically active in the common laboratory strains of rats and monkeys 
that were utilized in standard toxicology studies. Because these species exhibit normal clotting 
ability at baseline, toxicity studies with fitusiran in healthy animals were anticipated to 
overestimate clotting risk to hemophilia patients. However, such studies are still anticipated to 
provide valuable information, especially for off-target safety assessment. To address the 
limitations of studies in healthy animals, the Applicant conducted extensive studies in animal 
models of hemophilia, which provided a more relevant physiological system with which to 
assess on-target risk for patients with hemophilia. Off-target risk was assessed in both healthy 
animals and hemophilia animal models. Key nonclinical safety findings and their potential 
clinical relevance are discussed below (and we refer to the detailed nonclinical study summaries 
in Section 13.1 of this review). 
Exaggerated Pharmacology/Thrombosis in Healthy Animals 
A major risk of fitusiran is anticipated to be excessive clotting related to exaggerated 
pharmacology. To begin to characterize this risk, healthy rats were subcutaneously administered 
fitusiran at up to 1 mg/kg/week for 26 weeks (representing doses below the maximum 
recommended human dose [MRHD] based on BSA extrapolation). Deaths due to systemic 
thrombosis occurred at 1 mg/kg/week, a dose that decreased AT protein levels by up to 80% (AT 
activity not measured). Lower doses resulting in AT protein reductions up to 70% were tolerated. 
In a shorter, 7-week study, rats were given up to 3 mg/kg/week (representing 2 times the MRHD 
based on BSA comparisons). The 3 mg/kg/week dose was not tolerated due to systemic clotting, 
where AT activity and protein levels were decreased up to 91% and 83%, respectively; lower 
doses that reduced AT activity by up to 88% and AT protein up to 82% were tolerated. 
In monkeys with normal coagulation at baseline, fitusiran administration at up to 
0.5 mg/kg/week (representing a dose below the MRHD based on BSA comparisons) for 
39 weeks decreased AT protein and activity up to 74% and 71%, respectively, and all doses were 
well-tolerated. Fitusiran administered to monkeys for 7 weeks at up to 1 mg/kg/week 
(representing approximately 2 times the MRHD based on BSA comparisons) decreased AT 
activity and protein by up to 83% and 84%, respectively, and deaths occurred that were 
attributed to systemic clotting. Lower doses resulting in AT protein and AT activity reductions 
up to 60% were tolerated. 
Overall, in healthy animals with normal clotting at baseline, fitusiran was generally tolerated so 
long as AT protein levels remained >10% and activity levels remained >20%. These data 
highlight the lower limits of AT protein and activity reductions in healthy animals and 
underscore the potential limitations of safety assessments with fitusiran in animals with normal 
clotting at baseline. 
Exaggerated Pharmacology/Thrombosis in Hemophilia Animal Models 
An important question was whether the hemophilia condition might prevent excessive clotting 
with fitusiran, compared to animals with normal clotting at baseline. To address this, fitusiran 
was tested in various hemophilia animal models. 
Transgenic hemophilia A (HA) mice were administered fitusiran at up to 30 mg/kg/week 
(approximately 12 times the MRHD based on BSA comparisons) for 26 weeks. AT protein 
levels were reduced up to 90% (AT activity not measured), but deaths were only observed in 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 89 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
68 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
untreated control HA mice (caused by hemophilia-related bleeding). Fitusiran administration 
reduced deaths significantly; only 6% of HA mice given fitusiran died, compared to 40% of 
untreated control HA mice.  
A 4-week pharmacodynamic study was conducted using HA dogs (a genetic model) 
administered fitusiran by subcutaneous injection of up to 10 mg/kg/week (a dose representing 
26 times the MRHD based on BSA comparisons). At 90% AT activity reduction (AT protein 
levels not measured), a dog was euthanized with evidence of systemic clotting, but AT activity 
reductions up to 70% were well-tolerated at 8 times the MRHD (based on BSA comparisons).  
In a monkey study, fitusiran was administered to healthy animals at up to 0.5 mg/kg/week 
(representing a dose below the MRHD based on BSA comparisons) for 6 weeks, which was 
followed on Day 43 by induction of an HA phenotype using an antihuman Factor VIII antibody. 
Fitusiran was well tolerated in monkeys prior to the induction of HA, where AT activity was 
decreased up to 80% with no evidence of thrombosis. Induction of HA in monkeys on Day 43 
resulted in a normalization of thrombin generation at the 0.5 mg/kg/week dose (representing a 
dose below the MRHD based on BSA comparisons). A disadvantage of this model was that the 
fitusiran dose was limited (to below the clinical dose) to prevent thrombosis prior to 
administration of the FVIII antibody. 
Taken together, studies in animal models of hemophilia underscored the utility of employing 
nontraditional species/strains in both pharmacodynamic and toxicology studies, both of which 
contributed important safety information. Overall, these data strongly suggested that animal 
models of hemophilia were less sensitive to exaggerated pharmacology with fitusiran compared 
to animals with normal clotting at baseline. In contrast to the induction of clotting seen in healthy 
animals, fitusiran was generally found to be protective (of bleeding) in animal models of 
hemophilia along with low risk of excessive clotting at clinically relevant exposures. 
Hepatobiliary Toxicity 
Subcutaneous administration of fitusiran in animals was characterized by rapid hepatic 
deposition and slow hepatic clearance, which is typical of GalNAc-conjugated liver-targeted 
siRNA drugs. Indeed, liver fitusiran concentrations were in vast excess of those measured in 
other tissues, and liver tissue half-life (~60 hours) greatly exceeded that measured in the plasma 
(1 to 3 hours). The liver was also a clear target organ of toxicity across the fitusiran nonclinical 
program. Given that the liver is both the primary site of fitusiran distribution and 
pharmacodynamic activity, it was important to assess whether fitusiran was directly hepatotoxic. 
Animal studies consistently showed minimal to moderate hepatic necrosis and bile duct 
dilatation at the highest doses in the presence of extensive systemic clotting. It is unclear how 
these biliary tract findings in rats may translate to the human gallbladder. Residual uncertainty 
regarding a clinical risk of hepatobiliary toxicity should be based on clinical trial data. 
Hypersensitivity Reactions 
Fitusiran increased the incidence of hypersensitivity reactions (skin 
redness/dryness/scab/discharge at the injection site) in rats and monkeys at all doses tested. 
However, given potential differences between animals and humans, any potential labeling of a 
risk of hypersensitivity reactions should be based on clinical trial data. 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 90 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
69 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Reproductive and Developmental Effects 
Fitusiran was not tested in reproduction studies in female animals because hemophilia A and B 
occur almost exclusively in males. In the absence of such data, the use of effective contraceptive 
measures by female patients with hemophilia should be encouraged during treatment with 
fitusiran. Fitusiran did not adversely impact male fertility and did not negatively impact early 
embryonic development in a 26-week repeat-dose chronic toxicity study in rats where male rats 
were dosed at up to 1 mg/kg/week (a dose below the MRHD based on BSA comparisons) for 
16 weeks and then mated with untreated females. 
Clinical Safety Margins 
Fitusiran, like other GalNAc conjugated siRNAs, is quickly cleared from the plasma and taken 
up by the liver and to a lesser extent other tissues. In this context, plasma exposure levels may 
inadequately reflect total tissue exposure levels over time. Therefore, clinical safety margins 
shown in Table 38 below reflect total fitusiran doses over time, with additional corrections for 
body surface area differences between species.

Risk Summary and Recommendation 
Studies of fitusiran in animal models of hemophilia suggested reduced risk of excessive clotting 
at clinically relevant doses compared to healthy animals. Hepatotoxicity in animal studies was 
observed with fitusiran at high doses in the presence of severe systemic clotting. Adverse skin 
reactions were observed at the subcutaneous injection site with fitusiran in animals, but clinical 
risk should be based on clinical trial data. No developmental or reproduction studies were 
conducted in female animals administered fitusiran because hemophilia A and B occur almost 
exclusively in males. Fitusiran did not adversely impact male fertility or reproductive 
performance, and conceptuses from dosed males were normal. 
Overall, the nonclinical data support the marketing approval of fitusiran for the treatment of 
hemophilia. Regarding specific risks identified, and for product labeling, while animal models 
helped characterize the risks of excessive thrombosis, hepatobiliary toxicity, and injection site 
hypersensitivity reactions, the product label should emphasize clinical trial data. Data obtained 
from fertility and early embryonic development studies conducted using male animals given 
fitusiran should be described in Section 13.1 of the product label (refer also to Section 8.4 of this 
review). No animal data are recommended for inclusion in Section 13.2 (Animal Toxicology) of 
the product label. Finally, based on extensive review of the nonclinical program for fitusiran, the 
Reference ID: 5560526

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Pharmacology/Toxicology review team did not identify deficiencies that preclude approval of 
fitusiran for the treatment of hemophilia A and B with or without inhibitors. 
7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on 
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors 
Potential risks or safety concerns were reviewed based on known information from approved 
non-factor-replacement therapies emicizumab, marstacimab and concizumab. Approved non-
factor-replacement products increase thrombin generation by enhancing procoagulants (anti-
FIXa/FX bispecific antibody in the case of emicizumab), or by inhibiting anticoagulant pathways 
(antithrombin siRNA therapy in the case of fitusiran, and anti-TFPI antibodies in the case of 
concizumab and marstacimab). Increases in thrombin generation are associated with thrombotic 
risk, and this risk is included in the USPI Warnings and Precautions sections of HEMLIBRA 
(emicizumab) (Genentech 2024), HYMPAVZI (marstacimab) (Pfizer Laboratories Div Pfizer Inc 
2025), and ALHEMO (concizumab) (Novo Nordisk 2024) labels. A box warning in the 
HEMLIBRA USPI relates the risk of thrombotic microangiopathy and thrombotic events when a 
cumulative amount of >100 U/kg/24 hours of aPCC was administered for 24 hours or more to 
patients receiving HEMLIBRA prophylaxis (Genentech 2024). Section 7..1 discusses the risk of 
thromboembolic events in the fitusiran development program.  
Fitusiran is an siRNA, conjugated on the sense strand with triantennary N-acetylgalactosamine 
(GalNAc) to facilitate uptake by hepatocytes. In hepatocytes, fitusiran utilizes the RNA 
interference mechanism and directs catalytic breakdown of mRNA for antithrombin. Potential 
risks or safety concerns were reviewed based on known information from approved hepatic 
targeted siRNA therapies ONPATTRO (patisiran) (Alnylam Pharmaceuticals 2023a), 
GIVLAARI (givosiran) (Alnylam Pharmaceuticals 2024b), OXLUMO (lumasiran) (Alnylam 
Pharmaceuticals 2023b), LEQVIO (inclisiran) (Novartis Pharmaceuticals Corporation 2024), and 
AMVUTTRA (vutrisiran) (Alnylam Pharmaceuticals 2024a). Transaminase elevations (alanine 
aminotransferase [ALT]) of at least 3 times the upper limit of normal (ULN) were observed in 
15% of patients treated with GIVLAARI, and hepatic toxicity is listed in the Warnings and 
Precautions section of the USPI. Section 7..3 discusses the risk of hepatotoxicity observed in the 
fitusiran development program. 
7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified 
Through Postmarket Experience 
Fitusiran is not currently approved or marketed in the United States or in any country. 
7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review 
The primary safety assessment for the intended population of subjects with HA or HB with or 
without inhibitors was based on a pooled analysis of 286 subjects who received fitusiran AT-DR 
during the fitusiran developmental program. These individuals include subjects who received the 
AT-DR during studies EFC15110 (N=2), LTE14762 (N=18), and LTE15174 (N=269). Study 
EFC15110 was an open-label switching study in subjects with HA or HB with or without 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 92 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
71 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
inhibitors, previously on BPA/factor prophylaxis. Study LTE14762 was an open-label long-term 
extension study in subjects with HA or HB with or without inhibitors who were previously 
enrolled in a phase 1 study (TDR14767). Study LTE15174 was an open-label extension study in 
subjects with HA or HB with or without inhibitors who were previously enrolled in a phase 3 
parent study (see Table 5 in Section 3.3 for further details). 
To support the analysis of safety, pooled safety assessments was based on data derived from a 
total of 335 unique subjects who received at least one dose of fitusiran (whether on the non-AT-
DR dose or AT-DR dose) while enrolled in studies LTE14762, EFC14768, EFC14769, 
EFC15110, and LTE15174. Safety results from these studies were only described when 
applicable. Figure 13 shows the subject flow and fitusiran dose in the safety population. 
A supportive assessment of safety was also conducted for the randomized controlled studies 
EFC14768 and EFC14769, separately, in order to understand the safety profile of fitusiran 
compared to a concurrent control (on-demand therapy). The safety results should be interpreted 
with the understanding that this is based on the 80 mg QM dose regimen, which is a higher dose 
than the AT-DR. These results are presented in Section 17. 
Further, an exploratory assessment of safety was conducted comparing safety results of subjects 
who received the AT-DR in LTE15174 who were originally randomized to the fitusiran 80 mg 
QM dose in studies EFC14768 and EFC14769 to the control on-demand arm from studies 
EFC14768 and EFC14769. Results are provided in Section 17. 
Adverse events of special interest identified by the Agency were thrombosis, hepatoxicity, and 
gallbladder disease. The Drug-Induced Liver Injury (DILI) Team was consulted to examine 
safety signals of gallbladder disease and hepatotoxicity.

Clinical study data were independently analyzed by the Agency. Clinical data scientists (CDSs) 
from the Division of Biomedical Informatics generated the safety tables and figures. These 
include safety tables and figures for subject disposition, exposure data, treatment-emergent 
adverse events (TEAEs), and serious adverse events (SAEs). Additional exploration and analysis 
of TEAEs were performed by the clinical reviewer using JMP, JMP Clinical, and the Office of 
Computational Science Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA)-based Adverse 
Event Diagnostics tool. 
The Applicant’s translations of verbatim terms to the MedDRA (version 25.1) preferred terms 
(PTs) for TEAEs were reviewed and found to be acceptable. The review team did not identify 
any major data quality or integrity issues that precluded performing a thorough safety review.  
TEAEs were defined as any adverse event after receiving a dose of the study drug. TEAEs were 
collected after the last administration of fitusiran, until AT activity levels returned to at least 
60%. All adverse events (AEs) in the reviewed trials were graded by investigators using the 
terms mild (asymptomatic or mild symptoms, intervention not indicated), moderate (minimal, 
local or noninvasive intervention indicated; limiting age-appropriate instrumental activities of 
daily living), and severe (severe or medically significant but not immediately life-threatening; 
hospitalization or prolongation of hospitalization indicated; disabling; limiting self-care activities 
of daily living; life-threatening; or death). 
TEAEs were analyzed at all levels of the MedDRA hierarchy and using FDA Medical Queries 
(FMQ) narrow. FMQ (narrow) are a collection of approximately 110 standardized groups, where 
each group comprises adverse event terms that reflect a specific medical concept. Each FMQ 
term is composed of one or more PTs. FMQs are not mutually exclusive; that is, an individual 
preferred term (PT) may be counted in more than one FMQ. When reporting FMQ analyses, all 
FMQs, including overlapping terms, are included in the presentation of the data in this review. In 
addition, not all PTs are included in an FMQ. To assure that all potential safety signals are 
identified, analysis of all reported TEAEs not included in at least one FMQ were identified and 
analyzed. 
Review of safety was based upon: 
 Clinical Study Reports for studies 
 Protocols for studies 
 Data sets for the populations from clinical studies described above (data cutoff June 14, 
2023) 
 Summary of clinical safety 
 120-day safety update (data cutoff March 1, 2024) 
 Subject narratives 
 Case report forms 
 Case report forms and narratives were provided and reviewed for SAEs, and adverse events 
of special interest

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database 
The exposure for the primary safety population AT-DR period is shown in Table 39. The median 
duration of exposure to AT-DR was 96 (range 72.7 to 117.9) weeks. Approximately 84% of 
subjects were exposed to fitusiran AT-DR for 50 weeks or longer, with a total exposure to the 
AT-DR of 486 subject-years. 
The size of the safety database is considered adequate for a comprehensive safety assessment of 
fitusiran for the proposed indication, subject population, dosage regimen, and duration, given the 
rarity of hemophilia, and is consistent with other drug development programs for severe 
hemophilia.

7.5.1. Safety Results 
Fitusiran has a serious risk of thromboembolism, similar to other approved drugs for patients 
with hemophilia; however, fitusiran also has serious risks of gallbladder disease and 
hepatotoxicity. Overall, few subjects had serious adverse reactions or required drug 
discontinuation or dose interruption on the AT-DR. Safety analysis showed fitusiran to have an 
acceptable safety profile in subjects with either HA or HB, with or without inhibitors. No new 
safety concerns were identified in the 120-day safety update. As the AT-DR was implemented in 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 96 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
75 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
mainly long-term extension studies, the study design did not allow for randomized controlled 
safety data. 
Key safety findings of adverse event analyses of the AT-DR are: 
 There were no fatal adverse events on the AT-DR. 
 Serious adverse events (SAEs) occurred in 14.3% in subjects who received the AT-DR. The 
most common SAEs were hemorrhage (8.7%), which is most likely related to the underlying 
disease and unlikely to be related to fitusiran based on its mechanism of action and disease of 
study. Serious adverse reactions considered related to fitusiran occurred in 1.4% of subjects 
and included pancreatitis, cholecystitis, cholangitis, alanine aminotransferase increased, 
aspartate aminotransferase increased, and embolic stroke. 
 Rates of drug interruption and drug discontinuation were low. In total, two subjects (0.7%) 
experienced TEAEs that led to drug interruption. Both events leading to drug interruption 
were due to elevated liver enzymes and were determined to be related to fitusiran. Five 
subjects (1.7%) experienced TEAEs that led to permanent discontinuation of fitusiran. 
Adverse reactions leading to drug discontinuation included liver injury, postoperative deep 
vein thrombosis, cerebral infarction, and pruritus. 
 A total of 83.2% of subjects experienced a TEAE. The most common adverse reactions 
(>10%) were viral infection (29%), nasopharyngitis (26%), and bacterial infection (11%). 
Less common adverse reactions were hepatic injury, arthralgia, prothrombin fragment 1.2 
increased, injection site reaction, headache, cough, dyspepsia and abdominal pain. 
7.5.2. Safety Results, Pooled Trials LTE15174, 
LTE14762, and EFC15110, AT-DR Period 
7.5.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events 
Summary, Pooled Trials LTE15174, LTE14762, and 
EFC15110, AT-DR Period 
Table 40 summarizes the overall safety results in the pooled trials LTE15174, LTE14762 and 
EFC15110, AT-DR period, based on frequency analysis. Most TEAEs were reported as mild or 
moderate in severity, and 9.8% of subjects had a reported severe TEAE. There were no TEAEs 
leading to death. SAEs were reported in 14.3% of subjects; however, many of these were related 
to hemorrhagic events, due to underlying hemophilia. There were five TEAEs that led to 
permanent discontinuation of the study drug, and two TEAEs leading to drug interruption. 
Overall, there did not appear to be a difference in overall TEAEs based on hemophilia A or B 
nor inhibitor or noninhibitor subtypes.

7.5.2.2. Deaths, Pooled Analyses, Pooled Trials LTE15174, 
LTE14762, and EFC15110, AT-DR Period 
No deaths were reported in Pooled Trials LTE15174, LTE14762, and EFC15110, AT-DR 
Period. 
In supportive safety analyses, two subjects died while receiving the original fitusiran dosing 
regimen (80 mg every month). Brief subject narratives are provided below. 
1. Subject 
 was a 48-year-old white male with hemophilia A with inhibitors 
and a complicated medical history including hepatitis C and HIV. The subject completed 
parent study EFC14769 and developed a TESAE of “metastases to peritoneum” while 
participating in extension study LTE15174. The subject was diagnosed with metastatic 
adenocarcinoma (primary unknown). The subject’s death due to metastatic adenocarcinoma 
occurred approximately 6 months after enrolling into extension Study LTE15174.This event 
Reference ID: 5560526
(b) (6)

===== PAGE 98 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
77 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
is not considered related to fitusiran, as there is no evidence that fitusiran increases the risk of 
malignancy. Hepatitis C can increase the risk of hepatocellular carcinoma, although it is 
unclear whether the liver was the source of the subject’s cancer. 
2. Subject 
 was a 25-year-old white male with hemophilia A without 
inhibitors who completed Study TDR14767 and enrolled in extension Study LTE14762. The 
subject experienced a TEAE of cerebral venous sinus thrombosis (CVST) that resulted in a 
fatal outcome. On Day 452, the study subject received his last dose of fitusiran before the 
start of the event of cerebral venous sinus thrombosis. During the long-term extension study 
(date not specified), the subject received three doses of factor VIII for hip pain, with doses of 
31 IU/kg to 46 IU/kg. About 1 week later, the study subject presented with severe headache, 
vomiting, and fever, and was subsequently admitted to the hospital (40 days after the last 
dose of fitusiran). While initially suspected to have viral meningitis, a computed tomography 
scan performed to evaluate the headache was read as a subarachnoid hemorrhage, and the 
study subject was treated with replacement factor therapy two to three times daily. The 
subject subsequently deteriorated and died. Postmortem independent neuro-radiology 
consultants determined that the initial neuroimaging revealed it was a CVST (not 
subarachnoid hemorrhage) that led to the subject's initial presentation and subsequent 
cerebral edema and death. 
While the CVST event basis is multifactorial, related to recent factor administration and potential 
infection, the event is also likely related to fitusiran, given its long pharmacodynamic half-life. 
Following this event, the Applicant implemented Bleed Management Guidelines which include 
delineation of the use and dose of FVIII when treating breakthrough bleeds. See Section 7..1 for 
further discussion on thrombosis risk. 
7.5.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Pooled 
Trials LTE15174, LTE14762, and EFC15110, AT-DR 
Period. 
The frequency of SAEs was 14.3% in subjects who received the AT-DR. Table 41 provides a 
frequency analysis of serious TEAEs by preferred terms and Table 42 provides a frequency 
analysis by FMQs that occurred during the AT-DR period. The most common SAE was 
hemorrhage, which occurred in 8.7% of subjects, most likely related to hemophilia and unlikely 
to be related to treatment with fitusiran based on its procoagulant mechanism of action. 
Serious adverse reactions related to fitusiran occurred in 1.4% of subjects and included 
pancreatitis, cholecystitis, cholangitis, alanine aminotransferase increased, aspartate 
aminotransferase increased, and embolic stroke. Subject narratives are summarized below. See 
Section 7..2 for a discussion of acute gallbladder disease (including cholecystitis, cholelithiasis, 
and related complications), Section 7..3 for a discussion of hepatotoxicity, and Section 7..1 for a 
discussion of embolic stroke. Of note, adverse events that occurred perioperatively were not 
considered SAEs per the Applicant. 
There was one event of pneumonitis. This was not considered related to fitusiran as it occurred 
on day 606 of the study and the subject recovered after receiving antiviral and antibiotics. 
Fitusiran was not interrupted or discontinued.

Selected SAE Narratives Considered To Be Serious Adverse Reactions 
 Subject 
 was a 50-year-old male with HA with inhibitors and a history of 
diabetes mellitus, hypertension and hepatitis C who experienced an SAE of cholangitis. On 
Day 777 of Study LTE15174, he presented with symptoms of pancreatitis. Imaging showed 
obstruction due to a common bile duct stone. The subject was hospitalized and diagnosed 
with obstructive cholangitis, and 4 days later underwent pancreatobiliary sphincterotomy. 
The subject was treated with recombinant factor VIIa at the time of surgery. Fitusiran dose 
had been interrupted before the SAE of cholangitis due to a prior event of aspartate 
aminotransferase (AST) increase on Day 677 and was resumed 2 days after the 
sphincterotomy, and the subject remains on fitusiran. The review team assessed this SAE as 
likely to be related to fitusiran based on the association of fitusiran and development of 
gallbladder disease (see Section 7.6.2).

 Subject 
 was a 27-year-old male with hemophilia who experienced a 
SAE of cholecystitis. On Day 52 of LTE15174, the subject developed biliary colic and was 
admitted to hospital. A CT scan with contrast showed no visible gallstones. Magnetic 
resonance cholangiopancreatography showed dilated intrahepatic bile ducts, but no 
gallstones. On Day 56, the study subject recovered from the event of biliary colic and was 
discharged from the hospital on the same day. Fitusiran was permanently discontinued due to 
the event of biliary colic and the last dose was administered on Day 114. On Day 230 
(116 days after the last dose of fitusiran), the study subject recovered from the event of 
biliary colic. The review team assessed this SAE as likely to be related to fitusiran based on 
the association of fitusiran and development of gallbladder disease (see Section 7.6.2). 
 Subject 
 was a 27-year-old male who experienced the SAEs of 
cholecystitis, alanine aminotransferase increased, aspartate aminotransferase 
increased, and pancreatitis. On Day 136 of LTE15174, he experienced symptoms of 
cholecystitis, and an ultrasound showed gallbladder stones. On Day 151, AEs of ALT 
increased (ALT >3×ULN at 411 IU/L (normal range: 0 to 41 IU/L, baseline value: 12 IU/L 
[normal range: 10 to 40 IU/L]), aspartate aminotransferase increased (AST >3×ULN at 467 
IU/L (normal range: 0 to 40 IU/L, baseline value: 13 IU/L [normal range: 10 to 43 IU/L]), 
pancreatitis, and cholecystitis were reported and the subject was hospitalized. The study 
subject was discharged from the hospital on Day 156. On Day 217, the subject was 
readmitted to hospital with symptoms of abdominal pain, and an ultrasound of the 
gallbladder showed multiple subcentimetric stones in the gallbladder lumen. The subject 
recovered from the SAEs of alanine aminotransferase increased, aspartate aminotransferase 
increased, and pancreatitis. Fitusiran was continued without interruption. The review team 
considers that fitusiran is related to the SAEs of cholecystitis, alanine aminotransferase 
increased, aspartate aminotransferase increased, and pancreatitis as acute gallbladder disease 
has been identified as serious adverse reactions with fitusiran (see Section 7.6.2). 
 Subject 
 is a 54-year-old male with hemophilia who experienced an SAE 
of embolic stroke. The subject had a history of hypertension, hyperlipidemia, and diabetes. 
The subject was initially on fitusiran 80 mg QM during Study EFC14769, and then 
transitioned to the AT-DR in Study LTE15174. On Day 327, the fitusiran dose was reduced 
to 50 mg Q2M based on AT levels. On Day 508, fitusiran was permanently discontinued due 
to persistently low AT levels. On Day 673 (165 days after the last dose of fitusiran), the 
subject developed acute symptoms of lightheadedness and dizziness in the setting of elevated 
blood pressure, and subsequent slurring of speech and loss of strength in the right arm and 
leg. He was hospitalized. MRI showed acute nonhemorrhagic infarcts in the left corona 
radiata and ganglio-capsular region, chronic ischemic changes in the bilateral fronto-parietal 
region, and chronic lacunar infarcts in bilateral ganglio-capsular region. On Day 687 
(179 days after the last dose of fitusiran), the study subject recovered from the event embolic 
stroke and was discharged from the hospital. Given the multiple risk factors for stroke, it is 
difficult to discern if fitusiran contributed to the event. Due to the mechanism of action of 
fitusiran and fitusiran’s long pharmacodynamic half-life, it is possible that fitusiran increased 
the thrombotic risk. While the subject had discontinued the drug 165 days prior to the event, 
the subject had discontinued fitusiran due to AT <15%, a strong risk factor for thrombosis. 
At the time of the event, the AT level was 36%.

7.5.2.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment 
Discontinuation, Pooled Trials LTE15174, LTE14762, 
and EFC15110, AT-DR Period. 
During the AT-DR period, there were two subjects (0.7%) who experienced TEAEs that led to 
drug interruption. Both events leading to drug interruption were due to elevated liver enzymes 
and were determined to be related to fitusiran (see Section 7.6.3 for further discussion on 
hepatotoxicity). In total, five subjects (1.7%) experienced TEAEs that led to permanent 
discontinuation of fitusiran. Adverse reactions leading to drug discontinuation included liver 
injury, postoperative deep vein thrombosis, cerebral infarction, and pruritus. Subject narratives 
are summarized below. Risk of thrombosis with fitusiran is further discussed in Section 7.6.1. 
Subject narratives for TEAEs leading to drug interruption: 
1. Subject 
 experienced transaminases increased (PT). The study 
subject was a 35-year-old male with HA and a history of hepatitis C. On Day 1515 of 
LTE14762, ALT was 531 IU/L (normal range: 0 to 50 IU/L) and AST was 288 IU/L (normal 
range: 0 to 50 IU/L). These elevations were associated with an episode of alcohol 
consumption as reported by the study subject. Fitusiran was interrupted with the last dose 
given on Day 1480. The subject was instructed to abstain from alcohol; no other corrective 
treatment was given. The transaminases returned to the normal range by Day 1564, and 
fitusiran was restarted on Day 1564. The liver enzymes remained normal until Day 1955 
when ALT was >3×ULN, with ALT at 309 IU/L (normal range: 10 to 40 IU/L) and AST at 
139 IU/L (normal range: 10 to 43 IU/L). No further details regarding the event were 
provided. The subject interrupted fitusiran and subsequently withdrew from the study. 
2. Subject 
 experienced aspartate aminotransferase increased (PT) which 
led to drug interruption. See Section 7.6.3 for further details. 
Subject narratives of TEAEs leading to permanent drug discontinuation: 
1. Subject 
 was a 20-year-old male with HA who permanently 
discontinued fitusiran after a TEAE of liver injury on Day 56 of LTE15174, after his first 
dose of fitusiran (50 mg Q2M). On Day 56, LFTs showed AST at 164 IU/L (normal range: 
15 to 40 IU/L, baseline value: 24 IU/L), ALT at 624 IU/L (normal range: 9 to 50 IU/L, 
baseline value: 35 IU/L [normal range: 10 to 40 IU/L]). Alkaline phosphatase (ALP), 
bilirubin, and gamma glutamyl transferase were within the normal range. On Day 135, the 
study subject recovered from the event of liver injury with AST at 28 IU/L and ALT at 44 
IU/L. This event is considered related to fitusiran. 
2. Subject 
 permanently discontinued fitusiran after a TEAE of cerebral 
infarction. The subject was a 26-year-old male with HB with inhibitors and a history of type 
2 diabetes, dyslipidemia, hepatic steatosis, obstructive sleep apnea, and nephrotic syndrome. 
The subject was evaluated on Day 219 of LTE15174 for vertigo and an MRI revealed 
evidence of a cerebral infarction in the right temporal lobe and cerebellum, though acuity 
could not be determined. The subject received the last dose of fitusiran (50 mg Q2M) on 
Day 226. The review team considers that while the subject had cardiovascular risk factors for 
stroke, given the mechanism of action of fitusiran to increase thrombotic risk, its role in the 
causation of the event cannot be ruled out.

3. Subject 
 discontinued fitusiran after a TEAE of hepatocellular 
carcinoma. The subject was a 61-year-old male with HA and a history of hepatitis C. On 
Day 2340 of study LTE14762, the subject experienced a TEAE of hepatocellular carcinoma 
after investigation of elevated alkaline phosphatase and bilirubin. The last dose of fitusiran 
was Day 2309. The review team considers that fitusiran is unrelated to the event of 
hepatocellular carcinoma, as there is no mechanistic reason for fitusiran to cause 
malignancies and hepatitis C is a strong risk factor for hepatocellular carcinoma. 
4. Subject 
 permanently discontinued fitusiran after a TEAE of pruritis. The 
subject was a 47-year-old with HA and a history of pruritus, who on Day 627 of LTE15174, 
while receiving the AT-DR (dose 50 mg Q2M), developed worsening pruritus. Fitusiran was 
discontinued on Day 624. At the time of the cutoff date, the study subject had not recovered 
from the event of pruritus. As the pruritus worsened with fitusiran, it is possible fitusiran 
contributed to this adverse event. 
5. Subject 
 was a 40-year-old male with HA who permanently discontinued 
fitusiran due to an AE of deep vein thrombosis postoperative. On Day 1140 of LTE15174, 
the subject underwent a left total knee replacement. On Day 1148, he had a peripherally 
inserted central venous catheter. On Day 1155, he developed pain and swelling of the left leg 
and venous doppler ultrasound showed extensive thrombosis of the left common femoral, 
popliteal, and calf veins. The subject received anticoagulation with apixaban, and fitusiran 
was discontinued, with the last dose (50 mg QM) administered on Day 1148. The event of 
thrombosis is considered related to fitusiran based on the mechanism of action of the drug. 
Recent orthopedic surgery and the central line likely further increased the risk of thrombosis. 
7.5.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Pooled Trials 
LTE15174, LTE14762, and EFC15110, AT-DR Period  
Table 43 lists the adverse events by PT and Table 44 lists adverse events by FMQ from the 
Pooled Trials LTE15174, LTE14762, and EFC15110, AT-DR Period. A total of 83.2% of 
subjects experienced a TEAE. The most common TEAEs were viral infection (29%), 
nasopharyngitis (26%), and bacterial infection (11%). While it is not expected based on the 
mechanism of action of fitusiran for there to be an increased risk of infections, on further 
analyses of the randomized controlled studies (Studies EFC14768 and EFC14769), the rates of 
viral infection, nasopharyngitis, and bacterial infections were significantly higher in the fitusiran 
arm compared to the on-demand arm (see Section 17.1 of the review). Therefore, the clinical 
team considered these TEAEs as likely related to fitusiran. The next most common TEAEs were 
hemorrhage (10%), hepatic injury (9%), arthralgia (8%), pyrexia (7%), diarrhea (6%), pain in 
extremity (6%), prothrombin fragment 1.2 increased (6%), injection site reaction (5%), cough 
(5%), and headache (5%). Elevated prothrombin 1.2 levels can indicate an increased risk for 
thrombosis (Ota et al. 2008). Adverse events of pyrexia, diarrhea and pain in extremity did not 
occur at higher rates in the fitusiran arms compared to on-demand arms in randomized controlled 
trials, therefore, these events were not considered adverse reactions. Urticaria was reported in 
two subjects (0.7%). It is unclear if the events of urticaria were related to fitusiran due to limited 
details regarding the events. However, both subjects continued fitusiran without interruption, and 
similar rates of urticaria were seen in control and fitusiran arms in randomized controlled trials 
(see Section 17.1 for further details). The review team considered hemorrhage unrelated to 
Reference ID: 5560526

fitusiran as it is related to the underlying disease. Of note, there was a single event of mild 
angioedema (lip swelling). This was not considered related to fitusiran as it occurred on day 971 
of the study and the fitusiran dose was not interrupted.

Treatment-emergent adverse events defined as any AE with onset or ongoing after fitusiran dose restart and before the data cut off 
for NDA. However, AEs were not considered treatment-emergent if they occurred during the follow-up after a subject’s AT value was 
60% or greater.  
Duration is up to 128 weeks. 
Each FMQ is aligned to a single SOC based on clinical judgment. However, please be aware that some FMQs may contain PTs 
from more than one SOC.  
Some preferred terms are not included in any FDA medical query. Those preferred terms are not shown or counted in this table.  
Abbreviations: AE, adverse event; AT, antithrombin; AT-DR, antithrombin-based dose regimen; FMQ, FDA medical query; incl, 
including; LTE, long-term extension; N, number of subjects in treatment arm; n, number of subjects with adverse event; PT, 
preferred term; SOC, system organ class; TIA, transient ischemic attack 
7.5.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Pooled Trials LTE15174, 
LTE14762, and EFC15110, AT-DR Period  
Routine monitoring of blood and urine, including complete blood count, clinical chemistries, and 
hepatic and renal evaluations, were obtained regularly at prespecified points throughout the 
studies. This section presents a frequency-based analysis of laboratory assessments. 
Chemistries 
Table 45 presents a frequency-based analysis of laboratory assessments using objective 
laboratory data. There were no significant changes in laboratory assessments for sodium, 
potassium, chloride, bicarbonate, calcium, magnesium, phosphate, or albumin. 
Low glucose levels occurred in 16.1% of study subjects. Most low glucose levels were level 1 
and not associated with a continuous trend. High random glucose levels occurred in 6% of study 
subjects. Sustained elevated glucoses were in study subjects who had a concurrent diagnosis of 
diabetes mellitus. Of note, glucose levels were not fasting. There was no pattern of either low or 
high glucose based on hemophilia type or inhibitor status. 
Increases in creatinine ≥2× baseline occurred in 4.2% of subjects; however, these were not 
sustained increases. Reductions in eGFR of ≥50% from baseline occurred in 3 individuals. These 
cases were reviewed, and the reductions were not sustained and recovered to baseline. 
Overall, no clinically relevant chemistry laboratory findings were identified. Most abnormal 
laboratory values were not sustained and were associated with underlying comorbidities. In 
addition, while there appear to be outliers for hypokalemia and hypophosphatemia in this 
analysis, no imbalance was seen for fitusiran vs. on demand treatment in the randomized, 
controlled parent studies. 
Reference ID: 5560526

Note that glucose values for hyperglycemia do not follow a nested format like the other labs. Level 1 corresponds to the diagnosis of prediabetes and is not inclusive of Level 2 and 3. 
Level 2 corresponds to the diagnosis of diabetes. Level 3 represents significant hyperglycemia that may indicate need for insulin or increased risk for diabetic ketoacidosis or other 
complications. 
Threshold levels 1, 2, and 3 as defined by the Standard Safety Tables & Figures Integrated Guide. 
Duration is up to 128 weeks. 
In addition to central laboratory data, local laboratory data may be included in the analysis, if applicable. 
Abbreviations: AT-DR, antithrombin-based dose regimen; CPK, creatine phosphokinase; LTE, long-term extension; N, number of subjects in treatment arm; n, number of subjects 
meeting criteria; NA, not applicable; Nw, number of subjects with data 
Hematology Laboratory Findings 
Frequency of abnormal laboratory values for hematology assessments are shown in Table 46. No significant differences in abnormal 
laboratory values occurred across the hemophilia subtypes or inhibitor status. Changes in hemoglobin likely were related to the 
subjects’ bleeding profile. 
Overall, no clinically relevant laboratory findings were identified. The majority of abnormal laboratory values were not sustained and 
were associated with underlying comorbidities. In addition, while there appears to be outliers of low white blood cell counts, low 
neutrophil counts and low lymphocyte counts in this analysis, no imbalance was seen for fitusiran vs. on demand treatment in the 
randomized, controlled parent studies.

7.5.2.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Pooled 
Trials LTE15174, LTE14762, and EFC15110, AT-DR 
Period  
eDISH plots for hepatocellular injury are presented in Figure 17 and Figure 18. Two subjects met 
potential Hy’s Law criteria and are presented below. Upon further investigation, both cases were 
determined to be unlikely DILI due to fitusiran. In the pooled Trials LTE15174, LTE14762, and 
EFC15110, AT-DR Period, there was no case of confirmed DILI. 
 Subject 
 was a 50-year-old male with HA with inhibitors and a history of 
diabetes mellitus, hypertension, and hepatitis C, who was enrolled in Study EFC014768 at 
80 mg QM, and then rolled over to Study LTE15174. On Day 777 of Study LTE15174 
(Day 1030 since the initiation of fitusiran in Study EFC14768), he presented with symptoms 
of pancreatitis. Imaging showed obstruction due to a common bile duct stone. The subject 
was hospitalized on Day 778, was diagnosed with obstructive cholangitis, and on Day 782 
underwent pancreatobiliary sphincterotomy. Liver tests around this time were significant for 
an ALT >10× ULN and bilirubin >2× ULN. On Day 786, the study subject recovered from 
the events of cholangitis and obstructive pancreatitis, and liver tests normalized. Fitusiran 
had been previously held on Day 677 due to AST elevation and then was resumed Day 784 at 
20 mg Q2M and there was no recurrence of elevated liver enzymes. This case is unlikely 
DILI due to intervening obstructive biliary disease as the cause of ALT and bilirubin 
increase, and resolved with endoscopic retrograde cholangiopancreatography with no 
recurrence with drug rechallenge. 
Reference ID: 5560526

 Subject 
 was a 44-year-old male with HA without inhibitors who on 
Day 589 of Study LTE15174 developed AST >6× ULN, ALT 2.9× ULN, and bilirubin of 
2× ULN. There were exposures to alcohol, a cold medication, and protein powder, which 
were likely the etiology of liver abnormalities. Liver tests normalized 1 week later, and the 
subject remained on fitusiran treatment. 
In the original fitusiran dosing of 80 mg SQ QM, ALT elevations >3× ULN and potential cases 
of Hy’s Law were observed more frequently than in the AT-DR dosing period. The DILI consult 
team performed an in-depth analysis of both DILI and gallbladder disease across the fitusiran 
development program, including the original dosing regimens used in studies EFC14768, 
EFC14769, EFC15110, and LTE14762; refer to the finalized DILI consult in DARRTS for the 
full analysis and Section 7.6.3 for further discussion.

7.5.2.8. Vital Signs, Pooled Trials LTE15174, LTE14762, and 
EFC15110, AT-DR Period  
Table 49 shows the frequency of maximum systolic blood pressure ≥140 mm Hg to be 20.4% 
overall in the Safety Population, Pooled Trials LTE15174, LTE14762, and EFC15110, AT-DR 
Period. Figure 19 shows that the population mean systolic blood pressure fluctuates about the 
baseline and does not consistently rise in either the inhibitor or noninhibitor population. 
Moreover, the fluctuations are between 1 to 3 mm Hg above and below the baseline, and not 
clinically significant. The prevalence of maximum systolic blood pressure changes reflects the 
baseline cohort hypertension prevalence, which is 11.9%. Similar percentages of systolic blood 
pressure ≥ 140 mmHg were reported in both the fitusiran and on demand treatment arms of 
Studies EFC14768 and EFC14769.

7.5.2.9. Subgroups, Pooled Trials LTE15174, LTE14762, and

An overview of TEAEs by demographic subgroup is summarized in Table 50. The incidence of 
overall TEAEs did not appear to meaningfully differ between the subgroups of Age group 12 to

all other subgroups limit further assessment.

7.6. Key Safety Review Issues 
7.6.1. Thrombotic Events 
Issue 
Fitusiran was originally developed as an 80 mg QM fixed dose, however, due to serious events 
of thrombosis, including a thrombosis-related fatality, the dosing regimen was modified to the 
AT-DR. The risk of thrombotic events was assessed in both subjects who received the original 
80 mg QM dosing regimen and the AT-DR. 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 122 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
101 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Background 
Fitusiran is an siRNA that causes degradation of AT mRNA through RNA interference, which 
results in a reduction of plasma AT protein levels, reduced AT activity, and increased thrombin 
generation. The mechanism of action of fitusiran to increase thrombin generation inherently 
increases the ability to make clots, which can increase thrombosis risk though excessive 
pharmacologic effect. In the hemophilia context, increasing clot formation is beneficial due to 
underlying pathological bleeding risk, but there is a careful balance between sufficient 
antithrombin suppression to prevent bleeding and avoidance of the risk of thrombosis, which is 
associated with significant morbidity and mortality. Thromboembolic events are a common 
complication in congenital antithrombin deficiency, in which AT activity ranges from 40 to 60% 
(Patnaik and Moll 2008). Thrombosis formation was observed in nonclinical studies with 
fitusiran (see Section 7.1). 
Mitigation of potential thrombotic risks in the fitusiran development program included exclusion 
of subjects with severe hemophilia with significant thrombosis history or known thrombophilia 
disorder, and implementation of bleed management guidelines (BMG), which include 
recommendations to guide investigators on the use of factor replacement products or bypassing 
products during acute bleeding events and during surgical procedures. Bleed management 
guidelines are described in detail in Section 15. At the close of the controlled studies EFC14768, 
EFC14769, and EFC15110, an analysis of thrombotic events and correlation with AT activity led 
to institution of the AT-DR to target antithrombin levels between 15 to 35%. Per the Applicant, 
persistent AT levels <10% increases the risk of thromboembolic events. 
Assessment 
In total, 11 out of 335 subjects (3.3%) experienced a thrombotic event in the fitusiran clinical 
development program. Of the subjects who experienced thrombotic events, 7 out 270 subjects 
(2.6%) had a thrombotic event while receiving the original 80-mg dose regimen and 4 out of 286 
(1.4%) subjects had a thrombotic event on the AT-DR. No subjects in the on-demand (control) 
arms in the randomized controlled trials (studies EFC14768 and EFC14769) experienced a 
thrombotic event. A summary of the subjects with thrombotic events while receiving fitusiran is 
described in Table 51 below, and full subject narratives from all 11 subjects are in Section 17.3. 
It is notable that both arterial and venous thrombotic events were reported, including atypical 
events such as spinal vein thrombosis and thrombosis of the papilla of the eye. Events typically 
occurred after subjects had been taking fitusiran for over >5 months (range 57 to 1147 days on 
fitusiran). Almost all subjects had risk factors at baseline (e.g., hypertension, diabetes, smoking 
history, recent surgery, central line, history of thrombosis), however, the strongest risk factors 
appear to be recent administration of factor or bypassing factor agent, and persistent AT levels 
<15%. The risk of thrombosis remains for several months after discontinuing fitusiran, as it may 
take 6 months for AT activity to recover to >60%.
