Conclusion 
Patients exposed to fitusiran are at increased risk of thrombosis due to excessive pharmacologic 
effect. While implementing bleed management guidelines and reducing fitusiran exposure such 
that the AT activity range is targeted to between 15 to 35% appears to have decreased the rate of 
thrombotic events, the risk clearly remains, in particular in the setting of additional risk factors 
for thrombosis, including high doses of factor administration and persistent AT levels <15%. The 
risk of thromboembolism should be included as a boxed warning, as knowledge of this risk is 
necessary prior to a prescriber administrating the drug. Patients with a history of thrombosis or 
significant risk factors for thrombosis will need to the consider the benefit/risk profile of the 
drug, and it is imperative for prescribers to follow bleed management guidelines. With labeling 
which includes bleed management guidelines, this risk can be mitigated. 
7.6.2. Acute and Recurrent Gallbladder Disease 
Issue 
Fitusiran has a significant risk of acute and recurrent gallbladder disease. Subjects in clinical 
trials required surgical intervention and had complications such as pancreatitis. The risk of acute 
and recurrent gallbladder disease was assessed in subjects who received the original dosing 
regimen and the AT-DR. 
This review issue was largely assessed by the DILI team from the Division of Hepatology and 
Nutrition; please see the finalized consult review in DARRTS dated March 21, 2025.  
Background 
Patients with hemophilia are not typically at increased risk for gallbladder disease. In addition, 
gallbladder disease was not identified as an adverse reaction during the clinical development 
programs of other approved siRNA products. Therefore, it was an unexpected finding when a 
significant number of subjects developed gallbladder disease and related complications in this 
development program. 
Biliary tract abnormalities of biliary hyperplasia and bile duct dilation were noted in mice treated 
with fitusiran (see Section 13.2.5). However, biliary tract abnormalities were not observed across 
animal studies. 
Assessment 
In the fitusiran development program, 51 (15.2%) subjects experienced a gallbladder TEAE after 
receiving at least one dose of fitusiran. Of the 51 subjects who experienced a gallbladder event, 
the most common event was cholelithiasis (47.1%), followed by cholecystitis (33.3%), chronic 
cholecystitis (11.8%), gallbladder polyp (11.8%), acute cholecystitis (7.8%), biliary colic (5.9%), 
gallbladder enlargement (5.9%), cholestasis (3.9%), cholangitis (2%), and biliary dyskinesia 
(2%). The majority of events were moderate in severity. Gallbladder TEAEs were graded mild in 
12 subjects (23.5%), moderate in 36 subjects (70.6%) and severe in 9 subjects (17.6%). 
Frequency of gallbladder TEAEs are described below by dosing cohort. 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 127 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
106 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Frequency of Gallbladder Events in Pooled Safety Databases 
Original 80 mg QM Dose Cohort 
46/270 (17.3%) of subjects experienced gallbladder events. 
 Study 14768: 6/41 (14.6%) subjects in the fitusiran treatment arm and 0/19 in the on-demand 
control arm. 
 Study 14769: 7/79 (8.8%) subjects in the fitusiran treatment arm and 0/40 in the on-demand 
control arm. 
 Study 15110: 10/67 (14.9%) subjects during the fitusiran treatment period and 0/78 subjects 
during the control period of 15110. 
AT-DR Cohort 
11/286 (3.8%) of subjects experienced 15 acute gallbladder events, including cholelithiasis (4), 
cholecystitis (3), gallbladder polyp (3), cholecystitis acute (2), cholangitis (1), cholecystitis 
chronic (1), and gallbladder enlargement (1). 
Cholecystectomy 
Across the 335-subject safety database from the entire fitusiran exposed population, 12 
individuals (all on fitusiran) underwent a cholecystectomy (3.6%). Nine of these individuals 
experienced an SAE of cholecystitis/cholelithiasis before the cholecystectomy, and one 
experienced an SAE of cholangitis and pancreatitis caused by gallstone disease more than a year 
after cholecystectomy. Most subjects (11 of 12) did not interrupt fitusiran prior to the procedure; 
however, all subjects received factor concentrate or a bypassing agent perioperatively. 
Of the 12 subjects on fitusiran who underwent cholecystectomy, 11 subjects were receiving a 
fixed dose of fitusiran, and one was on AT-DR (20 mg Q2M). Eleven of these 12 subjects 
continued fitusiran after cholecystectomy. One subject experienced cholangitis and pancreatitis 
caused by gallstone disease more than two years after cholecystectomy and while on the AT-DR 
dose regimen. The average time to receiving a cholecystectomy was 417 days (range 57 to 
1035). Table 164 (Section 17.4) shows the demographics and associated AEs in the 
cholecystectomy cohort. 
Conclusion 
Gallbladder events only occurred in severe hemophilia patients exposed to fitusiran in clinical 
studies; none were reported in the control subjects. Gallbladder events were less common on the 
AT-DR dosing (3.8%) compared to the original 80 mg fixed dose regimen (17.3%), suggesting a 
fitusiran dose effect. However, this observation may be subject to selection. Most subjects on 
AT-DR dosing had first been dosed with the original 80 mg regimen. Their ability to stay in the 
study and change to the AT-DR dosing was therefore contingent on their ability to tolerate the 
original dosing, leading to potential selection bias in safety evaluation of the AT-DR dosing.  
The proportion of subjects who required cholecystectomy after they developed gallbladder 
disease is concerning for this patient population. Patients with hemophilia are prone to bleeding, 
which can increase the risk of surgical procedures. 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 128 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
107 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Acute and recurrent gallbladder disease should be included in a boxed warning in the label to 
alert prescribers of this risk prior to drug administration. Prescribers should consider this risk for 
patients at high risk for gallbladder disease, or high risk for surgical procedures. With labeling, 
acute and recurrent gallbladder disease risk can be mitigated. Enhanced pharmacovigilance for 
gallbladder disease will be recommended. 
7.6.3. Hepatotoxicity 
Issue 
Fitusiran has a significant risk of hepatotoxicity identified with the original 80-mg dose, as 
evidenced by a high incidence of ALT elevations greater than 3× ULN and a Hy’s law case. The 
risk of hepatotoxicity was assessed in subjects who received the original dosing regimen and the 
AT-DR. This review issue was largely assessed by the DILI team from the Division of 
Hepatology and Nutrition; please see the finalized consult review in DARRTS dated March 21, 
2025. 
Background 
Fitusiran targets the liver by active uptake via the ASPGR expressed on hepatocytes. Fitusiran 
inhibits hepatocyte AT production through RNA interference on the SERPINC1 gene, leading to 
its degradation by RNA-induced silencing complex and preventing translation of gene 
expression and function. The Division of Applied Regulatory Science (DARS) found no 
evidence that the knockdown of AT production would cause hepatotoxicity. Animal studies 
indicate minimal to moderate liver toxicity potential with findings of liver cell necrosis and bile 
duct dilatation at the highest dose exposures; however, there were differences in species. Only 
about 10% of the administered dose is accounted for in excretion studies, and the rest of the 
product may be retained in the liver, but how much is in smaller nucleotide forms (metabolites) 
that may be recycled for use by the body is unknown. See the DILI consult review for further 
details. 
FDA has approved several siRNAs that inhibit hepatic mRNA translation by targeted uptake by 
the ASPGR (see Section 7.2 for further discussion). Of the hepatic targeted siRNAs, givosiran 
has hepatotoxicity listed as a Warning in the label (Alnylam Pharmaceuticals 2024b). 
Assessment 
Overall, 66 out of 335 subjects (19.7%) who received fitusiran met DILI reporting requirements.  
Original Dose Regimen 
In the parent studies (fitusiran 80 mg QM dose regimen), ALT and AST elevations above 3 times 
the upper limit of normal (ULN) occurred in 13 subjects (31.7%) with hemophilia with inhibitors 
(Study EFC14769) and 14 subjects (17.7%) with hemophilia without inhibitors (Study 
EFC14768), compared to no events of AST/ALT elevation greater than 3× ULN in the on-
demand control groups from either study. See Table 52 and Table 53 below for liver enzyme 
abnormalities by study. Subjects treated with fitusiran 80 mg in the open-label study EFC15110 
had ALT elevations greater than 3× ULN at similar rates seen in fitusiran subjects treated in 
Study EFC14768 and EFC14769. 
Reference ID: 5560526

 was an 18-year-old with HB, receiving 80 mg QM, who had 
normal liver enzymes at baseline and developed probable DILI. On Day 110 the subject 
experienced an adverse event of ALT increased that was 3× ULN. Evaluation for alternative

observed. The subject remained on fitusiran in the long-term extension study.

regimen.

period compared to 59 individuals from the control, on-demand arms of studies EFC14678 and

3.4% (9/266 fitusiran AT-DR treated subjects) and higher than the 0% frequency in the 59 on-
demand control subjects (note that there were approximately 4.5× more AT-DR treated subjects 
than on-demand subjects). See Table 54 below for liver abnormalities seen in the AT-DR period, 
compared to on-demand arms in the original parent studies. The onset for initial ALT elevations 
exceeding 3× ULN had a median onset of 42.5 days after initial dosing (range 14 to 365 days).

Conclusion 
The below conclusion is adapted from the DILI consult review. 
Fitusiran use was associated with an increased incidence of liver test elevations in treated 
subjects compared to controls. The risk of hepatotoxicity is greater in the 80-mg original dosing 
compared to the AT-DR dosing. There was one Hy’s law case (Study subject 
 that was highly likely for DILI on the original 80-mg dose. This subject recovered with 
timely drug discontinuation, but the case indicates that the rise in liver enzymes associated with 
fitusiran can lead to liver injury with impairment of hepatic function, rather than being benign 
elevation in aminotransaminases. 
On the AT-DR, the incidence of ALT elevations was lower. The dose subjects received was 
lower (50 mg or 20 mg QM or Q2M) compared to the 80 mg QM in the original studies, 
indicating the liver injury may be partly dose-dependent. While the incidence of ALT elevation 
was lower in the AT-DR, the risk of hepatotoxicity was not eliminated. Furthermore, there was 
no control group for the AT-DR dosing, and the majority of subjects on the AT-DR dose were 
those who had tolerated the 80-mg dose, such that interpretation of the experience on the AT-DR 
is confounded by selection bias. Those on the original dosing regimen who tolerated the drug and 
were able to stay on it would have likely been less likely to have an issue on an even lower dose 
seen in AT-DR. 
Hepatotoxicity should be included as a Warning in the label to closely monitor patients for 
hepatic injury by monitoring liver blood tests monthly for 6 months and as clinically indicated 
thereafter, and to direct discontinuation with suspected DILI injury. While prompt 
discontinuation is not possible, given dosing is QM or Q2M, early recognition with monitoring 
liver enzymes should decrease the risk of serious liver injury. With labeling, including 
prescribing the AT-DR, hepatotoxicity risk can be mitigated. Enhanced pharmacovigilance for 
hepatotoxicity will be recommended. 
8. Therapeutic Individualization 
8.1. Intrinsic Factors 
The Applicant performed PopPK analysis using pooled data from all 6 clinical studies to 
evaluate the effects of intrinsic factors on the plasma PK and PD of fitusiran. The evaluated 
intrinsic factors included age, body weight, hemophilia type (hemophilia A or B), inhibitor status 
(inhibitor or noninhibitor), and race. A summary of the PopPK analysis can be found in the 
Pharmacometrics Assessment in Section 14 below. 
PopPK analysis showed that hemophilia type and inhibitor status do not impact fitusiran plasma 
PK. Body weight, race (Asian versus non-Asian), eGFR, albumin, and bilirubin were identified 
as statistically significant covariates in subjects with hemophilia, but the magnitude of the effect 
(<25% on fitusiran plasma PK) is not considered to be clinically relevant (fitusiran effect is 
driven by fitusiran concentrations in liver, rather than plasma). 
Reference ID: 5560526
(b) (6)

===== PAGE 135 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
114 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Hepatic Impairment 
There was no clinical experience of fitusiran in subjects with hepatic impairment, as the clinical 
development program excluded subjects with clinically significant liver disease, or with ALT 
and/or AST >1.5× upper limit of normal reference range (ULN) or total bilirubin > ULN. The 
Applicant did not conduct a dedicated clinical study to evaluate the impact of hepatic impairment 
on fitusiran PK/PD. The review team recommends “avoid use” of fitusiran in patients with all 
degrees of hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class A, B, and C). This recommendation is due to 
the known risk of hepatotoxicity with fitusiran, and patients with cirrhosis are at a greater risk of 
decompensation if they have a gallbladder event and require cholecystectomy. 
8.2. Extrinsic Factors 
The Applicant conducted a number of in vitro studies with human matrices to assess the potential 
for drug interactions mediated by drug metabolizing enzymes and transporters. These studies 
showed that fitusiran is unlikely to interact with drug-metabolizing enzymes and transporters 
and, therefore, the potential for drug interaction at clinically relevant concentrations is very low 
(see Section 14.1 for details). Thus, no clinical drug interactions studies were conducted by the 
Applicant. 
8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development 
Fitusiran will be approved for pediatric patients 12 years and older, as this age group was 
included in the clinical trials used to demonstrate efficacy and safety of fitusiran. The Applicant 
is currently conducting Study EFC15467, an open-label dose finding study in subjects with 
hemophilia A and B aged 1 to <12 years. The results of this study may be used to inform 
pediatric labeling for younger patients in the future. 
8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males 
of Reproductive Potential 
The following nonclinical information was used in support of the indicated labeling sections. 
Additional nonclinical details are in Section 13.1. 
Animal Data 
Animal reproductive and developmental toxicology studies have not been conducted with 
fitusiran, as the intended population is males with hemophilia, and the clinical development 
program was conducted exclusively in males. Hemophilia A and B are X-linked recessive 
inherited bleeding disorders primarily affecting males (99%), and the likelihood of women of 
childbearing potential receiving fitusiran is very low. There were no adverse effects of fitusiran 
on male fertility or reproductive performance parameters assessed in Sprague-Dawley rats in the 
26-week repeated dose chronic toxicity study. It is not known whether fitusiran can adversely 
impact the development of an embryo/fetus when administered to a pregnant woman.

Table 56. Nonclinical Data for Product Labeling 
Labeling Section 
Nonclinical Data 
8.1 Pregnancy 
There are no available data on QFITLIA use in pregnant women to 
evaluate for a drug-associated risk of major birth defects, and miscarriage 
or other adverse maternal or fetal outcomes. Reproduction studies in 
pregnant animals have not been conducted with fitusiran based on the 
target population being limited to male subjects. It is not known whether 
QFITLIA can cause fetal harm when administered to a pregnant woman or 
can affect reproduction capacity. QFITLIA should be used during 
pregnancy only if the potential benefit for the mother outweighs the 
potential risks, including those to the fetus. 
The estimated background risk of major birth defects and miscarriage for 
the indicated population is unknown. All pregnancies have a background 
risk of major birth defect, loss, or other adverse outcomes; In the U.S. 
general population, the estimated background risk of major birth defects 
and miscarriage in clinically recognized pregnancies is 2%-4% and 15%-
20%, respectively. 
8.2 Lactation 
There are no data on the presence of fitusiran or its metabolite in human 
milk, the effects on the breastfed child, or the effects on milk production. It 
is not known whether or not QFITLIA is safe for use during breastfeeding. 
The developmental and health benefits of breastfeeding should be 
considered along with the mother's clinical need for QFITLIA and any 
potential adverse effects on the breastfed infant from QFITLIA or from the 
underlying maternal condition.  
8.3 Females and Males 
of Reproductive 
Potential 
Contraception 
Females 
Use of QFITLIA in women using hormonal contraceptives may increase 
the risk of thrombotic events. Estrogen based hormonal contraceptives 
are an established risk factor for thrombosis in women with inherited AT 
deficiency. Advise patients using hormonal contraceptives to use an 
alternative nonhormonal contraception prior to starting treatment with 
and while receiving QFITLIA.

116 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Labeling Section 
Nonclinical Data 
13.1 Carcinogenesis, 
Mutagenesis, 
Impairment of Fertility 
 
 
 In a 2-year carcinogenicity study in Sprague Dawley 
rats, fitusiran was not carcinogenic up to the highest dose tested following 
subcutaneous administration at doses of 0.03, 0.1 or 0.3 mg/kg/week in 
males and 0.1, 0.3, or 1 mg/kg/week in females. All doses in males and 
females were below the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of 
50 mg per month based on body surface area (BSA) comparisons. 
Fitusiran was not carcinogenic in Tg-rasH2 mice following subcutaneous 
administration for 26 weeks at doses of 0.05, 0.15, or 0.5 mg/kg/week in 
males and 0.025, 0.08, or 0.25 mg/kg/week in females.  
 
Fitusiran was not mutagenic or clastogenic in a battery of genetic toxicity 
studies (i.e., in vitro bacterial reverse mutation assay, in vitro chromosomal 
aberration assay in human peripheral blood lymphocytes; in vivo bone 
marrow micronucleus test in rats).  
 
 
In a chronic repeat dose toxicology study in Sprague-Dawley rats, fitusiran-
treated males were cohabitated with fitusiran-naïve females following 
subcutaneous administration at doses of 0.25, 0.5, or 1 mg/kg/week for 16 
weeks. No adverse effects on male fertility endpoints were observed. All 
doses used in males were below the MRHD based on BSA comparisons. 
 
Source: Fitusiran Prescribing Information  
Abbreviations: AT, antithrombin; AUC, area under the plasma concentration-time curve; BSA, body surface area; MRHD, maximum 
recommended human dose 
9. Product Quality 
Approval 
The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality review team has assessed the application with respect to 
chemistry, manufacturing, and controls and has determined that it meets all applicable standards 
to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. As such, the Office of 
Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective; see 
finalized Integrated Quality Reviews in DARRTS dated January 17th, 2025 and March 26, 2025. 
A shelf life of 36 months is granted for the 20 mg drug product in a 0.2 mL single-dose vial (100 
mg/mL) when stored either at 2°C to 8°C (36°F to 46°F) or at 15°C to 30°C (59°F to 86°F). A 
shelf life of 24 months is granted for the 50 mg drug product in a 0.5 mL single-dose prefilled 
pen (100 mg/mL) when stored at 2° C to 8° C. The 50 mg drug product may be stored at 15°C to 
30°C (59°F to 86°F) for a single period of up to 3 months at the end of storage within the shelf 
life of 24 months.

Considerations 
Fitusiran is provided as a clear, colorless to pale yellow solution for injection either as a 
50 mg/0.5 mL prefilled pen for single use for cutaneous administration or as a 20 mg/0.2 mL

Companion Diagnostic on the date of drug approval.

and Other Good Clinical Practice 
Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review 
Clinical trials were conducted in compliance with Good Clinical Practice, as required by the 
International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) E6 Guideline for Good Clinical Practice 
(November 2016). The studies also meet the requirements of the Declaration of Helsinki, 
standard operating procedures for clinical investigations and documentation by the Applicant. 
A clinical site inspection consult review was completed by Dr. Anthony Orenica in the Office of 
Scientific Investigations and is summarized in Section 22. The results of the clinical site 
inspections and Applicant inspection support the conclusion that the trials were conducted 
adequately, and that the data generated support the proposed indication. Review of the financial 
disclosures did not raise any concerns about the validity or reliability of the data. A Financial 
Disclosure summary review is located in Section 25.

The application did not raise efficacy or safety issues requiring external expert advice. Therefore, 
an advisory committee meeting was not convened to discuss this application.

III. Additional Analyses and Information 
12. Summary of Regulatory History 
Fitusiran was granted orphan drug designation (ODD) on August 16, 2013, for the treatment of 
hemophilia A (ODD #13-4028) and August 12, 2013, for the treatment of hemophilia B (ODD 
#13-4029). 
Fitusiran was granted fast track designation (FTD) on December 23, 2020, for routine 
prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in patients with hemophilia 
A or B with or without factor VIII or IX inhibitors. 
Fitusiran was granted breakthrough therapy designation (BTD) on December 6, 2023, for routine 
prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in patients with hemophilia 
B with factor IX inhibitors. 
On June 26, 2015, the Sponsor submitted a new IND under Section 505(i) of the Federal Food, 
Drug, and Cosmetic Act (IND 125632). Prior to the IND submission, one type B meeting was 
held on April 23, 2015, to discuss the Sponsor’s overall chemistry, manufacturing, and controls, 
nonclinical data, and preliminary results from the ongoing phase 1 clinical study. 
On July 24, 2015, the Applicant was advised that the 30-day safety review of the application had 
concluded and that the study could proceed with their proposed clinical investigation for the 
treatment of patients with moderate to severe hemophilia A. 
On May 10, 2016, an End-of-Phase 2 meeting was held to discuss the Sponsor’s nonclinical 
development plan and proposals for the planned phase 3 trials. 
On September 28, 2016, a carcinogenicity special protocol assessment request was submitted, 
and on November 2, 2016, an agreement communication was issued. On November 22, 2016, 
another carcinogenicity special protocol assessment request was submitted, and on December 21, 
2016, an agreement communication was issued. 
On May 9, 2017, a prephase 3 meeting was held to discuss the proposed development of the 
phase 3 program in support of a marketing application for fitusiran. 
On September 8, 2017, the Sponsor was notified that all studies included in IND 125632 were 
being placed on full clinical hold based on the occurrence of a fatal case of cerebral venous sinus 
thrombosis reported for one of the subjects (27-year-old male with hemophilia A) from Bulgaria 
in Study ALN-AT3SC-002. On November 8, 2017, a Type A meeting was held to gain 
concurrence on the Sponsor’s proposal for additional measures to mitigate the risk of thrombosis 
and to remove the clinical hold on studies with fitusiran following implementation of these 
measures and IRB approval of the amended clinical study protocols. The Sponsor proposed to 
amend the study to limit the subject population to hemophilia A and B subjects with inhibitors 
only. On December 14, 2017, the full clinical hold was removed after the Bleed Management 
Guide (BMG) was revised. 
On January 26, 2018, preliminary comments were sent for a type C meeting request to discuss 
the combination product development of fitusiran, including development of the proposed 
 
autoinjector and clinical bridging plan, as well as the proposed prefilled syringe with safety 
Reference ID: 5560526
(b) (4)

===== PAGE 140 =====

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
device (PFS-S) human factors validation study approach and protocol. On January 30, 2018, the 
meeting scheduled for January 31, 2018, was canceled because the preliminary responses were 
clear, and no further discussion was needed. 
On October 30, 2020, the Sponsor voluntarily paused dosing to implement the antithrombin-
based dose regimen (AT-DR) dose regimen due to analysis of thrombotic events.  
On December 10, 2020, the Sponsor resumed Studies EFC14768, EFC14769, EFC15110, 
LTE14762, and LTE15467, and introduced the AT-DR dose regimen. 
On November 29, 2021, the proprietary name QFITLIA was conditionally approved for fitusiran 
after a June 19, 2021, denial of the proposed name 
. 
On June 7, 2022, Written Responses were issued to discuss the strategy intended to support 
registration of the prefilled pen for fitusiran. 
On June 4, 2023, Type C Written Responses was issued to discuss the NDA format in advance of 
a pre-NDA meeting for fitusiran. 
On September 18, 2023, a Type C guidance meeting was held to discuss the Sponsor’s plans to 
submit an NDA for fitusiran. FDA converted the requested pre-NDA meeting to a Type C 
meeting because FDA did not have sufficient information to determine whether the Sponsor’s 
proposed clinical data package was sufficient for filing. FDA provided feedback on the 
Sponsor’s proposed approach to demonstrating efficacy and safety of fitusiran for the proposed 
indication. 
On March 19, 2024, a pre-NDA meeting was held to discuss FDA’s concurrence regarding the 
Sponsor’s plan to submit an NDA in March/April 2024 and to discuss the clinical data package 
to support the filing and review of the planned NDA, the proposed indication in the label, and 
planned NDA regulatory milestones. 
On March 28, 2024, the Applicant submitted an NDA for fitusiran to prevent or reduce the 
frequency of bleeding episodes in adults and adolescents ≥12 years of age with hemophilia A or 
B, with or without factor VIII or IX inhibitors. The recommended dose regimen for fitusiran is a 
starting dose of 50 mg once every 2 months (Q2M), which may be escalated to 50 mg once 
monthly (QM) or de-escalated to 20 mg QM or Q2M based on antithrombin (AT) levels. 
Figure 20 shows a timeline of the fitusiran development program, and description of these

13.2.1. Primary Pharmacology 
Pharmacological activity of fitusiran was examined in hemophilia A and B animal models. 
Fitusiran was effective at enhancing coagulation and bleeding in these models, supporting the 
proposed prophylactic benefits of fitusiran. The summaries of these studies are shown in 
Table 57. 
Table 57. Tabulated In Vitro and In Vivo Pharmacology Studies With Fitusiran 
Study Details 
Findings 
Primary target sequence 
conservation across 
various species (per 
summary materials 
contained in the 
Applicant’s submission) 
Fitusiran is pharmacologically active in mice, rats, cynomolgus monkeys, and 
dogs because the sequences of antithrombin mRNAs (derived from the 
SERPINC1 genes) are highly conserved across species. The dog sequence 
contains a single nucleotide mismatch compared to others. Treatment with 
fitusiran demonstrated exaggerated pharmacodynamic effects of antithrombin 
suppression in all four healthy animal species 
Study No: BIO-12020 
Pharmacodynamic 
evaluation of fitusiran in 
wild-type mice following 
single subcutaneous 
injection 
(4 M/dose group) 
Dose: PBS, 0.3, 1, 3, 10 
and 30 mg/kg 
A single SC dose of fitusiran to wild-type mice led to dose-dependent AT 
suppression (as measured by decreases in mRNA, antigen levels, and 
activity) of approximately 50% at 1 mg/kg on Day 3 postdose, and maximum 
AT protein suppression up to 95% at doses up to 30 mg/kg by Day 10. 
Greater than 80% AT suppression was maintained for approximately 28 days 
postdose at ≥10 mg/kg.

Study Details 
Findings 
Study No.: BIO-13023 
 
Single or Repeat Dose 
Administration of 
Fitusiran in Cynomolgus 
Monkeys 
 
Dose: 0.1, 1, 3, 10, 
30 mg/kg (single) 
3M/dose group 
 
Cynomolgus monkeys received single doses of 0.1, 1, 3, 10, 30 mg/kg or 
3 weekly doses of 30 mg/kg fitusiran (AD-57213). Single SC administration 
showed a dose-dependent, durable lowering of AT protein levels at 1, 3, 10, 
and 30 mg/kg fitusiran. No pharmacologic activity was observed at 0.1 mg/kg 
with ~50% and >70% AT suppression for ~30 days at 1 mg/kg and 10 mg/kg, 
respectively. Single SC administration at 30 mg/kg showed minimal decreased 
mean fibrinogen (FIB) concentration and aPTT on Day 37 (-36% and -21% 
respectively compared to Day -8 pretreatment). These changes correlated 
with increased D-Dimer concentrations peaking between Days 15 and 37 
(peaks between 166% and 830%, individual values compared to 
pretreatment), decreased AT concentrations (≥90%), and increased thrombin 
generation levels. 
Figure 22. AT Suppression After Single Dose of Fitusiran in Monkeys 
 
Source: from Applicant’s submission 
AT protein concentration from individual animals were normalized to their respective individual 
predose serum concentration at each time point. Each graphical data point represents the 
remaining AT for the group mean (N=3 animals, samples assayed in technical triplicates 
± standard deviation). 
Abbreviation: AT, antithrombin 
Repeated once-weekly administration of fitusiran for 3 weeks at 30 mg/kg was 
not tolerated, resulting in mortality in 3/3 animals due to moderate to severe 
thrombosis, which was primarily seen in the pulmonary vasculature, and/or 
moderate or severe right atrial thrombosis in the heart. More than 95% 
suppression of AT levels was maintained during the dosing period, which 
correlated with decreased AT mRNA levels in the liver. Hematological 
changes of marked increased D-Dimer values (peaks up to 3,887% and 
36,259%), decreased red blood cell parameters, platelet counts, and/or FIB 
concentrations, were considered related to the exaggerated pharmacological 
effects of fitusiran. 
 
This study demonstrated durable pharmacological activity of fitusiran in 
normal monkeys at doses up to 30 mg/kg (12 times the MRHD based on BSA 
comparisons) following single dose.

NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
125 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Study Details 
Findings 
Study No: BIO-13025A 
Repeat Dose 
Pharmacology Study in 
Cynomolgus Monkeys by 
Subcutaneous Doses of 
Fitusiran with a 4-week 
Recovery 
3M/dose group 
The study evaluated different repeated dose regimens in monkeys (three 
males/group) of once weekly (QW) or once every other weekly (Q2W) dosing, 
as follows: 
g) QW at 0.5 for 8 doses or 0.125 mg/kg for 13 doses 
h) Q2W at 1 for 4 doses or 0.25 mg/kg for 13 doses 
i) 
QW at 1.5 mg/kg for 6 doses 
j) 
Q2W at 3 mg/kg for 3 doses 
 
Cumulative weekly doses of 0.5 mg/kg or 1 mg/kg resulted in approximately 
90% suppression of AT activity, and a steady-state AT suppression level of 
~50% and ~65% at lower doses of 0.125 mg/kg and 0.25 mg/kg, respectively. 
Animals dosed at a higher cumulative weekly dose of 1.5 mg/kg (1.5 mg/kg 
QW or 3 mg/kg Q2W) had >90% AT suppression. Reduction of AT was 
associated with increased thrombin generation, with a roughly 3-fold increase 
in peak thrombin over baseline levels at approximately 80% AT reduction. The 
data showed a strong correlation between the peak thrombin values and the 
levels of AT. 
 
Unscheduled deaths occurred in two monkeys at 3 mg/kg Q2W after 3 doses, 
which were caused by platelet depletion resulting from systemic clotting, 
which paradoxically led to severe systemic hemorrhage. These findings were 
consistent with the effects of the excessively suppressed AT activity of >90% 
and increase in D-Dimer values compared to predose. Monkeys given 
1.5 mg/kg QW survived; clinical signs included swelling of the left leg and 
groin/inguinal areas, inguinal area redness, left leg lameness, decreased 
activity, small feces, paleness, and decreased food consumption. Changes in 
platelet counts were observed at 1.5 and 3 mg/kg, consistent with increased 
platelet consumption and a compensatory regenerative rebound response. 
 
This study demonstrated durable pharmacological activity with repeat dosing 
up to 1.5 mg/kg (2× the MRHD based on BSA comparisons).  
Study No: BIO-14035  
A 6-Month 
Pharmacodynamic 
Evaluation of Fitusiran 
Following Once Monthly 
Subcutaneous Injection 
of Cynomolgus Monkeys 
 
Doses: 0.5,1 and 
2 mg/kg 
3M/dose group 
Animals administered 0.5 and 1 mg/kg were dosed on Days 1, 29, 57, 85, 
113, and 141. Animals administered 2 mg/kg were dosed on Days 1, 29, and 
57. 
 
Once-monthly doses of 0.5, 1 and 2 mg/kg fitusiran were well-tolerated and 
led to a dose-dependent reduction in AT activity of 30%, 40% and 70%, 
respectively. Repeated SC administration once monthly for 6 months led to 
further AT reduction of 40% at 0.5 mg/kg, 50% at 1 mg/kg and AT reduction of 
80% at 2 mg/kg for 3 months. Reductions of AT increased thrombin 
generation and suggested a similar sustained suppression of AT, e.g., via a 
monthly clinical dosing regimen, may be effective in the intended patient 
population. Administration of fitusiran by once-monthly subcutaneous injection 
was well tolerated in monkeys at the highest dose of 2.0 mg/kg/month (3× the 
MRHD based on BSA comparisons).

13.2.2. Secondary Pharmacology 
Table 61. Assessment for Off-Target Effects of Fitusiran 
Study Details 
Findings 
Study No: BIO-13007 
 
Study Title: In Vitro "Off-Target" 
Analysis of ALN-57213, the 
SERPINC1-Targeting siRNA 
Component of ALN-AT3  
Addition of fitusiran to a human hepatoma cell line (HepG2) in 
vitro at concentrations ranging from 35.7fM to 10nM resulted in a 
dose-dependent inhibition of endogenous antithrombin (AT) 
mRNA levels with an IC50 of 49.6pM. The 19-mer core sequence 
of A-116861, the antisense strand of fitusiran, was evaluated for 
off-target potential by conducting a comprehensive search against 
the human transcriptomes within the human NCBI Refseq 
dataset. There were a total of 9 mRNA sequences predicted as 
potentially possessing off-target potential. These 9 transcripts 
were then assessed experimentally. No IC50 values were 
calculable for 8 of the 9 off-target transcripts at up to 10nM 
fitusiran. An IC50 near 10nM was calculated for the remaining 
transcript, which suggests a 200-fold difference between 
fitusiran’s “on-target” binding and the most sensitive “off-target” 
transcript.  
Analysis of Human for polymorphism 
variations in support of 
Fitusiran/BIO15011 
 
 
A search of the NCBI dbSNP identified a single SNP within the AT 
gene SERPINC1 transcript NM_000488.The estimated frequency 
of rs144084678 SNP in the broader human population is 
<1/0.0002 that appears to fall outside the critical target recognition 
of fitusiran, suggesting negligible target impact.  
 
Bioinformatic analysis (Study no. BIO-13007) conducted to 
identify siRNAs targeting the human SERPINC1 transcript in the 
NCBI Gene database showed no off-target suppression up to 
concentrations of 10nM, confirming the specificity of fitusiran to 
SERPINC1 (IC50 of 49.6pM or 0.0496nM).  
Source: Applicant’s submission 
Abbreviations: AT, antithrombin; dbSNP, Single-Nucleotide Polymorphism database; IC50, half maximal inhibitory concentration; 
mRNA, messenger RNA; NCBI, National Center for Biotechnology Information; siRNA, small interfering RNA; SNP, single-
nucleotide polymorphism

Study No: ALN-AT3SC 
 
A Cardiovascular and Respiratory 
Safety Pharmacology Study in 
Cynomolgus Monkey Using 
Telemetry 
Doses: 0, 1, 3, or 10 mg/kg 0,  
3 M/dose group 
In a GLP safety pharmacology study that evaluated both 
cardiovascular and respiratory function, male telemetered 
cynomolgus monkeys were subcutaneously administered a single 
dose of vehicle (0.9% sodium chloride for injection), 1, 3, or 
10 mg/kg of fitusiran. 
 
There were no fitusiran-related changes up to 72 hours postdose 
in systemic blood pressure parameters, heart rate, electro-
cardiographic intervals, or body temperature at SC doses up to 
10 mg/kg (HED 194 mg/day or 4 times the MRHD based on BSA 
comparisons). There were no effects on respiratory rate or blood 
gas parameters at timepoints up to 24 hours postdose.  
 
Plasma exposures (Cmax 3490 ng/mL, Day 1) at the 10 mg/kg 
dose in male monkeys, exceeded the Cmax (84.1 ng/mL) at the 
MRHD (50 mg) by approximately 42-fold. 
 
Taken together, these results did not identify any cardiovascular 
or respiratory risk associated with fitusiran exposure.

Table 64. Summary of ADME/PK Studies 
Type of Study

Absorption 
Study No:AT3-NCD13-006, AT3-

Species: Rats (9M +9F/dose group)  
Dose: 0.3, 1, 3 mg/kg 
Species: Monkeys (3M +3F/dose 
group) Dose: 0.1, 0.3, 1 mg/kg. 
Repeat once weekly SC dosing 
Fitusiran was rapidly absorbed in both rats and monkeys. The 
plasma concentrations of fitusiran peaked at 0.5 hours in rats and 1-
2 hours in monkeys and rapidly declined followed by distribution to 
tissues. The subcutaneous bioavailability was 33% in rats and 19% 
in monkeys at a dose of 3 mg/kg. The plasma terminal elimination 
was 1 hour in rats and 3.64 hours in monkeys. 
Distribution 
 
Study No: LPR1221 
Binding of Fitusiran to Mouse, Rat, 
Dog, Monkey, and Human Plasma 
Proteins 
Concentrations: 0.5, 1 5, 10, or 
50 µg/mL. 
The percent protein binding of fitusiran decreased with increasing 
drug concentrations. There was no apparent difference in plasma 
protein binding among the different species tested. The mean 
percentage of fitusiran plasma protein binding ranged from 93.4% to 
47.3% for mouse plasma, 99.2% to 69.1% for rat plasma, 95.2% to 
58.9% for dog plasma, 98.0% to 83.6% for monkey plasma, and 
96.6% to 52.4% for human plasma over the concentration range of 
0.5 to 50 μg/mL. Fitusiran has a very short half-life in plasma as it is 
rapidly cleared and distributed to tissues. Thus, the of plasma 
protein binding is unlikely to impact safety of the drug. 
Study no: AT3-NCD13-002, AT3-
NCD14-009 
Species: Rats 
Dose 3 mg/kg  
Single dose IV or SC dosing 
 
Dose: 0.25, 0.5, or 1 mg/kg/week. 
Repeat SC doing for 26 weeks. 
Fitusiran distributed mainly to liver (the primary target organ of 
pharmacology), followed by kidney and spleen. Following single IV 
dose administration of fitusiran at 3 mg/kg, the percentage of the 
dose in the liver and kidney were 29.1% and 2.39% after 1 hour, 
respectively. Following single SC administration of 3 mg/kg, the 
highest percentage of the dose was observed in the liver and 
kidney: 36.2% (after 4 h) and 1.03% (after 180 h), respectively. The 
liver tissue terminal elimination half-life (t1/2) was approximately 
55 hours and 60 hours by IV and SC, respectively. Exposures in 
other tissues (e.g., brain, thymus, thyroid, and heart) were 
negligible. After repeat SC administration to rats, limited 
accumulation was observed in liver; however, several-fold increase 
in fitusiran exposure was noted in the kidney.  
Metabolism 
 
Study No: BA13003 
In vitro:  
Matrix: serum and S9 liver fractions 
from mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and 
human. 
 
In vivo 
Matrix: Plasma and liver 
Species: 
Mice, dose 25 mg/kg 
Rat, dog and monkey, 3 mg/kg 
Fitusiran is metabolized by sequential loss of the three sugar 
moieties and GalNAc linkers from the GalNAc group from the sense 
strand. The antisense strand of fitusiran is metabolized to AS(N-1)3’ 
in in vivo plasma and liver samples consistent with in vitro liver 
samples. The sense strand was metabolized to form major 
metabolites, S(N-10)5’ and S(N-11)3’ by endonuclease metabolism 
and S(N-3)3’ by exonuclease metabolism in in vitro liver S9 
fractions while it was metabolized to S(N-1)5’ as major metabolite in 
in vivo liver samples.  
 
Based on the metabolites identified in the in vitro and in vivo studies 
for the antisense and sense strands, the following metabolic 
pathways have been proposed by the Applicant for fitusiran.

13.2.4.1. Excretion 
Excretion of fitusiran was evaluated in a single subcutaneous (SC) dose of 3 mg/kg in rats (Study 
no. AT3-NCD13-002). Approximately 4.67% of the dose administered (3 mg/kg) was recovered 
in the urine over 8 weeks, with most of the dose (~3.75%) recovered in the first 24 hours 
postdose. Fecal excretion of fitusiran has not been evaluated. In subjects with hemophilia, mean 
recovery of intact fitusiran in 0 to 24 hours urine was 14.6% at the recommended dose of 50 mg. 
13.2.5. Toxicology 
13.2.5.1. General Toxicology 
Repeat-dose toxicology studies were conducted in healthy rats for up to 26 weeks, healthy 
monkeys for up to 39 weeks, and in hemophilia mice for up to 26 weeks. Fitusiran is 
pharmacologically active in mice, rats, monkeys, and dogs because the antithrombin mRNA 
sequences are highly conserved across species. Animals were dosed once weekly by the SC 
route. Key findings from these studies are summarized below. 
Reference ID: 5560526
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

===== PAGE 153 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
132 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
13.2.5.1.1. A 26-Week Subcutaneous Injection Toxicity, 
Toxicokinetic, and Fertility and Early Embryonic 
Development (to Implantation) Study in the Rat 
Followed By a 13-Week Recovery Period (AT3-
NCD14-009) 
Key Study Findings 
Toxicity 
 Once-weekly SC administration of fitusiran for 26 weeks was well tolerated by female rats 
up to 1 mg/kg/week and by male rats up to 0.5 mg/kg/week. 
 Unscheduled deaths occurred in 5/5 males at 1 mg/kg/week. The cause of deaths was 
excessive clotting (leading to platelet reductions and, paradoxically, systemic hemorrhage), 
necrosis, and/or thrombosis in various organs and tissues, consistent with the exaggerated 
pharmacology of fitusiran. 
 In surviving animals, microscopic findings were observed at the injection site secondary to 
the experimental procedure (needle trauma associated with the injection procedure), that 
resolved by the end of the recovery period. 
 Dose-related decreases in antithrombin protein levels were observed during the dosing period 
consistent with the pharmacology of fitusiran. 
 The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) was the high dose (HD) of 1 mg/kg/week in 
females, based on the absence of toxicity, and the MD of 0.5 mg/kg/week in males, based on 
severe clotting. Both doses represent doses below the maximum recommended human dose 
(MRHD) of 50 mg per month based on BSA comparisons. 
Juvenile Toxicity 
There were no drug-related adverse effects on growth or physical development in juvenile rats 
given up to 1 mg/kg/week fitusiran by once-weekly SC dosing up to Day 58 (equivalent to week 
4 to 8 age). The NOAEL in juvenile rats was 1 mg/kg/week (below the MRHD of 50 mg per 
month based on BSA comparisons), based on no adverse findings up to 1 mg/kg/week. 
Reproductive Performance 
There were no fitusiran-related impacts on male fertility or early embryonic development up to 
1 mg/kg/week. The NOAEL for male reproductive effects was 1 mg/kg/week (below the MRHD 
of 50 mg per month based on BSA comparisons).

from a 4-week repeat dose range study in juvenile rats that 
showed fitusiran was tolerated at ≤1 mg/kg (AT3-NCD14-005).

Table 66. Observations and Results, ALN-AT3SC 
Parameters  
Major Findings 
Mortality 
Unscheduled deaths occurred in male rats: 1 each in control, LD, and MD, and 10 in 
HD. The HD males 4029, 4033, 4034, 4113, and 4123 were found dead on Days 
175, 49, 78, 110 and 110, respectively. Cause of deaths in 5/5 at high dose in the 
main study was considered related to the exaggerated pharmacology of fitusiran 
and consisted of systemic clotting (which led to platelet reductions and, 
paradoxically, to minimal to marked systemic hemorrhage). Clinical signs noted in 
one male (#4123) at HD included decreased activity, shallow breathing, and cold to 
the touch prior to death. Cause of deaths in control was not determined, and the 
deaths in LD and MD were not fully investigated. Five male rats that were found 
dead in the TK study were either not fully investigated (2/5) or associated with blood 
collection procedures.  
 
Macroscopic findings correlated with hemorrhage at 1 mg/kg/week including 
swelling, thickened or mass in subcutis, thymus, adipose tissue, enlargement of 
various lymph nodes, prostate, adrenal, tongue, abnormal consistency-firm prostate, 
seminal vesicle, dark focus in lungs, epididymis, various lymph nodes, stomach, 
subcutis, thymus, material accumulation-clot in skeletal muscle, pelvic body cavity, 
and pancreas. 
 
Microscopic findings in the unscheduled deaths included minimal to marked 
hemorrhage/thrombosis/necrosis in multiple organ and tissues.

Major Findings 
Antithrombin 
protein 
Dose-related decrease in antithrombin protein levels (measured with ELISA) were 
observed in males and females throughout the dosing period and returned to 
baseline following a 13-week recovery period (Day 275), consistent with the 
mechanism of action of fitusiran. The decreases were higher in males (-50% 
to -80%) than females (-10% to -50%) relative to respective control groups.

Tissue TK 
Parameters (Liver, 
Kidney, and 
Spleen) 
High concentrations of fitusiran were detected in liver and kidney tissues from the 
main study animals on Day 58 and Day 184, and in kidney tissues at Day 275. Liver 
concentrations were higher on Day 58, while kidney concentrations were higher on 
Day 184. Fitusiran levels were ≤LLOQ for most spleen tissue samples. 
Accumulation ratio values in liver ranged from 1.06 to 1.27 for Cmax and from 0.521 
to 4.10 for AUC0-t. Accumulation ratio values in kidney ranged from 16.0 to 18.9 for 
Cmax and from 115 to 1619 for AUC0-t. Despite high concentrations in these tissues, 
there were no fitusiran-related toxicities in the liver and kidney in surviving animals 
up to 1 mg/kg/week. However, evidence of hepatobiliary toxicity was observed in the 
7-week GLP repeat dose study in rats at 3 mg/kg/week (AT3-NCD13-006) and 
hemophilia dogs at intolerable doses (BIO-013027).

Quantification 
Bone Marrow 
Evaluation 
No differences were observed in the myeloid:erythroid (M:E) ratios in the 300-cell 
bone marrow differential count, and there were no observations in the maturation of 
the hematopoietic cell lineages on Day 58 or at the end of the main (Day 184) and 
recovery (Day 275) periods related to the administration of fitusiran. 
Gross pathology 
There were no gross findings related to fitusiran in the main or recovery studies. 
Organ weights 
There were no fitusiran-related organ weight changes in the main or recovery 
studies (Days 58, 184, and 275). 
Histopathology 
The most frequent microscopic changes at the scheduled necropsy observed at the 
subcutaneous injection site were minimal to mild perivascular mononuclear cell 
infiltration, minimal myofiber degeneration, minimal to moderate acute hemorrhage, 
and minimal subacute inflammation on Day 58 and Day 184. These changes were 
not considered to be related to fitusiran based on similar incidence and severity in 
control and treated animals. They were considered secondary to the experimental 
procedure (needle trauma associated with the injection procedure). 
 
Increased incidence of tubular testicular degeneration and marked oligospermia 
were observed in MD and HD on Day 58, and in HD on Day 184. At the end of the 
recovery period, Day 275, the testis findings appeared to resolve, however, marked 
oligospermia was present in one recovery animal at HD. 
 
Findings of increased incidence of minimal to mild rosette in the eye were noted at 
HD in males on Day 58, and in one male and one female on Day 184 and Day 275. 
In short-term single-dose studies (Study No. AT3-NCD12-001 and Study AT3-
NCD12-002), findings of porphyrin staining (eyes, nose, paws) were observed at 
≥3 mg/kg. No data on the distribution of fitusiran in the eyes were provided. Thus, 
the toxicological significance of this finding could be related to systemic toxicity due 
to exaggerated pharmacology of fitusiran. 
 
There were findings in pancreas at terminal necropsy in one male at HD with mild 
acinar cell atrophy, in one control male, and in one HD female at recovery necropsy. 
Additional findings at recovery necropsy included minimal hemorrhage, and mild 
inflammation in males at HD. Findings in pancreas were also observed in the 7-
week repeat dose rat study with adverse atrophy/single cell necrosis and/or 
hemorrhage at 3 mg/kg/week (unscheduled deaths), and mild acinar atrophy in the

Toxicokinetic 
Fitusiran concentrations were ≤LLOQ of 50.0 ng/mL for LD (0.25 mg/kg) on Days 1, 
92, and 183, and for MD (0.5 mg/kg) on Day 1. Exposure to fitusiran (Cmax and AUC) 
generally increased with the increase in dose level from 0.5 to 1 mg/kg on Days 92 
and 183. Sex differences in exposure were generally less than 2-fold, except for the 
AUC in males, 3.7-fold, and 2.7-fold higher at 0.5 mg/kg/week on Day 92 and Day 
183, respectively. Plasma concentrations reflect rapid clearance and uptake by 
tissues (primarily liver and kidney) indicating shorter AUC distribution phase.

development phase 
Day 58  
There were no fitusiran-related effects on bone length (tibia and femur) or on growth 
of the animals (crown-rump measurement) in juvenile rats up to 1 mg/kg/week. 
Sexual maturation (vaginal opening or balano-preputial skinfold separation) was 
normal for the treated males and females.  
Spermatogenic 
cycle assessment  
Following weekly SC administration of fitusiran for a total of 16 weeks in juvenile 
male Sprague Dawley rats (initiation at approximately 4 weeks), there were no 
treatment-related effects on sperm morphology and motility, and histopathological 
evaluation of testes showed no changes in fitusiran-treated animals compared to 
control on Days 58 (juvenile), 184 (terminal), and 285 (recovery).

13.2.5.1.2. A 39-Week Subcutaneous Injection Toxicity 
and Toxicokinetic Study in the Cynomolgus 
Monkey Followed by a 13-Week Recovery Period 
(AT3-NCD14-007) 
Key Study Findings 
 Once-weekly SC administration of fitusiran for 39 weeks was well tolerated by male and 
female monkeys up to the highest dose of 0.5 mg/kg/week. 
 Fitusiran-related toxicities included elevated D-dimer values at all doses during the dosing 
period that correlated with decreases in AT levels and tended to recover at the end of 
recovery period. 
 The NOAEL was determined to be the HD, 0.5 mg/kg/week, for both males and females, 
based on absence of adverse fitusiran-related effects, which is lower than the MRHD of 
50 mg per month based on BSA comparisons. 
Reference ID: 5560526

Mortality

Clinical signs 
Skin redness/dryness/scab, thin cover or staining of the fur, and occasional 
occurrences of liquid/soft feces were noted in all dose groups, including 
control, and considered incidental and unrelated to fitusiran due to their low 
incidence and/or lack of dose relationship.

Unremarkable

Unremarkable on Days 92, 183, and 274, and following 13-week recovery 
period. 
D-dimer 
Nonadverse dose-related increased D-dimer levels were observed in males 
at ≥0.3 mg/kg/week and females at ≥0.15 mg/kg/week on Day 92, and at all 
dose levels with no dose response on Day 183. The D-dimer levels stayed 
elevated in females on Day 274 and tended to recover following the recovery 
period. Elevated D-dimer concentrations were also observed in an 
exploratory single-dose pharmacodynamic study (Study No. BIO-13023), and 
7-week repeat-dose toxicity study (Study no. AT3-NCD12-007) in monkeys 
that were adverse (>500-fold). These findings were correlated with 
decreased platelet counts, decreased FIB values, and thrombosis consistent 
with the intended procoagulant effects of target antithrombin protein 
suppression. Increases in fibrin D-Dimer have been reported in clinical 
studies with fitusiran.

145 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
Parameters  
Major Findings 
Histopathology 
Injection site findings (acute hemorrhage and/or presence of inflammatory 
cells) were similar in control and treated animals from all dose groups and 
were considered secondary to the experimental procedures (i.e., 
subcutaneous injection). 
 
Effects on male reproductive organs were observed that included mild to 
severe immaturity in epididymis, prostate gland, and seminal vesicle gland at 
≥0.15 mg/kg/week and marked to severe immaturity in testis in control and 
fitusiran-treated animals consistent with small size of the male reproductive 
organs at the end of recovery necropsy. However, the organ weights relative 
to body weight either show no change or increase. These findings are likely 
related to differences in the sexual maturity of the monkeys, therefore were 
considered unrelated to administration of fitusiran. 
 
Additional nonadverse findings included effects on pancreas including 
minimal angiectasis of islet of Langerhans in a male (LD) and a female (MD) 
on Day 184 and mild acinar atrophy with ductular hyperplasia in male at HD 
on day 365 (recovery). Acinar atrophy was also noted in rats in the 26-week 
chronic study and considered adverse (atrophy/single cell necrosis, 
hemorrhage at 5× MRHD) in the 7-week rat study. Although this finding is 
seen infrequently as a background finding in cynomolgus monkeys, 
relationship to fitusiran cannot be excluded in light of similar findings in rats.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
13.2.5.1.3. A 7-Week Repeat Subcutaneous Injection 
Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in the Albino Rat 
With a 2-Month Recovery Period (AT3-NCD13-006) 
Key Findings 
 Unscheduled deaths occurred in seven male and six female rats at the HD of 3 mg/kg/week, 
which were caused by the exaggerated pharmacology of fitusiran (thrombosis in various 
organs and tissues), which led to platelet reductions and paradoxical systemic hemorrhage. 
 Hepatobiliary toxicity was observed in some animals at the high dose, consisting of elevated 
liver enzymes and adverse histopathology findings that were associated with extensive and 
prolonged pharmacodynamic activity of fitusiran that led to systemic thrombosis and/or 
hemorrhage at >80% reduction in AT protein levels. 
 A NOAEL of 1 mg/kg/week was considered based on deaths and adverse toxicities at 
3 mg/kg/week (less than the MRHD of 50 mg per month based on BSA comparisons). 
Table 85. Study Information, AT3-NCD13-006 
Study Features and Methods 
Details 
GLP compliance 
Yes 
Dose and frequency of dosing 
0, 0.3, 1, or 3 mg/kg once weekly 
Route of administration 
SC 
Formulation/vehicle 
0.9% Sodium Chloride for Injection, USP 
Species/strain 
Sprague-Dawley rats 
Number/sex/group 
15 
Age 
8 weeks old 
Toxicokinetics 
3/sex in control and 9/sex in treatment 
Source: Applicant’s submission 
Abbreviations: GLP, good laboratory practice; SC, subcutaneous; USP, United States Pharmacopeia 
Unscheduled Euthanasia 
Seven males and six females given fitusiran at 3 mg/kg were found dead or euthanized on days 
ranging from 7 to 11 and 16 to 34, respectively. Because of the earlier mortalities and adverse 
clinical signs in the males at 3 mg/kg/week, this entire group was terminated and necropsied on 
Days 15 and 16, along with 3 control-group males. Histopathology evaluations were performed 
only on the unscheduled animals from the main study and not on the animals from the 
toxicokinetic (TK) study. 
 Clinical signs prior to death included decreased activity, cold to touch; swollen firm areas 
(e.g., hind paws, cranium, ventral cervical), swollen soft areas (e.g., ventral cervical, 
cranium, tongue, forelimbs, hind paws), red/black fur staining (e.g., around eyes, forelimbs, 
head, muzzle, urogenital); labored/shallow breathing; thin fur; and dry skin on tail. 
 Clinical pathology parameters in 1 male and 2 female rats compared to control on Day 15/16 
showed decreases in platelets (-68%), reticulocytes (-40%), and lymphocytes (-38%), and 
increases in neutrophils (+390%), WBC (+48%), monocytes (+133%). Changes in 
coagulation included activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) (+37%) and FIB 
concentration (+22%). Clinical chemistry changes showed slight increases in alanine 
Reference ID: 5560526

===== PAGE 169 =====
NDA 219019 
QFITLIA (fitusiran) 
148 
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23) 
aminotransferase (ALT) (+54%) and alkaline phosphatase (ALP) (+77%) activity, with 
increased total bilirubin concentrations (+128%). 
 Clinical pathology findings correlated with microscopic findings in the liver (single cell 
necrosis, increased mitoses, mononuclear infiltration, and effects of biliary duct hyperplasia 
or dilation). Dosing was discontinued for the remaining HD males, and they were sacrificed 
on Day 15/16. Additional hematology findings included marked platelet clumps in female rat 
4006. 
 Macroscopic findings correlated with hemorrhage and/or edema and thrombosis and included 
dark discoloration/focus in several organs including adrenals, kidneys, lymph nodes 
(mandibular, mesenteric), testis, and thymus. In addition, the subcutis was thickened with or 
without dark discoloration. In Animal No. 4503, the tongue was thickened and dark. In 
Animal No. 4005, the penis was protruded, thickened, and dark. The gross changes described 
above usually correlated with hemorrhage microscopically. Dark fluid/material (compatible 
with blood) was seen in the urinary bladder, muscle, and thymus of a few rats. In one rat (No. 
4006) there was a dilatation of the bile duct. 
 Microscopic findings included hemorrhage, necrosis, edema, and/or thrombosis in various 
organs and tissues (e.g., nasal cavity, larynx, lungs, trachea, brain, eye, mandibular lymph 
node, penis, prostate, subcutis, and tongue), and liver changes recorded in few animals 
including single cell necrosis, increased mitoses and biliary (biliary duct) hyperplasia, and 
lymphoid necrosis/depletion in lymph nodes (mandibular and mesenteric), GALT, spleen, 
and thymus.

Abbreviations: F, female; FD, found dead; M, male; TK, toxicokinetics; UE, unscheduled euthanasia 
Early Euthanasia (Day 15/16) 
Microscopic findings in scheduled euthanasia of HD males included minimal to moderate 
hemorrhage and/or thrombosis in multiple organs including brain, lung, pancreas, and 
femorotibial joint. 
Compared to control on Day 44, mild to moderate increases in neutrophil (+68%), lymphocyte 
(+42%) and monocyte (+100%) counts, reflected in total white blood cell counts (+48%), 
increases in aspartate aminotransferase (AST) (+117%), ALT (+184%), ALP (+45%), with mild 
to marked increases in total bilirubin (+372%), cholesterol (+26%), triglyceride (+29%), and 
globulin (+20%) concentrations were observed at 3 mg/kg/week correlated with histological 
findings in the liver with minimal to moderate necrosis (focal to multifocal), minimal single cell 
necrosis, minimal to moderate biliary hyperplasia, minimal portal mononuclear cell infiltration, 
and minimal increased mitoses. 
Scheduled Euthanasia (Day 44) 
Clinical signs: Hypersensitivity to the touch, skin redness/dryness/scab, and thin cover or 
staining of the fur were noted in males at 0.3 and 1 mg/kg/week. 
Minimal increases in ALP in females at 0.3 (+14%), and 1 mg/kg (+14%) resolved at the end of 
the recovery period. Histopathology findings included minimal brain hemorrhage in one male at 
1 mg/kg and minimal thrombosis in the lungs in two males at 0.3 mg/kg, one male and one 
female at 1 mg/kg, and one female at 3 mg/kg, and minimal thrombosis of the larynx in one 
female at 3 mg/kg/week was observed.

Toxicokinetic Profile 
Based on exposure (AUC0-t and maximum plasma concentration [Cmax]) at 0.3 and 
1 mg/kg/week, there was no sex difference observed in the TK parameters. The exposure to 
fitusiran increased approximately in a dose-proportional manner over the dose range. The time to 
maximum concentration was 0.5 hours after SC administration.

13.2.5.1.4. A 7-Week Repeat Subcutaneous Injection 
Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in the 
Cynomolgus Monkey With a 2-Month Recovery 
Period (AT3-NCD12-007) 
Key Findings 
 Unscheduled deaths occurred in two male monkeys at 1 mg/kg/week with progressive 
increases in D-dimer values, decrease in fibrinogen, and decrease in platelets (in one 
monkey) associated with moderate multifocal cerebral necrosis with hemorrhage and 
moderate thrombosis or minimal pulmonary vessel thrombosis following second dose 
administration. 
 A NOAEL of 1 mg/kg/week was considered based on deaths and adverse toxicities at 
3 mg/kg/week (2 times the MRHD of 50 mg per month based on BSA comparisons). 
Table 91. Study Information, AT3-NCD12-007 
Study Features and Methods 
Details 
GLP compliance 
Yes 
Dose and frequency of dosing 
0, 0.1, 0.3, or 1 mg/kg once weekly 
Route of administration 
SC 
Formulation/vehicle 
0.9% Sodium Chloride for Injection, USP. 
Species/strain 
Cynomolgus monkeys 
Number/sex/group 
3 
Age 
2-3 years old 
Toxicokinetics 
3/sex in control and 9/sex in treatment 
Source: Applicant’s submission 
Abbreviations: GLP, good laboratory practice; SC, subcutaneous; USP, United States Pharmacopeia 
Unscheduled Euthanasia 
Two male monkeys given fitusiran at high dose 1 mg/kg were found dead on Day 22 (4001) and 
Day 35 (4004). Both animals had changes in coagulation parameters related to fitusiran 
treatment. Clinical signs included soft swelling of the ventral cervical and cranial regions. 
 Animal #4001 showed progressive increases in D-dimer on Day 15 and 22 (26% to 543%), 
minimal to mild decreases in fibrinogen concentration (-8% to -26%), and a moderate 
decrease in platelet count (-58%) compared to individual pretreatment values. 
 Animal #4004 had progressive increases in D-dimer (31% to 386% individual values 
compared to second prestudy value) and mild decreases in fibrinogen concentration (-18% to 
-26%) between Days 15 and 29, and no noticeable change in platelet count compared to 
individual pretreatment values. 
 These findings correlated macroscopically with moderate multifocal cerebral necrosis with 
hemorrhage and moderate thrombosis in animal 4004 and chronic and minimal pulmonary 
vessel thrombosis in animal 4001. 
In surviving animals, clinical observations of skin redness/dryness/scab and thin cover or 
staining of the fur were observed in all dose groups. Clinical pathology findings included 
minimal to mild increases in D-dimer values in males at ≥0.1 mg/kg (83% to 598%) and in 
females at ≥0.3 mg/kg (55% to 139%) compared to control on Day 44, and mild decreases in FIB 
Reference ID: 5560526

protein and activity levels.

Transient, nonadverse, slight increases in plasma concentrations of complement factors, Bb (up 
to 1.6-fold), C3a (up to 1.6-fold), and cytokines IL-6 (up to 5.4-fold) and/or TNF-alpha (up to 
3.2-fold) were observed in individual animals. For complement factors, no overall trend in group 
means and no statistically significant changes were observed for either sex after fitusiran 
administration. The relationship of IL-6 and TNF-alpha to administration of fitusiran was unclear 
based on the lack of any dose-dependent trend in responses, the small magnitude, and the low 
incidence of the increases. 
Histopathology findings on day 44 at 1 mg/kg/week included minimal or mild focal thrombosis 
and mononuclear cell inflammation in the lung in one male and one female monkey, likely an 
on-target pharmacological effect of fitusiran.

13.2.5.2. Other Toxicology Studies 
13.2.5.2.1. A 26-Week Repeat Subcutaneous Injection 
Toxicity Study in the Transgenic Hemophilia A 
Mouse (AT3-NCD14-008) 
Key Findings 
 Unscheduled deaths occurred in control untreated hemophilia mice (40%), and in fitusiran 
treated hemophilia mice 10 (3%) and 30 (8.5%) mg/kg/week caused by hemorrhage mainly 
at the injection site, associated with hemophilia condition. 
 Fitusiran-related findings included dose-related decreases in AT protein levels and decreases 
in aPTT at both fitusiran dose levels, supporting the reduced incidence of mortality/morbidity 
secondary to hemorrhage in fitusiran-treated groups. 
 The study demonstrates therapeutic benefit of fitusiran in hemophilia A mice when compared 
to untreated hemophilia mice. 
 A NOAEL could not be determined because of unscheduled deaths at both fitusiran doses 
evaluated in this study. 
Table 98. Study Information, AT3-NCD14-008 
Study Features and Methods 
Details 
GLP compliance 
Yes 
Dose and frequency of dosing 
0, 10 or 30 mg/kg once weekly 
Route of administration 
SC 
Formulation/vehicle 
0.9% Sodium Chloride for Injection, USP. 
Species/strain 
Male mice (B6;129S4-F8tm1Kaz/J) mice with hemophilia A 
Number/sex/group 
35 
Age 
6 to 8 weeks old 
Toxicokinetics 
Not conducted 
Source: Applicant’s submission 
Abbreviations: SC, subcutaneous; USP, United States Pharmacopeia 
Unscheduled deaths occurred in 40% of control mice (11/35 males and 15/35 females) and 6% of 
fitusiran-treated animals (1 male and 1 female at 10 mg/kg [3%], and 3 males and 3 females at 
30 mg/kg [8.5%]). Findings included hemorrhage at injection site, intestine, nasal cavity, spinal 
cord, and subcutis in the control and injection site, ear, subcutis in the fitusiran-treated mice, 
suggesting bleeding in association with hemophilia condition. Clinical signs noted prior to death 
in control animals and fitusiran-treated mice included decreased activity, weakness, partly closed 
eyes, cold to touch, suspected dehydration, hunched posture, and labored breathing. Mortality 
attributed to lethal hemorrhage in the control group correlated with the hemostatic disease 
condition in this strain of mice. 
Coagulation 
A decrease in aPTT and statistically significant decrease in males was observed at both doses on 
Day 93 and Day 184. 
Reference ID: 5560526

There were no fitusiran-related findings/changes in hematology, clinical chemistry, organ 
weights, or gross pathology in fitusiran-treated hemophilia A (HA) mice. 
Histopathology findings in the surviving animals included minimal to mild pigmented 
macrophages consistent with hemosiderin in the mandibular lymph nodes in both control and

hemoglobin degradation following hemorrhage in these hemophiliac mice and was not 
considered to be a direct effect of fitusiran. Other findings included minimal hepatocellular 
necrosis (males only), and minimal to mild Kupffer cell pigmentation in HD male and female 
mice.

Table 102. Toxicological Effects of Fitusiran in Dogs With Hemophilia A 
Type of Study 
Major Findings 
Study No: BIO-13027 
 
Study Title: Pharmacology of 
Fitusiran in Hemophilia A Dogs 
After Repeat SC Dosing 
Although this study was designed to assess the pharmacodynamic 
activity in female HA dogs (see primary pharmacology section), 
routine hematology and clinical chemistry parameters and limited 
histopathology were assessed. Fitusiran at 10 mg/kg/week in dogs 
was not tolerated after the 4th dose, producing adverse liver and 
kidney findings including increases in ALP (32 to 93-fold), ALT (34 to 
93-fold), AST (12 to 18-fold), and total bilirubin (2 to 110-fold) and 
decrease in platelets (33 to 82%). 
 
Based on the degree of fitusiran-related clinical pathology parameter 
changes, two dogs with HA were humanely euthanized 17 days after 
the 4th dose of 10 mg/kg/week (26 times the MRHD of 50 mg per 
month based on BSA comparisons); macroscopic findings included 
both livers with abnormal appearance (discolored, mottled, pitted). 
There was no evidence of thrombosis or hemorrhage in the 
mesenteric vasculature. Microscopic findings were observed in the 
liver and kidney. Gallbladders were not evaluated. 
 
Liver: minimal to mild hepatocellular degeneration/necrosis and mild 
to moderate inflammation in both dogs; moderate acute/chronic 
hemorrhage, and/or sinusoidal congestion. There was no evidence of 
thrombosis or fibrin clots in the liver.  
Kidneys: minimal tubular degeneration and/or necrosis, minimal 
regeneration, and minimal dilatation, minimal tubule epithelia pigment 
(presumably hemosiderin). 
Source: Applicant’s submission

13.2.5.3. Carcinogenicity Studies 
Study No/ Study Title: ALN-AT3SC: A 2-Year Subcutaneous Injection Carcinogenicity 
Study in Rats  
Good laboratory practice (GLP) compliance: Yes 
Key Study Findings 
In agreement with doses recommended by CDER’s Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment 
Committee, Sprague Dawley rats were administered fitusiran at subcutaneous doses of 0, 0.03, 
0.1, or 0.3 mg/kg/week to males and 0, 0.1, 0.3, or 1 mg/kg/week to females once weekly for 
2 years. The highest doses were based on mortality due to hemorrhagic thrombosis at ≥1 mg/kg 
in males and at 3 mg/kg in females in a 26-week and a 7-week repeat dose toxicity study, 
respectively. The survival rate in fitusiran-treated groups was comparable to control groups. 
There were no dose-dependent or statistically significant fitusiran-related neoplastic findings 
noted in either male or female rats up to the highest dose tested. All fitusiran dose levels tested in 
male and female rats were below the MRHD based on BSA comparisons.

Adequacy of Carcinogenicity Study 
The Committee concluded that the 2-year carcinogenicity study conducted in Sprague-Dawley 
rats with fitusiran was adequate and there was no evidence of drug-related neoplasms in male or 
female rats. 
Study No/ Study Title: AT3-GLP17-001 / ALN-AT3SC: A 26-Week Subcutaneous Injection 
Carcinogenicity Study in TgRasH2 Mice  
Good laboratory practice (GLP) compliance: Yes 
Key Study Findings 
In agreement with doses recommended by CDER’s Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment 
Committee, Tg.RasH2 mice were administered fitusiran at subcutaneous doses of 0, 0.05, 0.15, 
or 0.5 mg/kg/week to males and 0, 0.025, 0.08, or 0.25 mg/kg/week to females once weekly for 
26 weeks. The highest doses were selected based on body weight gain decrements in a dose 
range finding study in wild-type littermates. A positive control group of Tg.RasH2 mice was 
administered a single intraperitoneal dose of N-methyl-N-nitrosourea (MNU) at 75 mg/kg on 
Day 1. The survival rate in fitusiran-treated animals was comparable to control animals. No 
statistically significant increases in dose-response relationships or pairwise comparisons were 
observed for neoplastic findings after exposure to doses of fitusiran up to 0.5 mg/kg. The 
positive control MNU induced increased incidences of malignant squamous cell carcinoma and 
papilloma of the stomach in male and female RasH2 mice relative to control. 
ECAC Conclusions 
The Committee concluded that the 26-week carcinogenicity study conducted in Tg.RasH2 mice 
with fitusiran was adequate and there was no evidence of drug-related neoplasms in male and 
female mice. 
13.2.5.4. Reproductive Toxicology 
No reproductive and developmental toxicity studies were conducted with fitusiran in female 
animals because the intended population is almost exclusively male, and the clinical 
development program was conducted exclusively in males. Hemophilia A and B are X-linked 
recessive inherited bleeding disorders predominantly affecting males (99%), and the likelihood 
of women of childbearing potential receiving fitusiran is very low; therefore, only a male fertility 
study was integrated into the 26-week repeated dose chronic toxicity study in rats. There were no 
fitusiran-related effects on male fertility and early embryonic development up to 1 mg/kg/week 
(below the MRHD based on BSA comparisons). It is not known whether fitusiran can adversely 
impact the development of embryo/fetus when administered to a pregnant woman. Only male 
subjects were enrolled in clinical trials with fitusiran. 
 
 
 
Female Fertility and Early Embryonic Development 
Because hemophilia is primarily an X-linked disease, no embryo-fetal toxicity studies in rats and 
rabbits have been performed.

Juvenile Animal Studies 
Non-GLP range finding (AT3-DSM16-001) and repeat-dose tolerability (AT3-NCD14-005) 
studies in juvenile rats were conducted. Four-day-old pups (n=8/sex/group) were administered 
fitusiran by SC administration with doses of 0, 1, 3, or 10 mg/kg/week ×4 or 0, 0.5, and 
1.0 mg/kg/week ×5.  
Results 
Study AT3-DSM16-001: At 10 mg/kg/week, mortality was observed in 7 pups (6 males and 
1 female) occurring between Study Days 19 and 28, due to jugular venipuncture or head trauma 
based on swelling or clotting in the ventral cervical region (4 pups) or in the cranial cavity (1 of 
those 4 pups) likely due to the pharmacological effect of fitusiran. One pup had poor and/or 
deteriorating condition that led to unscheduled euthanasia, likely due to unilateral findings of 
irregular surface and mottled discoloration in the kidney. The cause of the death was 
undetermined for the remaining two pups.  
Clinical signs in these moribund pups included abnormal gait, decreased activity, cold to touch, 
incoordination, eyes partly closed, skin pallor, and labored breathing. Decreased body weight 
gains were noted at 10 mg/kg/week. 
Toxicokinetics 
Plasma 
Plasma TK parameters at 1 mg/kg/week were below the lower limit of quantitation on Days 4 
postpartum (PP) and 25 PP. There were no sex differences in exposure (Cmax and AUC); the data 
are presented for males and females combined.

14. Clinical Pharmacology 
14.1. In Vitro Studies 
14.1.1. Distribution Study 
14.1.1.1. Study AT3-RS13-001: Plasma Protein Binding of 
Fitusiran 
The Applicant evaluated the binding of fitusiran to animal (mouse, rat, monkey, dog) and human 
plasma proteins. Briefly, as detailed in the nonclinical study report LPR1221 (submitted on 
September 16, 2024, Module 4.2.2.3), in vitro human plasma protein binding of fitusiran at 
concentrations of 0.5, 1, 5, 10, 25, and 50 µg/mL was 96.6%, 95.6%, 90.2%, 83.9%, 78.7% and 
52.4% respectively.

14.1.2.2. Study MET0981: Evaluation of the Percentage of 
N-1 Metabolite in Clinical Samples 
Study MET0981 evaluated fitusiran N-1 metabolite in human plasma samples from phase 1 
study ALN-AT3SC-001. The objective of this study was to obtain an estimation of the 
percentage of N-1 metabolite in human plasma by retrospectively reanalyzing select 
chromatograms from the fitusiran pharmacokinetic (PK) sample analysis in Study TDR14767 
(ALN-AT3SC-001). 
The evaluation included: (i) analyzing HPLC chromatograms which had already been generated 
as part of fitusiran analysis in PK samples from Study TDR14767 (ALN-AT3SC-001), (ii) 
identification of N-1 metabolite as a peak distinct from fitusiran, and (iii) reprocessing of the 
chromatograms to integrate the N-1 peak areas for comparison with fitusiran peak areas. 
Noncompartmental pharmacokinetic analysis was then performed on the chromatogram peak 
areas to generate area under the concentration-time curve to the last measurable concentration 
(AUClast) values for 16 subjects who had received 3 fitusiran SC doses at 80 mg QM, and the 
amount of N-1 metabolite relative to fitusiran was estimated from the ratio of N-1 metabolite 
AUClast/fitusiran AUClast. 
The data indicated that after repeated SC administration of fitusiran at 80 mg QM, the N-1 
metabolite is present in plasma at levels <10% of intact fitusiran (N-1 
metabolite/fitusiran =5.26±0.98%). No sample analysis or reanalysis to specifically quantitate 
N-1 metabolite was performed for any clinical sample. In addition, it could not be ascertained 
Reference ID: 5560526
